Chapter 17 Calculus of Vector-Valued Functions

Exercise 17.1: Differentiation of vector-valued functions

Solution

We differentiate each component of $\vec f(t)=[\,t^3-t,\ e^{2t},\ \ln(t+1)\,]$ with respect to $t$, following Definition 17.1.

First component: $\dfrac{d}{dt}(t^3-t)=3t^2-1$ (we used the differentiation formula for power functions on each term).

Second component: $\dfrac{d}{dt}e^{2t}=2e^{2t}$ (chain rule, $\dfrac{d}{dt}e^{kt}=ke^{kt}$).

Third component: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\ln(t+1)=\dfrac{1}{t+1}$ (chain rule, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\ln u=\dfrac{u'}{u}$ with $u=t+1,\ u'=1$).

Therefore,

$$ \vec f\,'(t) = \left[\,3t^2-1,\ \ 2e^{2t},\ \ \frac{1}{t+1}\,\right] $$

Substituting $t=0$, we obtain

$$ \vec f\,'(0) = \left[\,3\cdot0^2-1,\ \ 2e^{0},\ \ \frac{1}{0+1}\,\right] = [\,-1,\ 2,\ 1\,] $$

(differentiation verified with SymPy).

Exercise 17.2: Length of a helix

Solution

Differentiating $\vec f(t)=[\,3\cos t,\ 3\sin t,\ 4t\,]$ with respect to $t$ gives

$$ \vec f\,'(t) = [\,-3\sin t,\ \ 3\cos t,\ \ 4\,] $$

Its magnitude is

$$ \abs{\vec f\,'(t)} = \sqrt{(-3\sin t)^2+(3\cos t)^2+4^2} = \sqrt{9\sin^2t+9\cos^2t+16} = \sqrt{9(\sin^2t+\cos^2t)+16} $$

(we factored the common factor $9$ out of $9\sin^2t$ and $9\cos^2t$). Using $\sin^2t+\cos^2t=1$ (a basic formula from high school mathematics), we obtain

$$ \abs{\vec f\,'(t)} = \sqrt{9\cdot1+16} = \sqrt{25} = 5 $$

which is a constant independent of $t$. By Definition 17.2 (the formula in Chapter 17),

$$ S = \int_0^{2\pi}\abs{\vec f\,'(t)}\,dt = \int_0^{2\pi}5\,dt = 5\times[\,t\,]_0^{2\pi} = 5\times2\pi = 10\pi $$

($10\pi\fallingdotseq31.4$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 17.3: Computing a definite integral

Solution

Following Definition 17.4, we compute the definite integral component by component.

First component: $\displaystyle\int_0^1t^2\,dt=\left[\frac{t^3}{3}\right]_0^1=\frac13-0=\frac13$ (the integration formula for power functions).

Second component: $\displaystyle\int_0^1e^t\,dt=[\,e^t\,]_0^1=e^1-e^0=e-1$ (the integration formula for the exponential function).

Third component: $\displaystyle\int_0^1\frac{1}{1+t^2}\,dt=[\,\tan^{-1}t\,]_0^1=\tan^{-1}1-\tan^{-1}0=\frac{\pi}{4}-0=\frac{\pi}{4}$ (we used the derivative of the inverse tangent function from Chapter 3, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\tan^{-1}t=\dfrac{1}{1+t^2}$, in reverse. The value $\tan^{-1}1=\pi/4$ follows from $\tan(\pi/4)=1$).

Therefore, the integral equals

$$ \int_0^1\left[\,t^2,\ e^t,\ \frac{1}{1+t^2}\,\right]dt = \left[\,\frac13,\ \ e-1,\ \ \frac{\pi}{4}\,\right] $$

($\frac13\fallingdotseq0.333$, $e-1\fallingdotseq1.718$, $\pi/4\fallingdotseq0.785$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 17.4: Applying the integration by parts formula

Solution

Differentiating $\vec a(t)=[\,e^t,\ t^2\,]$ gives $\vec a\,'(t)=[\,e^t,\ 2t\,]$. Taking $f(t)=t$ (so that $f'(t)=1$) and substituting into Formula 17.2(I), we obtain

$$ \int t\,\vec a\,'(t)\,dt = t\,\vec a(t) - \int 1\cdot\vec a(t)\,dt = t[\,e^t,\ t^2\,] - \int[\,e^t,\ t^2\,]\,dt $$

Computing the indefinite integral $\displaystyle\int\vec a(t)\,dt$ component by component, we have $\displaystyle\int e^t\,dt=e^t+C_1$ and $\displaystyle\int t^2\,dt=\frac{t^3}{3}+C_2$, so that

$$ \int t\,\vec a\,'(t)\,dt = [\,te^t,\ t^3\,] - \left[\,e^t,\ \frac{t^3}{3}\,\right] + \vec C = \left[\,te^t-e^t,\ \ t^3-\frac{t^3}{3}\,\right] + \vec C = \left[\,te^t-e^t,\ \ \frac23t^3\,\right] + \vec C $$

(for the second component we combined the terms over a common denominator: $t^3-\dfrac{t^3}{3}=\dfrac{3t^3-t^3}{3}=\dfrac{2t^3}{3}$). As a check, we differentiate this result with respect to $t$. For the first component, the product rule gives $\dfrac{d}{dt}(te^t-e^t)=(e^t+te^t)-e^t=te^t$, and for the second component, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\dfrac23t^3\right)=2t^2$. On the other hand, the original integrand is $t\,\vec a\,'(t)=t[\,e^t,2t\,]=[\,te^t,\ 2t^2\,]$, and the two indeed agree (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 17.5: A first-order linear differential equation for a vector (a case where resonance occurs)

Solution

Since $p(t)=3$ (a constant), we have $\displaystyle\int p\,dt=3t$. We split the equation into its three components and solve them one at a time by variation of parameters (the formula in Chapter 17).

[First component] $y_1'+3y_1=1$. Take the particular solution to be a constant, $y_{1p}=k$; then $3k=1$, so $k=\dfrac13$. The general solution is $y_1(t)=\dfrac13+C_1e^{-3t}$. From the initial condition $y_1(0)=0$ we get $0=\dfrac13+C_1$, so $C_1=-\dfrac13$. Hence $y_1(t)=\dfrac13-\dfrac13e^{-3t}=\dfrac{1-e^{-3t}}{3}$.

[Second component] $y_2'+3y_2=t$. Assuming the particular solution to be $y_{2p}=At+B$ and substituting, we get $A+3(At+B)=t$, that is, $3At+(A+3B)=t$. Comparing coefficients, $3A=1$ gives $A=\dfrac13$, and $A+3B=0$ gives $B=-\dfrac19$. The general solution is $y_2(t)=\dfrac t3-\dfrac19+C_2e^{-3t}$. From the initial condition $y_2(0)=0$ we get $0=-\dfrac19+C_2$, so $C_2=\dfrac19$. Hence $y_2(t)=\dfrac t3-\dfrac19+\dfrac19e^{-3t}$.

[Third component (resonance)] $y_3'+3y_3=e^{-3t}$. The solution of the homogeneous equation $y_3'+3y_3=0$ is $e^{-3t}$ itself, and the nonhomogeneous term $e^{-3t}$ has exactly the same form. A situation like this, in which the nonhomogeneous term has the same form as a solution of the homogeneous equation, is called resonance. In this case, if we assume the particular solution in the usual form of a constant multiple, $Ae^{-3t}$, then the left-hand side becomes identically $0$ the moment we substitute (naturally, since we are substituting a homogeneous solution), and the coefficient $A$ cannot be determined. So we revise our assumption and take the particular solution to be $t$ times this, in the form $y_{3p}=At\,e^{-3t}$. By the product rule, $y_{3p}'=Ae^{-3t}-3Ate^{-3t}$, so substituting into the equation gives

$$ (Ae^{-3t}-3Ate^{-3t})+3(Ate^{-3t}) = Ae^{-3t} = e^{-3t} $$

($-3Ate^{-3t}$ and $+3Ate^{-3t}$ canceled each other exactly). Hence $A=1$ and $y_{3p}=te^{-3t}$. The general solution is $y_3(t)=te^{-3t}+C_3e^{-3t}=(t+C_3)e^{-3t}$. From the initial condition $y_3(0)=1$ we get $1=(0+C_3)e^0=C_3$, so $C_3=1$. Hence $y_3(t)=(t+1)e^{-3t}$.

Putting these together, the solution is

$$ \vec y(t) = \left[\ \frac{1-e^{-3t}}{3},\ \ \ \frac t3-\frac19+\frac19e^{-3t},\ \ \ (t+1)e^{-3t}\ \right] $$

(verified with SymPy's dsolve. We confirmed that the technique for linear differential equations with constant coefficients learned in Chapters 2 and 3—“assume the particular solution in the same form as the nonhomogeneous term, and multiply by $t$ when it overlaps with a homogeneous solution”—can be used unchanged in the vector case as well).

Exercise 17.6: Lateral area of a cylinder

Solution

Taking the partial derivatives of $\vec P(u,v)=[\,a\cos u,\ a\sin u,\ v\,]$, we get

$$ \pdiff{\vec P}{u} = [\,-a\sin u,\ \ a\cos u,\ \ 0\,],\qquad \pdiff{\vec P}{v} = [\,0,\ \ 0,\ \ 1\,] $$

(for $\pdiff{\vec P}{u}$ we differentiated with respect to $u$ with $v$ held fixed, and for $\pdiff{\vec P}{v}$ with respect to $v$ with $u$ held fixed). Using the component formula for the cross product, $\vec u\times\vec v=[\,u_2v_3-u_3v_2,\ u_3v_1-u_1v_3,\ u_1v_2-u_2v_1\,]$ (Chapter 15), with $\vec u=\pdiff{\vec P}{u}=[-a\sin u,a\cos u,0]$ and $\vec v=\pdiff{\vec P}{v}=[0,0,1]$, we compute

$$ \text{First component:}\ a\cos u\cdot1-0\cdot0=a\cos u \qquad \text{Second component:}\ 0\cdot0-(-a\sin u)\cdot1=a\sin u \qquad \text{Third component:}\ (-a\sin u)\cdot0-a\cos u\cdot0=0 $$

Hence

$$ \pdiff{\vec P}{u}\times\pdiff{\vec P}{v} = [\,a\cos u,\ \ a\sin u,\ \ 0\,] $$

Its magnitude is

$$ \left|\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\times\pdiff{\vec P}{v}\right| = \sqrt{(a\cos u)^2+(a\sin u)^2+0^2} = \sqrt{a^2(\cos^2u+\sin^2u)} = \sqrt{a^2} = a $$

(we used $a>0$; we simplified with $\cos^2u+\sin^2u=1$). This is a constant independent of both $u$ and $v$. Substituting into Formula 17.3, we obtain

$$ S = \iint_D\left|\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\times\pdiff{\vec P}{v}\right|\,du\,dv = \int_0^h\!\!\int_0^{2\pi}a\,du\,dv = a\left(\int_0^{2\pi}du\right)\left(\int_0^hdv\right) = a\times2\pi\times h = 2\pi ah $$

which confirms that it agrees with the familiar formula “lateral area = circumference of the base $2\pi a$ × height $h$” (verified with SymPy). It also agrees with the intuition that, if we think of the lateral surface of the cylinder as a thin rectangular sheet of paper rolled up, its area is that of a rectangle whose horizontal side is the circumference of the base, $2\pi a$, and whose vertical side is the height, $h$, namely $2\pi ah$.

Exercise 17.7: Why does the cross product point in the normal direction of the surface? (Reasoning from the figure)

Solution

The filled parallelogram in Figure 17.7 is the figure spanned by the two tangent vectors $\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\Delta u$ and $\pdiff{\vec P}{v}\Delta v$ issuing from the point $\vec P(u,v)$. As we saw in the intuition behind the total differential $d\vec P=\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\,du+\pdiff{\vec P}{v}\,dv$ of Definition 17.6 (Section 17.5 of the main text), in the limit $\Delta u,\Delta v\to0$ this parallelogram approaches the tangent plane itself (the plane that touches the surface $\vec P(u,v)$ snugly at the point $\vec P(u,v)$). In other words, the two vectors $\pdiff{\vec P}{u}$ and $\pdiff{\vec P}{v}$ are both vectors contained in this tangent plane.

Now recall the definition of the cross product that we learned in Chapter 15. The cross product $\vec u\times\vec v$ of two vectors $\vec u,\vec v$ is a vector whose direction is perpendicular to both $\vec u$ and $\vec v$ (the direction determined by the right-hand screw rule), and whose magnitude equals the area of the parallelogram spanned by $\vec u,\vec v$.

Here, $\vec u=\pdiff{\vec P}{u}$ and $\vec v=\pdiff{\vec P}{v}$ are both vectors in the tangent plane, so the cross product $\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\times\pdiff{\vec P}{v}$ has a direction perpendicular to both of these two vectors, that is, perpendicular to the tangent plane itself. The direction perpendicular to the plane tangent to the surface is precisely the definition of the normal direction of the surface. Therefore, $\pdiff{\vec P}{u}\times\pdiff{\vec P}{v}$ points in the normal direction of the surface $\vec P(u,v)$. (The fact that the magnitude of this cross product gives the infinitesimal area element was already used in the derivation of Formula 17.3. What is interesting is that a single operation, the cross product, gives exactly the right quantity for both the direction and the magnitude.)

← Back to Chapter 17, “Calculus of Vector-Valued Functions”

Chapter 18 Scalar Fields, Vector Fields, and the Gradient

Exercise 18.1: Computing the values of a scalar field and a vector field

Solution

Following Definitions 18.1 and 18.2, we only need to substitute the coordinates of the point $(2,1,-1)$ directly into $f,\vec F$.

$$ f(2,1,-1) = 2^2+2\cdot1\cdot(-1) = 4-2 = 2 $$ $$ \vec F(2,1,-1) = [\,1+(-1),\ (-1)^2,\ 2\cdot1\,] = [0,\,1,\,2] $$

Therefore, $f(2,1,-1)=2$ and $\vec F(2,1,-1)=[0,1,2]$. (verified with SymPy)

Exercise 18.2: Finding a level curve

Solution

First we find the value of $f$ at the point $(1,3)$.

$$ f(1,3) = 1\cdot3 = 3 $$

By Definition 18.3, the level curve through the point $(1,3)$ is the set of points satisfying the equation $f(x,y)=3$. Hence the level curve we want is

$$ xy = 3 $$

This is a hyperbola with the shape of the graph of inverse proportion, and we can confirm that the point $(1,3)$ lies on it because $1\times3=3$. (verified with SymPy)

Exercise 18.3: Finding a level surface

Solution

We find the value of $f$ at the point $(2,1,3)$.

$$ f(2,1,3) = 2^2-1^2-3 = 4-1-3 = 0 $$

By Definition 18.4, the level surface through the point $(2,1,3)$ is given by the equation $f(x,y,z)=0$, that is,

$$ x^2-y^2-z = 0 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad z = x^2-y^2 $$

This is a surface of the form $z=x^2-y^2$. As we learned in Example 6.1 of Chapter 6, it is a surface with a saddle point, curving upward in the $x$-axis direction and downward in the $y$-axis direction (a saddle-shaped surface, resembling a horse's saddle). We can confirm that the point $(2,1,3)$ lies on this surface because $2^2-1^2=4-1=3$. (verified with SymPy)

Exercise 18.4: Finding streamlines

Solution

Since $\vec F=[f,g]=[y,x]$, the equation of the streamlines, $\dfrac{dx}{f}=\dfrac{dy}{g}=\dfrac{dz}{h}$ (Eq. (18.1); in the planar case, omit the $z$ component), becomes

$$ \frac{dx}{y} = \frac{dy}{x} $$

Multiplying both sides by $xy$ to clear the denominators gives $x\,dx=y\,dy$. Integrating the two sides separately, we obtain

$$ \int x\,dx = \int y\,dy \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{x^2}{2}+C_1 = \frac{y^2}{2}+C_2 $$

Multiplying both sides by $2$ and combining the constants (we rename $2(C_2-C_1)$ as $C$), we get

$$ x^2 - y^2 = C $$

Therefore, the streamlines form the family of hyperbolas $x^2-y^2=C$ (for $C=0$, the two lines $y=\pm x$). (Check: if a curve $x(t),y(t)$ satisfies $\dfrac{dx}{dt}=y,\ \dfrac{dy}{dt}=x$, then $\dfrac{d}{dt}(x^2-y^2)=2x\dfrac{dx}{dt}-2y\dfrac{dy}{dt}=2xy-2yx=0$, which confirms that $x^2-y^2$ is constant, independent of $t$. Verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 18.5: Applying the gradient formulas

Solution

(a) $|\vec P|^{-3}$ is the case $n=-3$ of Formula 18.3, so

$$ \nabla|\vec P|^{-3} = (-3)\,|\vec P|^{-3-2}\,\vec P = -3\,|\vec P|^{-5}\,\vec P = -\frac{3\vec P}{|\vec P|^5} $$

Let us verify this directly in the case of two variables ($\vec P=[x,y]$). Differentiating $|\vec P|^{-3}=(x^2+y^2)^{-3/2}$ partially with respect to $x$, by the chain rule for composite functions we get

$$ \pdiff{}{x}(x^2+y^2)^{-3/2} = -\frac32(x^2+y^2)^{-5/2}\cdot2x = -3x(x^2+y^2)^{-5/2} = -\frac{3x}{|\vec P|^5} $$

The $y$ component is likewise $-\dfrac{3y}{|\vec P|^5}$, so $\nabla|\vec P|^{-3}=\left[-\dfrac{3x}{|\vec P|^5},-\dfrac{3y}{|\vec P|^5}\right]=-\dfrac{3}{|\vec P|^5}[x,y]=-\dfrac{3\vec P}{|\vec P|^5}$, which agrees completely with the result from Formula 18.3.

(b) Let $f=\ln|\vec P|$. Then $\big(\ln|\vec P|\big)^2=f^2$ is the composition of $f$ with the one-variable function $g(u)=u^2$ ($g'(u)=2u$). By Formula 18.1(IV),

$$ \nabla\big(\ln|\vec P|\big)^2 = g'(f)\cdot\nabla f = 2f\cdot\nabla f = 2\ln|\vec P|\cdot\nabla\ln|\vec P| $$

Substituting Formula 18.4 ($\nabla\ln|\vec P|=\dfrac{\vec P}{|\vec P|^2}$) here, we get

$$ \nabla\big(\ln|\vec P|\big)^2 = 2\ln|\vec P|\cdot\frac{\vec P}{|\vec P|^2} = \frac{2\ln|\vec P|}{|\vec P|^2}\,\vec P $$

(verified with SymPy: this agrees with the direct partial differentiation in the case of two variables)

Exercise 18.6: Geometric meaning of the gradient

Solution

Since $f(x,y)=2x^2+y^2$, we have $\pdiff{f}{x}=4x,\ \pdiff{f}{y}=2y$, and hence

$$ \nabla f = [4x,\,2y] \qquad\therefore\ \nabla f(1,1) = [4,\,2] $$

By item (i) of the Summary, the direction in which the value of $f$ increases fastest is the same as the direction of $\nabla f(1,1)=[4,2]$ (as a unit vector, $\left[\dfrac{2}{\sqrt5},\dfrac{1}{\sqrt5}\right]$).

Next, we find the slope of the tangent line to the level curve through the point $(1,1)$, $f(x,y)=f(1,1)=2+1=3$, that is, $2x^2+y^2=3$, by implicit differentiation. Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ gives

$$ 4x + 2yy' = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad y' = -\frac{4x}{2y} = -\frac{2x}{y} $$

At the point $(1,1)$ we have $y'=-\dfrac{2\cdot1}{1}=-2$, so we may take $[1,\,-2]$ as a direction vector of the tangent line.

Check of perpendicularity. The inner product of $\nabla f(1,1)=[4,2]$ and the direction vector $[1,-2]$ is

$$ [4,2]\cdot[1,-2] = 4\cdot1+2\cdot(-2) = 4-4 = 0 $$

so the two vectors are indeed orthogonal (as Theorem 18.1 states). (verified with SymPy)

Exercise 18.7: Equation of the tangent plane via the gradient vector

Solution

First we confirm that the point $(2,1,3)$ lies on the surface: $2^2-1^2=4-1=3$, which agrees. Writing the equation of the surface in the form $F(x,y,z)=0$, we have

$$ F(x,y,z) = x^2-y^2-z = 0 $$

Following the same procedure as in Example 18.7, we compute $\nabla F$.

$$ \nabla F = \left[\pdiff{F}{x},\,\pdiff{F}{y},\,\pdiff{F}{z}\right] = [\,2x,\ -2y,\ -1\,] $$

The normal vector at the point $(2,1,3)$ is

$$ \nabla F(2,1,3) = [\,2\cdot2,\ -2\cdot1,\ -1\,] = [4,\,-2,\,-1] $$

From this normal vector and the point $(2,1,3)$ through which the plane passes, the equation of the tangent plane is

$$ 4(x-2)-2(y-1)-(z-3) = 0 $$

Expanding the parentheses gives $4x-8-2y+2-z+3=0$, and combining the constant terms, we obtain

$$ 4x-2y-z-3 = 0 $$

(verified with SymPy)

← Back to Chapter 18, “Scalar Fields, Vector Fields, and the Gradient”

Chapter 19 Divergence and Curl

Exercise 19.1: Divergence of a polynomial vector field

Solution

We compute the divergence of $\vec F=[x^3,\ y^3,\ z^3]$ according to Definition 19.2. It suffices to differentiate each component partially with respect to its own variable only and add the results. We have $\pdiff{}{x}(x^3)=3x^2$ (differentiation of a power function; since it does not contain $y,z$, there is no need to differentiate with respect to the other variables), and similarly $\pdiff{}{y}(y^3)=3y^2$ and $\pdiff{}{z}(z^3)=3z^2$, so

$$ \operatorname{div}\vec F=3x^2+3y^2+3z^2 . $$

(SymPy check: diff(x**3,x)+diff(y**3,y)+diff(z**3,z) = 3*x**2+3*y**2+3*z**2.) As in the result $\operatorname{div}\vec p=$ (the number of dimensions) seen in Illustrations 19.1 and 19.2, we see that for a symmetric vector field whose components all have the same form ($x^3,y^3,z^3$), the divergence is also an expression symmetric in $x,y,z$.

Exercise 19.2: A spherically symmetric harmonic function

Solution

Let $r=\abs{\vec p}=\sqrt{x^2+y^2+z^2}$ ($\vec p=[x,y,z]$); then $f=\dfrac1r=r^{-1}$. As the hint suggests, we show the claim by the shortcut that uses the result of Illustration 19.2 in Section 19.1.

First, substituting $n=-1$ into Chapter 18, Formula 18.3, $\grad(r^n)=nr^{n-2}\vec p$, we obtain

$$ \grad f=\grad(r^{-1})=(-1)\cdot r^{-1-2}\vec p=-r^{-3}\vec p . $$

Next, by the definition of the Laplacian (Definition 19.4), $\Delta f=\operatorname{div}(\grad f)$, we have

$$ \Delta f=\operatorname{div}(-r^{-3}\vec p)=-\operatorname{div}(r^{-3}\vec p) $$

Now substituting $n=-3$ into the result $\operatorname{div}(r^n\vec p)=(n+3)r^n$ of Illustration 19.2(II), we get

$$ \operatorname{div}(r^{-3}\vec p)=(-3+3)r^{-3}=0\cdot r^{-3}=0 $$

(the point of this shortcut is that the coefficient comes out to be exactly $0$). Hence,

$$ \Delta f=-0=0 $$

and so $f=1/r$ ($r\neq0$) satisfies Laplace's equation; that is, it is a harmonic function. (SymPy check: simplify(diff(1/sqrt(x**2+y**2+z**2),x,2)+diff(...,y,2)+diff(...,z,2)) = 0.) At $r=0$ (the origin), $f$ itself cannot even be defined (the denominator is $0$), so note that this result is limited to the range $r\neq0$. This function $f=1/r$ appears frequently as the Coulomb potential in electrostatics and as the gravitational potential.

Exercise 19.3: Curl of the position vector field (a recheck)

Solution

For $\vec F=[x,\ y,\ z]$, we substitute $f=x,\ g=y,\ h=z$ into Definition 19.5.

$$ \rot\vec F=\left[\pdiff zy-\pdiff yz,\ \ \pdiff xz-\pdiff zx,\ \ \pdiff yx-\pdiff xy\right] $$

Every term is “a partial derivative of one variable with respect to a different variable.” Differentiating $z$ partially with respect to $y$ gives $0$, because $z$ is an independent variable unrelated to $y$; differentiating $y$ partially with respect to $z$ also gives $0$; and so on—all six partial derivatives are $0$. Therefore,

$$ \rot\vec F=[0-0,\ 0-0,\ 0-0]=[0,\ 0,\ 0]=\vec 0 $$

and Formula 19.5 ($\rot\vec P=\vec 0$) indeed holds. (Verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 19.4: Curl of a field mixing vortex and shear

Solution

For $\vec F=[-y,\ x,\ z]$, we substitute $f=-y,\ g=x,\ h=z$ into Definition 19.5.

$$ \rot\vec F=\left[\pdiff{z}{y}-\pdiff{x}{z},\ \ \pdiff{(-y)}{z}-\pdiff{z}{x},\ \ \pdiff{x}{x}-\pdiff{(-y)}{y}\right] =[0-0,\ \ 0-0,\ \ 1-(-1)]=[0,\ 0,\ 2] . $$

(Verified with SymPy.) We obtain exactly the same result $[0,0,2]$ as in Example 19.5 ($\vec F=[-y,x,0]$). This is because, even if we change the third component from $0$ to $z$, the partial derivatives $\pdiff hy=\pdiff zy=0$ and $\pdiff hx=\pdiff zx=0$ appearing in the first and second components of the curl both remain $0$ ($h=z$ does not contain $x,y$).

Interpretation: Viewed in the $xy$-plane, the $x,y$ components $[-y,x]$ of $\vec F$ represent exactly the same “counterclockwise rotation about the origin” as in Example 19.5. Superimposed on it, as the $z$ component, is a simple upward flow $z$ (a flow in which the velocity in the $z$ direction increases in proportion to the height $z$), so $\vec F$ as a whole represents a helical flow that “goes upward (faster where $z$ is larger) while rotating counterclockwise.” However, the curl vector $\rot\vec F=[0,0,2]$ itself is unaffected by this upward flow and takes the same value as in Example 19.5, representing only the strength and direction of the rotation in the $xy$-plane.

Exercise 19.5: Direct verification of the product formula for divergence

Solution

Let $\varphi=x$ and $\vec F=[y,\ z,\ x]$.

Left-hand side. We compute the divergence of $\varphi\vec F=[xy,\ xz,\ x^2]$ directly from Definition 19.2.

$$ \operatorname{div}(\varphi\vec F)=\pdiff{}{x}(xy)+\pdiff{}{y}(xz)+\pdiff{}{z}(x^2)=y+0+0=y . $$

($\pdiff{}{x}(xy)=y$: differentiate with respect to $x$, regarding $y$ as a constant. $\pdiff{}{y}(xz)=0$: $xz$ does not contain $y$. $\pdiff{}{z}(x^2)=0$: $x^2$ does not contain $z$.)

Right-hand side. We have $\grad\varphi=\grad(x)=[1,\ 0,\ 0]$. Also,

$$ \operatorname{div}\vec F=\pdiff{}{x}(y)+\pdiff{}{y}(z)+\pdiff{}{z}(x)=0+0+0=0 $$

so

$$ (\grad\varphi)\cdot\vec F+\varphi\operatorname{div}\vec F=[1,0,0]\cdot[y,z,x]+x\cdot0=(1\cdot y+0\cdot z+0\cdot x)+0=y . $$

The left-hand side and the right-hand side both equal $y$, so they agree. (Verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 19.6: Direct verification of the product formula for curl

Solution

Let $\varphi=xyz$ and $\vec F=[1,\ 1,\ 1]$.

Left-hand side. We compute the curl of $\varphi\vec F=[xyz,\ xyz,\ xyz]$ directly from Definition 19.5.

$$ \rot(\varphi\vec F)=\left[\pdiff{(xyz)}{y}-\pdiff{(xyz)}{z},\ \ \pdiff{(xyz)}{z}-\pdiff{(xyz)}{x},\ \ \pdiff{(xyz)}{x}-\pdiff{(xyz)}{y}\right] $$

Substituting $\pdiff{}{y}(xyz)=xz$, $\pdiff{}{z}(xyz)=xy$, and $\pdiff{}{x}(xyz)=yz$, we obtain

$$ \rot(\varphi\vec F)=[xz-xy,\ \ xy-yz,\ \ yz-xz] . $$

Right-hand side. We have $\grad\varphi=[yz,\ zx,\ xy]$. Substituting $\vec a=\grad\varphi=[yz,zx,xy]$ and $\vec b=\vec F=[1,1,1]$ into the component formula for the cross product from Chapter 15, $\vec a\times\vec b=[a_2b_3-a_3b_2,\ a_3b_1-a_1b_3,\ a_1b_2-a_2b_1]$, we obtain

$$ (\grad\varphi)\times\vec F=[zx\cdot1-xy\cdot1,\ \ xy\cdot1-yz\cdot1,\ \ yz\cdot1-zx\cdot1]=[zx-xy,\ \ xy-yz,\ \ yz-zx] . $$

Also, since $\vec F=[1,1,1]$ is a constant vector field, all the partial derivatives of its components are $0$, so $\rot\vec F=\vec 0$, and therefore $\varphi\rot\vec F=x yz\cdot\vec 0=\vec 0$. Hence,

$$ (\grad\varphi)\times\vec F+\varphi\rot\vec F=[zx-xy,\ xy-yz,\ yz-zx]+\vec 0=[zx-xy,\ xy-yz,\ yz-zx] . $$

This coincides exactly with the left-hand side $[xz-xy,\ xy-yz,\ yz-xz]$ (since $zx=xz$). (Verified with SymPy.)

← Back to Chapter 19, “Divergence and Curl”

Chapter 20 Line Integrals, Surface Integrals, and the Integral Theorems

Exercise 20.1: Find $\int_C f\,\dd s$ for $f(x,y)=x+y$ along $C:x=\cos\theta,y=\sin\theta\ (0\le\theta\le\pi)$

Solution

From $x(\theta)=\cos\theta,\ y(\theta)=\sin\theta$ we obtain $x'(\theta)=-\sin\theta,\ y'(\theta)=\cos\theta$. Therefore

$$ \sqrt{x'(\theta)^2+y'(\theta)^2}=\sqrt{\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta}=\sqrt1=1 $$

(Since this is the unit circle, the arc length element is always $\dd s=1\cdot\dd\theta$; this is just a restatement of the obvious fact that the length of an arc of a circle of radius 1 is equal to its central angle itself.) Also, $f(x(\theta),y(\theta))=\cos\theta+\sin\theta$. Hence

$$ \int_C f\,\dd s=\int_0^{\pi}(\cos\theta+\sin\theta)\,\dd\theta=\bigl[\sin\theta-\cos\theta\bigr]_0^{\pi} =(\sin\pi-\cos\pi)-(\sin0-\cos0)=(0-(-1))-(0-1)=1+1=2 . $$

Answer: $\displaystyle\int_C f\,\dd s=2$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 20.2: Find $\int_C\vec f\cdot\dd\vec p$ for $\vec f=[x,-y]$ along the straight line $C:(0,0)\to(2,2)$

Solution

Parametrizing the straight line $C$ as $x=t,\,y=t\ (0\le t\le2)$, we have $x'(t)=1,\ y'(t)=1$. Since the components of the vector field are $\vec f=[x,-y]=[t,-t]$,

$$ \int_C\vec f\cdot\dd\vec p=\int_0^2\bigl[t\cdot1+(-t)\cdot1\bigr]\dd t=\int_0^2(t-t)\,\dd t=\int_0^2 0\,\dd t=0 . $$

Answer: $\displaystyle\int_C\vec f\cdot\dd\vec p=0$ (verified with SymPy). On the straight line $y=x$ we have $\vec f=[x,-y]=[x,-x]$, which is exactly orthogonal to the direction of travel $[1,1]$ (the inner product is $x\cdot1+(-x)\cdot1=0$); that is why no work is done at all.

Exercise 20.3: Verify, using two different paths, that the line integral of the gradient of $f=x^2+y^2$ does not depend on the path

Solution

We have $\nabla f=[2x,2y]$.

(a) The straight line $(0,0)\to(1,1)$: taking $x=t,\,y=t\ (0\le t\le1)$ gives $x'=y'=1$, and with $\nabla f=[2t,2t]$,

$$ \int_{C_a}\nabla f\cdot\dd\vec p=\int_0^1(2t\cdot1+2t\cdot1)\,\dd t=\int_0^1 4t\,\dd t=\bigl[2t^2\bigr]_0^1=2 . $$

(b) The polygonal path $(0,0)\to(1,0)\to(1,1)$: we split it into two line segments and use additivity (Eq. (20-vec-additive)).

First segment $(0,0)\to(1,0)$: $x=t,\,y=0\ (0\le t\le1)$. With $\nabla f=[2t,0]$ and $x'=1,\,y'=0$,

$$ \int_{C_{b1}}\nabla f\cdot\dd\vec p=\int_0^1(2t\cdot1+0\cdot0)\,\dd t=\int_0^1 2t\,\dd t=\bigl[t^2\bigr]_0^1=1 . $$

Second segment $(1,0)\to(1,1)$: $x=1,\,y=t\ (0\le t\le1)$. With $\nabla f=[2,2t]$ and $x'=0,\,y'=1$,

$$ \int_{C_{b2}}\nabla f\cdot\dd\vec p=\int_0^1(2\cdot0+2t\cdot1)\,\dd t=\int_0^1 2t\,\dd t=\bigl[t^2\bigr]_0^1=1 . $$

Therefore, over the whole polygonal path, $\int_{C_b}\nabla f\cdot\dd\vec p=1+1=2$.

On the other hand, $f(1,1)-f(0,0)=(1^2+1^2)-(0^2+0^2)=2-0=2$, and (a), (b), and the claim of Theorem 20.1 all agree on the value $2$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 20.4: Find the area of the lateral surface $x^2+y^2=1,\ 0\le z\le1$ of a cylinder by a surface integral

Solution

Letting $\vec p(\theta,z)=[\cos\theta,\sin\theta,z]$ ($0\le\theta\le2\pi,\ 0\le z\le1$), we have

$$ \pdiff{\vec p}{\theta}=[-\sin\theta,\cos\theta,0],\qquad \pdiff{\vec p}{z}=[0,0,1] . $$

Computing the cross product by the criss-cross method, we obtain

$$ \pdiff{\vec p}{\theta}\times\pdiff{\vec p}{z}=(\cos\theta\cdot1-0\cdot0,\ 0\cdot0-(-\sin\theta)\cdot1,\ (-\sin\theta)\cdot0-\cos\theta\cdot0)=(\cos\theta,\ \sin\theta,\ 0) $$

whose magnitude is $\sqrt{\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta+0^2}=1$ (a constant). Therefore,

$$ \iint_S 1\,\dd S=\int_0^{2\pi}\!\!\int_0^1 1\,\dd z\,\dd\theta=\int_0^{2\pi}1\,\dd\theta=2\pi . $$

Answer: lateral area $=2\pi$ (verified with SymPy). This agrees exactly with the value $2\pi\cdot1\cdot1=2\pi$ given by the formula learned in high school, “lateral area of a cylinder = circumference of the base × height.” The reason for this agreement is that the lateral surface of a cylinder rises vertically at every point, so the Jacobian (the area correction factor) is always $1$.

Exercise 20.5: Verify Gauss's divergence theorem for a ball of radius 2

Solution

The divergence of $\vec f=[x,y,z]$ is $\nabla\cdot\vec f=1+1+1=3$ (the same as in Example 20.8). Since the volume of a ball of radius 2 is $\dfrac43\pi\cdot2^3=\dfrac{32}{3}\pi$, the volume integral is

$$ \iiint_V(\nabla\cdot\vec f)\,\dd V=3\times\frac{32}{3}\pi=32\pi . $$

For the surface integral, we use the fact that on the sphere $x^2+y^2+z^2=4$ the outward unit normal is $\hat n=\dfrac{(x,y,z)}{2}$ (the position vector normalized by dividing by its magnitude 2).

$$ \vec f\cdot\hat n=(x,y,z)\cdot\frac{(x,y,z)}{2}=\frac{x^2+y^2+z^2}{2}=\frac{4}{2}=2\quad(\text{always constant on the sphere}) $$

so, using the surface area $4\pi(2)^2=16\pi$ of the sphere of radius 2, we obtain

$$ \iint_S\vec f\cdot\dd\vec S=\iint_S 2\,\dd S=2\times16\pi=32\pi . $$

Both sides equal $32\pi$, so they agree (verified with SymPy). When the radius is doubled, the volume-integral side grows as the cube of the radius ($2^3=8$ times); the surface-integral side also grows as the cube of the radius, because two facts combine: “the value on the surface grows in proportion to the radius ($\vec f\cdot\hat n=$ radius)” and “the surface area grows as the square of the radius”—and we see that this is consistent with dimensional analysis as well.

Exercise 20.6: Verify Stokes's theorem for $\vec f=[y,-x,0]$ and compare the sign with Example 20.9

Solution

Along the unit circle $C:\vec p(\theta)=[\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0]\ (0\le\theta\le2\pi,\text{ counterclockwise})$, we have $f=y=\sin\theta,\ g=-x=-\cos\theta$, so

$$ \oint_C\vec f\cdot\dd\vec\ell=\int_0^{2\pi}\bigl[\sin\theta\cdot(-\sin\theta)+(-\cos\theta)\cdot\cos\theta\bigr]\dd\theta =\int_0^{2\pi}\bigl[-\sin^2\theta-\cos^2\theta\bigr]\dd\theta=\int_0^{2\pi}(-1)\,\dd\theta=-2\pi . $$

On the other hand, the curl is $\nabla\times\vec f=\left[\pdiff{}{y}0-\pdiff{}{z}(-x),\ \pdiff{}{z}y-\pdiff{}{x}0,\ \pdiff{}{x}(-x)-\pdiff{}{y}y\right]=[0,0,-1-1]=[0,0,-2]$. Since $S$ is the unit disk in the $xy$-plane and $\dd\vec S=[0,0,1]\dd S$, we have $(\nabla\times\vec f)\cdot\dd\vec S=-2\,\dd S$, and

$$ \iint_S(\nabla\times\vec f)\cdot\dd\vec S=-2\times(\text{area of the unit disk})=-2\times\pi=-2\pi . $$

Both sides equal $-2\pi$, so they agree (verified with SymPy). For $\vec f=[-y,x,0]$ in Example 20.9 the answer was $+2\pi$, whereas this time, for $\vec f=[y,-x,0]=-[-y,x,0]$ (a vector field with exactly the opposite sign), the answer also turned out to be $-2\pi$ (the opposite sign). Since both the line integral and the surface integral are linear (of degree one) in $\vec f$, reversing the sign of $\vec f$ as a whole reverses the signs of the answers on both sides in exactly the same way—this is a natural consequence of the fact that the very definitions of the line integral and the surface integral are linear operations that merely “substitute $\vec f$ and add up.”

← Back to Chapter 20, “Line Integrals, Surface Integrals, and the Integral Theorems”

Chapter 21 Complex Numbers and the Complex Plane

Exercise 21.1: $z^3,\ z^{-1}$ for $z=2e^{i\pi/3}$

Solution

We have $z=2e^{i\pi/3}=2\left(\cos\dfrac{\pi}3+i\sin\dfrac{\pi}3\right)$. By de Moivre's theorem (the formula in Chapter 21), in general $z^n=r^n(\cos n\theta+i\sin n\theta)$ holds.

For $z^3$. Substituting $r=2,\theta=\pi/3,n=3$, we obtain

$$z^3=2^3\left(\cos\left(3\cdot\frac{\pi}3\right)+i\sin\left(3\cdot\frac{\pi}3\right)\right)=8(\cos\pi+i\sin\pi)$$

Since $\cos\pi=-1,\ \sin\pi=0$,

$$z^3=8(-1+i\cdot0)=-8$$

For $z^{-1}$. Substituting $n=-1$, we obtain

$$z^{-1}=2^{-1}\left(\cos\left(-\frac{\pi}3\right)+i\sin\left(-\frac{\pi}3\right)\right)=\frac12(\cos\tfrac{\pi}3-i\sin\tfrac{\pi}3)$$

(Since $\cos$ is an even function and $\sin$ is an odd function, $\cos(-\pi/3)=\cos(\pi/3)$ and $\sin(-\pi/3)=-\sin(\pi/3)$.) Substituting $\cos\frac{\pi}3=\frac12,\ \sin\frac{\pi}3=\frac{\sqrt3}2$, we obtain

$$z^{-1}=\frac12\left(\frac12-i\frac{\sqrt3}2\right)=\frac14-\frac{\sqrt3}4i$$

(Check: $z\cdot z^{-1}=2e^{i\pi/3}\cdot\frac12e^{-i\pi/3}=1\cdot e^{i(\pi/3-\pi/3)}=e^{0}=1$, which shows that $z^{-1}$ is indeed the reciprocal of $z$.)

Exercise 21.2: Computing $(1+i)^{10}$

Solution

We convert $1+i$ to polar form. Since $a=1,b=1$, we have $r=\sqrt{1^2+1^2}=\sqrt2$. The angle satisfying $\cos\theta=\dfrac1{\sqrt2},\sin\theta=\dfrac1{\sqrt2}$ is $\theta=\dfrac{\pi}4$. Hence $1+i=\sqrt2\left(\cos\dfrac{\pi}4+i\sin\dfrac{\pi}4\right)$. Using de Moivre's theorem with $n=10$, we obtain

$$(1+i)^{10}=(\sqrt2)^{10}\left(\cos\left(10\cdot\frac{\pi}4\right)+i\sin\left(10\cdot\frac{\pi}4\right)\right)$$

Here $(\sqrt2)^{10}=(2^{1/2})^{10}=2^5=32$. Also, $10\cdot\dfrac{\pi}4=\dfrac{10\pi}4=\dfrac{5\pi}2$, and this angle is equivalent to $\dfrac{5\pi}2-2\pi=\dfrac{\pi}2$ (the functions $\cos,\sin$ have period $2\pi$). Therefore,

$$(1+i)^{10}=32\left(\cos\frac{\pi}2+i\sin\frac{\pi}2\right)=32(0+i\cdot1)=32i$$

(Check: $(1+i)^2=1+2i+i^2=2i$. $(1+i)^4=(2i)^2=4i^2=-4$. $(1+i)^8=(-4)^2=16$. $(1+i)^{10}=(1+i)^8\cdot(1+i)^2=16\times2i=32i$, which agrees with the result above.)

Exercise 21.3: The $n$th roots of $1$ form a regular $n$-gon (general $n$)

Solution

Let $z_k=e^{i2k\pi/n}$ ($k=0,1,\ldots,n-1$). We compute the distance between two adjacent points.

$$|z_{k+1}-z_k|=\left|e^{i\frac{2(k+1)\pi}n}-e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}\right|=\left|e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}\left(e^{i\frac{2\pi}n}-1\right)\right|$$

(We used the law of exponents $e^{i\frac{2(k+1)\pi}n}=e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}\cdot e^{i\frac{2\pi}n}$ and factored out $e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}$.) The product of absolute values equals the absolute value of the product ($|AB|=|A||B|$, which corresponds to the case $r_1=|A|,r_2=|B|$ of Formula 21.3), so

$$=\left|e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}\right|\cdot\left|e^{i\frac{2\pi}n}-1\right|$$

By Formula 21.5 ($|e^{i\theta}|=1$), $\left|e^{i\frac{2k\pi}n}\right|=1$ (it is always $1$, regardless of $k$). Therefore,

$$|z_{k+1}-z_k|=\left|e^{i\frac{2\pi}n}-1\right|$$

The right-hand side does not contain $k$; that is, it is constant, independent of $k$. (Incidentally, if we actually compute this value, we obtain $\left|e^{i\frac{2\pi}n}-1\right|^2=\left(\cos\frac{2\pi}n-1\right)^2+\sin^2\frac{2\pi}n=2-2\cos\frac{2\pi}n=4\sin^2\frac{\pi}n$ (we used the half-angle formula $1-\cos\phi=2\sin^2(\phi/2)$ with $\phi=2\pi/n$), so the distance between two adjacent points is $2\sin(\pi/n)$.)

Moreover, as we saw in Section 21.5.3, all the $z_k$ lie at distance $1$ from the origin (on the unit circle), and their arguments are equally spaced at intervals of $\frac{2\pi}n$. The statement “$n$ points equidistant from the center are arranged so that the distances between adjacent vertices are all equal and the central angles are also equal” is precisely the definition of a regular $n$-gon, so for any natural number $n\ (\ge3)$ the $n$th roots of $1$ form the vertices of a regular $n$-gon inscribed in the unit circle.

Exercise 21.4: Proof that $\tanh ix=i\tan x$

Solution

Substituting $y=ix$ into the definition of the hyperbolic tangent $\tanh y=\dfrac{\sinh y}{\cosh y}$, we obtain

$$\tanh ix=\frac{\sinh ix}{\cosh ix}$$

Substituting Formula 21.10 (i) $\cosh ix=\cos x$ and Formula 21.10 (ii) $\sinh ix=i\sin x$, we obtain

$$\tanh ix=\frac{i\sin x}{\cos x}=i\cdot\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}$$

Since $\tan x=\dfrac{\sin x}{\cos x}$ (the definition of the tangent),

$$\therefore \tanh ix=i\tan x$$

(Check: for $x=\pi/4$, the right-hand side $=i\tan(\pi/4)=i\cdot1=i$. For the left-hand side, $\sinh(i\pi/4)=i\sin(\pi/4)=i\frac{\sqrt2}2$ and $\cosh(i\pi/4)=\cos(\pi/4)=\frac{\sqrt2}2$, so $\tanh(i\pi/4)=\dfrac{i\sqrt2/2}{\sqrt2/2}=i$, which agrees.)

Exercise 21.5: The line equation when $\alpha$ is a pure imaginary number

Solution

Recall the correspondence $\alpha=\dfrac a2+\dfrac b2i$ of Formula 21.11 ($a,b$ are the coefficients of the original line $ax+by+c=0$). That $\alpha$ is a pure imaginary number means that its real part is $0$, that is, $\dfrac a2=0$, in other words $a=0$.

Substituting $a=0$ into the equation of the line $ax+by+c=0$, we obtain

$$by+c=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ y=-\frac cb\quad(b\ne0)$$

This is an equation that does not contain $x$; that is, it is a line parallel to the real axis (a horizontal line).

We can also check this directly using the equation in $z,\bar z$. When $\alpha=\dfrac b2i$ (a pure imaginary number), $\bar\alpha=-\dfrac b2i$, so the equation of Formula 21.11 becomes

$$-\frac b2iz+\frac b2i\bar z+c=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \frac b2i(\bar z-z)+c=0$$

Substituting $z=x+iy,\bar z=x-iy$, we have $\bar z-z=-2iy$, so

$$\frac b2i\cdot(-2iy)+c=-bi^2y+c=by+c=0$$

(We used $i^2=-1$.) This is indeed $by+c=0$, that is, a horizontal line, in agreement with the argument above.

Exercise 21.6: The circle equation when $\alpha=0$

Solution

Substituting $\alpha=0$ into $C=\alpha\bar\alpha-r^2$ of Formula 21.12, we have $\bar\alpha=\overline{0}=0$, so

$$C=0\cdot0-r^2=-r^2$$

Also, substituting $\alpha=0$ (hence $\alpha\bar z=0,\ \bar\alpha z=0$) and $C=-r^2$ into the equation $z\bar z-(\alpha\bar z+\bar\alpha z)+C=0$ of Formula 21.12, we obtain

$$z\bar z-(0+0)+(-r^2)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ z\bar z-r^2=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ z\bar z=r^2$$

This coincides exactly with what we get by squaring both sides of $|z-0|=r$, that is, $|z|=r$, the equation of the circle with center at the origin ($\alpha=0$) and radius $r$, to obtain $|z|^2=r^2$, and then rewriting it using Formula 21.1 ($|z|^2=z\bar z$). Thus we have verified that Formula 21.12 gives, for $\alpha=0$, a special case that is consistent and correct.
