Chapter 22 Complex Functions and Mappings

Exercise 22.1: Is $W=\bar z$ (the conjugation mapping) one-to-one or many-to-one?

Solution

Let $z=x+iy$. Since $\bar z=x-iy$, we have

$$ u(x,y)=x,\qquad v(x,y)=-y $$

If the value of $(u,v)$ is known, then, setting

$$ x=u,\qquad y=-v $$

the original $z=x+iy=u-iv$ can be recovered in exactly one way. Hence distinct $(x,y)$ always give distinct $(u,v)$, so this mapping is one-to-one.

Geometrically, the correspondence $(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y)$ is the operation that flips only the sign of the $y$-coordinate, so it is nothing other than a reflection across the real axis (the $x$-axis). This is the same geometric operation, the “reflection across the real axis,” that appeared in Section 22.4 when we examined the polar form of $W=1/z$.

Exercise 22.2: The image of the imaginary axis under $W=z^2+1$

Solution

A point on the imaginary axis can be written $z=iy$ ($y$ an arbitrary real number, $x=0$). Substituting $x=0$ into the equations of Chapter 22 in the main text ($u=x^2-y^2,\ v=2xy$), we obtain

$$ u(0,y)=0^2-y^2=-y^2,\qquad v(0,y)=2\cdot0\cdot y=0 $$

so the real and imaginary parts of $W=z^2+1$ are

$$ u=1-y^2,\qquad v=0 $$

Since $v$ is identically $0$, the image lies on the real axis of the $W$-plane ($v=0$). As $y$ ranges over all real numbers, $y^2\ge0$ gives $1-y^2\le1$, and the maximum value $u=1$ is attained when $y=0$. Also, as $y\to\pm\infty$ we have $u\to-\infty$, so $u$ takes every value from $-\infty$ up to $1$. Therefore the image is

$$ \{u+iv : u\le1,\ v=0\} $$

that is, a half-line of the real axis in the $W$-plane (the part not exceeding $1$). Note also that $y$ and $-y$ give the same $u=1-y^2$, so (except at $y=0$) this mapping is two-to-one on the imaginary axis (e.g., $y=\pm2\to u=-3$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 22.3: The image of the circle $\abs z=2$ under $W=1/\bar z$

Solution

A point on the circle $\abs z=2$ can be written in polar form as $z=2e^{i\theta}$ ($0\le\theta\lt2\pi$). Taking the conjugate gives $\bar z=2e^{-i\theta}$, so

$$ w=\frac1{\bar z}=\frac1{2e^{-i\theta}}=\frac12\,e^{i\theta} $$

Hence the absolute value of $w$ is $\abs w=\dfrac12$ (constant, independent of the value of $\theta$), and its argument is $\arg w=\theta$ (the same as the argument of the original $z$). As $\theta$ runs from $0$ up to (but not including) $2\pi$, $w=\frac12e^{i\theta}$ goes around the circle with center at the origin and radius $\frac12$ exactly once. Therefore the image is the circle

$$ \abs w=\frac12 $$

(we also verified numerically that $\abs w=0.5$ for several values of $\theta$). Compare this with Formula 22.3 in the main text (for $W=1/z$ we had $R=1/r,\ \Theta=-\theta$, so the sign of the argument was reversed), and note the difference: because the conjugate is taken first, the sign of the argument is reversed once more, so in the end the original argument $\theta$ is preserved unchanged.

Exercise 22.4: Where the linear fractional function $W=\dfrac{z-1}{z+1}$ sends the special points

Solution

When $z=1$: Substituting directly, we get

$$ W=\frac{1-1}{1+1}=\frac02=0 $$

When $z=-1$: The denominator becomes $\gamma z+\delta=z+1=0$, and the absolute value of $W$ becomes arbitrarily large ($\abs W\to\infty$ as $z$ approaches $-1$). Following Definition 22.4, we agree that $z=-1$ is mapped to $W=\infty$ (the point at infinity).

When $z=\infty$: Dividing the numerator and denominator by $z$ gives

$$ W=\frac{z-1}{z+1}=\frac{1-\dfrac1z}{1+\dfrac1z} $$

As $z\to\infty$ we have $\dfrac1z\to0$, so

$$ W\to\frac{1-0}{1+0}=1 $$

Hence $z=\infty$ is mapped to $W=1$. To summarize, $z=1\to W=0$, $z=-1\to W=\infty$, and $z=\infty\to W=1$ (also verified with SymPy's limit computation). In general, for a linear fractional function $W=\dfrac{\alpha z+\beta}{\gamma z+\delta}$, the same computation shows that $z=-\delta/\gamma$ (the point that makes the denominator $0$) corresponds to $W=\infty$, and that $z=\infty$ corresponds to $W=\alpha/\gamma$ (the $C_2$ of Formula 22.4).

Exercise 22.5: The image of the unit circle under $W=z^n$ winds around the unit circle $n$ times

Solution

A point on the unit circle can be written $z=e^{i\theta}$ ($0\le\theta\lt2\pi$). Then

$$ W=z^n=e^{in\theta} $$

As $\theta$ runs from $0$ up to (but not including) $2\pi$ (exactly once around the unit circle), $n\theta$ runs from $0$ up to (but not including) $2\pi n$. Since $e^{i\phi}$ is a periodic function that returns to the original point each time $\phi$ increases by $2\pi$, the fact that $n\theta$ runs from $0$ to $2\pi n$ corresponds to going around the unit circle exactly $n$ times. Therefore the image is the unit circle $\abs W=1$ (the same as the original), and it winds around the unit circle exactly $n$ times.

We also see at the same time that this mapping is $n$-to-one: the points in the $z$-plane that correspond to a point $W_0=e^{i\Phi_0}$ on the unit circle in the $W$-plane are those with $\theta$ satisfying $n\theta=\Phi_0+2\pi k$ ($k$ an integer)—which is also written as the congruence $n\theta\equiv\Phi_0\pmod{2\pi}$ (“congruent modulo $2\pi$,” a notation meaning that the difference $n\theta-\Phi_0$ is an integer multiple of $2\pi$)—that is,

$$ \theta=\frac{\Phi_0}n+\frac{2\pi k}n\qquad(k=0,1,\dots,n-1) $$

These are $n$ angles in all, and this is nothing other than Theorem 22.1 (the $n$-to-one property of $W=z^n$) restricted to the unit circle (for the case $n=4$ we computed with a specific $\theta_0$ and verified with SymPy that four distinct values of $\theta$ give the same $W$).

Exercise 22.6: Which circle corresponds to the equator of the Riemann sphere?

Solution

Let $R$ be the radius of the sphere, let $S$ (the point where the sphere touches the $z$-plane) be the origin, and let $h$ be the height measured from it ($0\le h\le2R$; $h=0$ is $S$ and $h=2R$ is $N$). The center of the sphere is at height $R$ above $S$, so if we let $\rho$ be the radius of the cross section of the sphere at height $h$ (the radius in the direction parallel to the $z$-plane), then the equation of the sphere (in the cross section: the circle with center $(0,R)$ and radius $R$) is

$$ \rho^2+(h-R)^2=R^2 $$

Consider the line drawn from $N=(0,2R)$ through a point on the sphere at height $h$ and horizontal distance $\rho$, and extended down to the $z$-plane (height $0$). The height from $N$ to the $z$-plane is $2R$, and the difference in height from $N$ to the point at height $h$ is $2R-h$. So, by similar triangles (two right triangles with vertex $N$, in which the horizontal extent at vertical distance $2R-h$ from the vertex is $\rho$, and the horizontal extent at vertical distance $2R$ from the vertex, namely at the $z$-plane, is $r$), the following relation holds:

$$ \frac{r}{2R}=\frac{\rho}{2R-h}\qquad\therefore\ r=\rho\cdot\frac{2R}{2R-h} $$

The equator is the great circle exactly midway between $S$ and $N$, that is, at height $h=R$. Substituting $h=R$ into the equation of the sphere, we get

$$ \rho^2+(R-R)^2=R^2\quad\therefore\ \rho=R $$

(at height $R$ the radius of the cross section of the sphere is exactly equal to the radius $R$ of the sphere itself; that is, this is the “widest” part of the sphere). Substituting this into the formula for $r$, we obtain

$$ r=R\cdot\frac{2R}{2R-R}=R\cdot\frac{2R}{R}=2R $$

Therefore the equator corresponds to the circle in the $z$-plane with center at the origin and radius $2R$ (a circle whose radius equals the diameter of the sphere),

$$ \abs z=2R $$

(also verified with SymPy that $\rho=R,\ r=2R$). Let us check the endpoints as well. At $h=0$ (the south pole $S$), $\rho^2=0\cdot2R=0$ gives $\rho=0$, and hence it corresponds to $r=0\cdot\dfrac{2R}{2R}=0$ (the origin). In the case $h\to2R$ (the north pole $N$), not only the denominator $2R-h$ but also the numerator $\rho$ approaches $0$ ($\rho^2=h(2R-h)\to2R\cdot0=0$), so we cannot simply say “the denominator is $0$, so $r\to\infty$”; we need to compare how the two approach zero. So set $h=2R-\varepsilon$ ($\varepsilon\to0^+$). Then

$$ \rho^2=h(2R-h)=(2R-\varepsilon)\varepsilon=2R\varepsilon-\varepsilon^2\approx2R\varepsilon\quad(\varepsilon\to0^+\ \text{limit}) $$

gives $\rho\approx\sqrt{2R\varepsilon}$ (the numerator approaches $0$ at the order of the $\frac12$ power of $\varepsilon$). On the other hand, the denominator is $2R-h=\varepsilon$ (on the order of the $1$st power of $\varepsilon$), so

$$ r=\rho\cdot\frac{2R}{2R-h}\approx\sqrt{2R\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}=2R\sqrt{\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty\qquad(\varepsilon\to0^+) $$

(the numerator is of order $\sqrt\varepsilon$ and the denominator is of order $\varepsilon$, and since the denominator approaches $0$ faster, the whole expression diverges—it is an indeterminate form $0/0$, but by comparing the orders we can conclude that $r\to\infty$). In this way we have confirmed that $h\to2R$ (the north pole $N$) corresponds to $r\to\infty$ (the point at infinity), which is consistent with the content of Definitions 22.4 and 22.5. That is, the lower half of the sphere (the side near $S$) corresponds to points near the origin of the $z$-plane, the equator corresponds to the circle of radius $2R$, and the upper half (the side near $N$) corresponds to points outside the circle of radius $2R$.

← Back to Chapter 22, “Complex Functions and Mappings”

Chapter 23 Elementary Complex Functions — Exponential, Logarithm, Power, and Trigonometric Functions

Exercise 23.1: Solving an exponential equation

Solution

We write $-2$ in polar form. We have $\abs{-2}=2$, and since $-2$ lies on the negative part of the real axis, its argument is $\pi$ (or that value plus an integer multiple of $2\pi$). That is,

$$ -2=2e^{i(\pi+2n\pi)}\qquad(n\in\Z) $$

By Definition 23.2 (complex logarithm), any $z$ satisfying $e^z=-2$ is given by $z=\log(-2)$, and so

$$ z=\log\abs{-2}+i\arg(-2)=\log2+i(\pi+2n\pi)\qquad(n\in\Z) $$

As a check, we verify the case $n=0$. We get $e^{\log2+i\pi}=e^{\log2}\cdot e^{i\pi}=2\cdot(\cos\pi+i\sin\pi)=2\cdot(-1+0)=-2$, so the equation is indeed satisfied (for the other values of $n$, the same holds by the periodicity in property (iv), $e^{i2n\pi}=1$).

Answer: $z=\log2+i(\pi+2n\pi)$ ($n\in\Z$). There are infinitely many solutions.

Exercise 23.2: The principal value of a complex logarithm

Solution

Let $z=3+4i$. The absolute value is

$$ \abs z=\sqrt{3^2+4^2}=\sqrt{9+16}=\sqrt{25}=5 $$

(the right triangle with sides $3,4,5$). As for the argument, since $z$ lies in the first quadrant (the real and imaginary parts are both positive), within the range of principal values $-\pi\lt\arg z\le\pi$ we have

$$ \arg z=\tan^{-1}\frac43 $$

(since the denominator is positive, the case-dependent correction of $\pm\pi$ is not needed). Therefore, by Definition 23.2, the principal value is

$$ \log(3+4i)=\log5+i\tan^{-1}\frac43 $$

Numerically, $\log5=1.6094\ldots$ and $\tan^{-1}(4/3)=0.9273\ldots$ (in radians), so $\log(3+4i)=1.6094\ldots+0.9273\ldots\,i$ (verified with SymPy and numpy: the value agrees with $e^{1.6094+0.9273i}=3+4i$).

Answer: $\log(3+4i)=\log5+i\tan^{-1}(4/3)$.

Exercise 23.3: A square root as a complex power

Solution

By Definition 23.3, $z^c=e^{c\log z}$. Substituting $z=-1$ and $c=1/2$ and using the principal value $\log(-1)=i\pi$ found in Example 23.3, we get

$$ (-1)^{1/2}=e^{\frac12\log(-1)}=e^{\frac12\cdot i\pi}=e^{i\pi/2} $$

By Euler's formula, $e^{i\pi/2}=\cos\dfrac\pi2+i\sin\dfrac\pi2=0+i\cdot1=i$. Therefore

$$ (-1)^{1/2}=i $$

This agrees exactly with what you remember from high school mathematics, $\sqrt{-1}=i$. That is, even if we start from the definition of complex powers, the principal value of $\sqrt{-1}$ settles on the familiar $i$. (However, if we use a general branch $n\ne0$, we get $(-1)^{1/2}=e^{i(\pi/2+n\pi)}$, and when $n$ is odd we also obtain $-i$. The fact that the square root has “two values” can be explained here in the language of the branches of the logarithm.)

Answer: $(-1)^{1/2}=i$ (principal value). It agrees with what we remember about the ordinary square root.

Exercise 23.4: The image of a strip (revisited)

Solution

By the derivation in Section 23.2, for $z=x+iy$ the function $e^z$ gives the correspondence $R=e^x$, $\theta=y$ in polar form. In the strip-shaped region $\{0\le y\lt2\pi\}$, $x$ can range over all real numbers without restriction, so $R=e^x$ takes every value in $(0,\infty)$ ($R\to0$ as $x\to-\infty$ and $R\to\infty$ as $x\to+\infty$), and since $y$ ranges over $[0,2\pi)$, $\theta$ takes exactly one full turn's worth of the possible values of the argument (from $0$ up to, but not including, $2\pi$). Therefore this strip is mapped exactly one-to-one, with no overlap, onto the whole $W$-plane with the origin removed (as we saw in Figure 23.3).

Suppose we take the width of the strip wider than $2\pi$, for example $\{0\le y\lt4\pi\}$. Then the range of $y$ exceeds one full turn of the argument ($2\pi$). In that case, for example, the point with $y=\pi/2$ and the point with $y=\pi/2+2\pi=5\pi/2$ are different values of $z$ for the same value of $x$, and as values of $\theta=y$ they appear to be different, namely $\pi/2$ and $5\pi/2$; but since the argument does not distinguish differences of integer multiples of $2\pi$, they point in the same direction (the same $\theta\bmod2\pi$). That is, for the same $x$ (that is, the same $R=e^x$), two distinct points $y$ and $y+2\pi$ are mapped to the same image point in the $W$-plane. This is precisely property (iv) of the previous section (the periodicity $e^{z+2\pi i}=e^z$): once the width of the strip exceeds $2\pi$, the mapping is no longer one-to-one but becomes a two-to-one correspondence (and, if we widen the strip further, a three-to-one correspondence, and so on).

Answer: A strip of width $2\pi$ is mapped one-to-one onto the whole $W$-plane with the origin removed, but if the width of the strip is taken wider than $2\pi$, the correspondence becomes many-to-one because of the periodicity.

Exercise 23.5: A complex trigonometric equation

Solution

By Theorem 23.2, $\cos z=\dfrac{e^{iz}+e^{-iz}}2$. Setting $\cos z=2$, we get

$$ \frac{e^{iz}+e^{-iz}}2=2\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad e^{iz}+e^{-iz}=4 $$

Setting $t=e^{iz}$ (and noting that $e^{-iz}=1/t$), we have

$$ t+\frac1t=4\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad t^2-4t+1=0 $$

This is a quadratic equation in $t$. By the quadratic formula, we get

$$ t=\frac{4\pm\sqrt{16-4}}2=\frac{4\pm2\sqrt3}2=2\pm\sqrt3 $$

Note that $2+\sqrt3=3.732\ldots\gt0$ and $2-\sqrt3=0.268\ldots\gt0$ are both positive real numbers. For $t=e^{iz}$, the fact that $t$ is a positive real number means that $iz$ is real ($z$ is a pure imaginary number). In the case $t=2+\sqrt3$, we have $iz=\log(2+\sqrt3)$ (the real logarithm), so

$$ z=-i\log(2+\sqrt3) $$

Check: $\cos\bigl(-i\log(2+\sqrt3)\bigr)=\cosh\bigl(\log(2+\sqrt3)\bigr)=\dfrac{(2+\sqrt3)+(2+\sqrt3)^{-1}}2$. Since $(2+\sqrt3)^{-1}=2-\sqrt3$ (because $(2+\sqrt3)(2-\sqrt3)=4-3=1$), this equals $\dfrac{(2+\sqrt3)+(2-\sqrt3)}2=\dfrac42=2$, so indeed $\cos z=2$ is satisfied (verified numerically with SymPy and numpy). The other solution $t=2-\sqrt3$ gives $z=-i\log(2-\sqrt3)=i\log(2+\sqrt3)$ (since $\log(2-\sqrt3)=-\log(2+\sqrt3)$), and this too satisfies $\cos z=2$ by the same check.

Answer: $z=-i\log(2+\sqrt3)$ (or equivalently $z=i\log(2+\sqrt3)$) satisfies $\cos z=2$. In the world of real numbers, $\abs{\cos\theta}\le1$, so there is no real number $\theta$ satisfying $\cos\theta=2$, but in the world of complex numbers a solution exists.

Exercise 23.6: The logarithmic mapping: the image of a half-line

Solution

A point on the half-line $\arg z=\pi/4$ ($z\neq0$) can be written in polar form as $z=re^{i\pi/4}$ ($r\gt0$ arbitrary). Then we have

$$ \log z=\log r+i\cdot\frac\pi4 $$

As $r$ runs over all values in $(0,\infty)$, $\log r$ takes every value in $(-\infty,\infty)$ (as the range of the real logarithm function). Meanwhile, the imaginary part always stays equal to $\pi/4$ and does not change. Therefore the image is given by

$$ v=\frac\pi4\qquad(u\ \text{is any real number}) $$

That is, the image is the horizontal line $v=\pi/4$ in the $W$-plane (a line parallel to the $u$-axis). This is the case $\theta_0=\pi/4$ of the general rule seen in Example 23.5, “a half-line is mapped to a horizontal line” (the half-line $\arg z=\theta_0$ is mapped to the horizontal line $v=\theta_0$).

Answer: The image is the horizontal line $v=\pi/4$ ($u\in\R$).

← Back to Chapter 23, “Elementary Complex Functions — Exponential, Logarithm, Power, and Trigonometric Functions”

Chapter 24 Holomorphic Functions and the Cauchy–Riemann Equations

Exercise 24.1: Holomorphy of $f(z)=x^2-y^2+2ixy$

Solution

Let $u(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ and $v(x,y)=2xy$. The partial derivatives are

$$ u_x=2x,\quad u_y=-2y,\quad v_x=2y,\quad v_y=2x $$

Therefore $u_x=2x=v_y$ and $u_y=-2y=-(2y)=-v_x$, so the C-R equations hold identically for all $(x,y)$. Hence $f(z)$ is holomorphic on the whole plane (all of $\mathbb C$). By Corollary 24.1, the derivative is

$$ f'(z) = u_x+iv_x = 2x+i\cdot2y = 2(x+iy) = 2z $$

Incidentally, if we notice that $(x+iy)^2=x^2-y^2+2ixy$, we see that $f(z)$ is in fact $z^2$ itself, and that the result is consistent with the computation for $z^2+3z$ in Example 24.5 (or with $(x^2)'=2x$ from high school mathematics) (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 24.2: Harmonic conjugate—constructing a holomorphic function from $u=x^3-3xy^2$

Solution

We find the partial derivatives of $u(x,y)=x^3-3xy^2$.

$$ u_x = 3x^2-3y^2 ,\qquad u_y = -6xy $$

From the C-R equation $v_y=u_x$,

$$ v_y = 3x^2-3y^2 $$

Integrating this with respect to $y$ (regarding $x$ as a constant), we obtain

$$ v(x,y) = \int(3x^2-3y^2)\,dy = 3x^2y-y^3+h(x) $$

(Instead of an integration constant, an arbitrary function $h(x)$ that does not depend on $y$ appears—in order to capture all the terms that vanish upon differentiation with respect to $x$.) We determine $h(x)$ by using the other C-R equation, $v_x=-u_y$. Differentiating the left-hand side partially with respect to $x$ gives

$$ v_x = 6xy+h'(x) $$

The right-hand side is $-u_y=-(-6xy)=6xy$. Comparing the two sides,

$$ 6xy+h'(x) = 6xy \quad\Longrightarrow\quad h'(x)=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad h(x)=C\ (\text{constant}) $$

Taking the simplest case $C=0$, we get

$$ v(x,y) = 3x^2y-y^3 $$

Therefore the required holomorphic function is

$$ f(z) = u+iv = (x^3-3xy^2)+i(3x^2y-y^3) $$

Indeed, expanding gives $(x+iy)^3=x^3+3x^2(iy)+3x(iy)^2+(iy)^3=x^3+3ix^2y-3xy^2-iy^3=(x^3-3xy^2)+i(3x^2y-y^3)$, so this is precisely $f(z)=z^3$. Checking the derivative by Corollary 24.1,

$$ f'(z) = u_x+iv_x = (3x^2-3y^2)+i(6xy) = 3(x^2-y^2+2ixy) = 3(x+iy)^2 = 3z^2 $$

This has the same form as $(x^3)'=3x^2$ in high school mathematics, and it agrees with the natural result $f(z)=z^3$, $f'(z)=3z^2$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 24.3: Holomorphy and derivative of $f(z)=\cos z$

Solution

By the “Math Note” in Section 24.4, $\cos z=\cos x\cosh y-i\sin x\sinh y$. Hence

$$ u(x,y)=\cos x\cosh y ,\qquad v(x,y)=-\sin x\sinh y $$

We compute the partial derivatives.

$$ u_x = -\sin x\cosh y ,\qquad u_y = \cos x\sinh y ,\qquad v_x = -\cos x\sinh y ,\qquad v_y = -\sin x\cosh y $$

Comparing them, $u_x=-\sin x\cosh y=v_y$ and $u_y=\cos x\sinh y=-(-\cos x\sinh y)=-v_x$, so the C-R equations hold identically for all $(x,y)$. Hence $\cos z$ is holomorphic on the whole plane. By Corollary 24.1, the derivative is

$$ f'(z) = u_x+iv_x = -\sin x\cosh y+i(-\cos x\sinh y) = -(\sin x\cosh y+i\cos x\sinh y) $$

Since, by the “Math Note” in Section 24.4, $\sin z=\sin x\cosh y+i\cos x\sinh y$,

$$ f'(z) = -\sin z $$

This agrees with $\dfrac{d}{dz}\cos z=-\sin z$, which we derived in Formula 24.3 (verified numerically with SymPy at six points with random complex numbers).

Exercise 24.4: Application of the quotient rule—$g(z)=\dfrac{z^2+1}{z-1}$

Solution

Let $f(z)=z^2+1$ and $h(z)=z-1$; then $g(z)=f(z)/h(z)$. Since $f'(z)=2z$ and $h'(z)=1$, Formula 24.2 (the quotient rule) gives

$$ g'(z) = \frac{f'(z)h(z)-f(z)h'(z)}{\{h(z)\}^2} = \frac{2z(z-1)-(z^2+1)\cdot1}{(z-1)^2} $$

Expanding and simplifying the numerator,

$$ 2z(z-1)-(z^2+1) = 2z^2-2z-z^2-1 = z^2-2z-1 $$

Therefore

$$ g'(z) = \frac{z^2-2z-1}{(z-1)^2} $$

The function $g(z)$ cannot be defined at the point where the denominator $z-1$ becomes $0$, that is, at $z=1$, so differentiation there is out of the question. Therefore the function is not holomorphic at $z=1$ (at every other point it is holomorphic, being a quotient of polynomials; verified with SymPy: direct differentiation and the formula give the same result).

Exercise 24.5: A concrete check that the limits from two directions agree—$f(z)=z^2$

Solution

From $f(z)=z^2=(x+iy)^2=(x^2-y^2)+i(2xy)$, we have $u=x^2-y^2$ and $v=2xy$.

[1] $\beta=0,\alpha\to0$ (real-axis direction): By the formula in Chapter 24,

$$ u_x+iv_x = 2x+i(2y) = 2x+2iy $$

[2] $\alpha=0,\beta\to0$ (imaginary-axis direction): By the formula in Chapter 24,

$$ -iu_y+v_y = -i(-2y)+2x = 2x+2iy $$

(We substituted $u_y=-2y$ and $v_y=2x$.) Both [1] and [2] agree with $2x+2iy=2(x+iy)=2z$, so they indeed take the same value. This is also consistent with the fact that the C-R equations $u_x=2x=v_y$ and $u_y=-2y=-v_x$ hold ($-v_x=-2y$ because $v_x=2y$)—recall that the very condition that [1] and [2] agree was precisely the C-R equations (Section 24.3.1). The result also agrees with $f'(z)=2z$ found in Example 24.5 and Exercise 24.1 (this is the $2z+3$ of the case $z^2+3z$ with the constant term $3$ removed) (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 24.6: The real part of a holomorphic function is a harmonic function

Solution

Suppose that $f=u+iv$ is holomorphic and that the C-R equations $u_x=v_y$ and $u_y=-v_x$ hold (wherever $u,v$ are twice continuously differentiable). Differentiating both sides of the first equation $u_x=v_y$ partially with respect to $x$ gives

$$ u_{xx} = v_{yx} $$

Differentiating both sides of the second equation $u_y=-v_x$ partially with respect to $y$ gives

$$ u_{yy} = -v_{xy} $$

Now we use Schwarz's theorem in Chapter 6, Section 6.3 (if $v$ is twice continuously differentiable, the result does not change when the order of partial differentiation is exchanged: $v_{xy}=v_{yx}$). The right-hand sides of the two equations are equal in absolute value and differ only in sign—since $v_{yx}=v_{xy}$, we have $u_{xx}=v_{yx}=v_{xy}=-u_{yy}$. Therefore

$$ u_{xx}+u_{yy} = v_{yx}+(-v_{xy}) = v_{yx}-v_{xy} = 0 $$

That is, the real part $u$ of a holomorphic function is a harmonic function ($u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$, a solution of Laplace's equation). (Similarly, one can show that the imaginary part $v$ is also a harmonic function.) As a concrete example, checking directly with the real part $u=e^x\cos y$ of $f(z)=e^z$, we obtain

$$ u_{xx} = e^x\cos y ,\qquad u_{yy} = -e^x\cos y \quad\Longrightarrow\quad u_{xx}+u_{yy} = e^x\cos y-e^x\cos y = 0 $$

so we confirm that it is indeed a harmonic function (verified with SymPy). This fact is used in the following way: the real and imaginary parts of holomorphic functions serve as tools for describing the potentials of physical phenomena governed by Laplace's equation, such as electrostatic fields and heat conduction—this is one of the important bridges connecting complex analysis with physics and engineering.

← Back to Chapter 24, “Holomorphic Functions and the Cauchy–Riemann Equations”

Chapter 25 Complex Integration and Cauchy's Integral Theorem

Exercise 25.1: Contour integral of the exponential function

Solution

Writing $f(z)=e^z$ in the form $u+iv$, we have $e^z=e^{x+iy}=e^x\cos y+ie^x\sin y$, so $u=e^x\cos y,\ v=e^x\sin y$. Checking the Cauchy–Riemann equations, we compute

$$\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}=e^x\cos y=\frac{\partial v}{\partial y},\qquad \frac{\partial u}{\partial y}=-e^x\sin y=-\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}$$

Both equations hold on the whole complex plane, and moreover these partial derivatives (such as $e^x\cos y$ and $e^x\sin y$) are continuous on the whole complex plane, being products of exponential and trigonometric functions. Therefore $f(z)=e^z$ is holomorphic on the whole complex plane, and its derivative $f'(z)=e^z$ is also continuous.

Let $C$ be any simple closed curve in the complex plane. Then $C$ and its interior $D$ are both contained in the complex plane, and $f$ is holomorphic there with a continuous derivative. Hence the hypotheses of Theorem 25.2 (Cauchy's integral theorem) are satisfied as they stand, so that

$$\oint_C e^z\,dz=0$$

holds. (Numerical-integration check with mpmath: computing over the unit circle gives $\approx-6\times10^{-38}-9\times10^{-31}i$, which is $0$ to within floating-point error.)

Exercise 25.2: Handling three poles with partial fractions

Solution

Set $\dfrac{z+2}{z(z-1)(z+1)}=\dfrac{A}z+\dfrac{B}{z-1}+\dfrac{C}{z+1}$ and multiply both sides by $z(z-1)(z+1)$ to get

$$z+2=A(z-1)(z+1)+Bz(z+1)+Cz(z-1)$$

Substituting $z=0$: $2=A(-1)(1)=-A$, so $A=-2$. Substituting $z=1$: $3=B(1)(2)=2B$, so $B=\dfrac32$. Substituting $z=-1$: $1=C(-1)(-2)=2C$, so $C=\dfrac12$. Hence

$$\frac{z+2}{z(z-1)(z+1)}=-\frac2z+\frac3{2(z-1)}+\frac1{2(z+1)}$$

The singularities $z=0,1,-1$ all lie inside $|z|=3$ (since $|0|=0,\ |1|=1,\ |-1|=1$ are all less than $3$). Applying Formula 25.3 (the contour integral formula) to each of the three poles, we find that the contour integral of each term is $2\pi i$ in every case, and

$$\oint_{|z|=3}\frac{z+2}{z(z-1)(z+1)}\,dz=-2\cdot(2\pi i)+\frac32\cdot(2\pi i)+\frac12\cdot(2\pi i)=2\pi i\Big(-2+\frac32+\frac12\Big)=2\pi i\cdot0=0$$

Even though all three poles are enclosed, the answer is $0$. This is because the sum $A+B+C$ of the coefficients of the partial fraction decomposition (this is the sum of the residues at the poles; a residue is the coefficient that appears when a contour integral is taken around a singularity—the coefficient of $\frac1{z-\alpha}$ in the contour integral formula, Formula 25.3, is exactly this—and residues will be studied properly in a later chapter) happened to be $0$. (A rational function whose numerator has degree at least two less than that of its denominator decays at a rate of about $1/z^2$ as $z\to\infty$, and the present case is in fact an instance of the general fact that, for such a function, the sum of the residues at the poles is always $0$.) Checking by numerical integration (mpmath), we find that the integral over $|z|=3$ is $\approx1.7\times10^{-38}$, which agrees with $0$ within the numerical error.

Exercise 25.3: Direct computation for a non-holomorphic function

Solution

On the unit circle we have $z=e^{i\theta}\ (0\le\theta\le2\pi)$, $\operatorname{Re}(z)=\cos\theta$, and $dz=ie^{i\theta}d\theta$. Hence

$$\oint_{|z|=1}\operatorname{Re}(z)\,dz=\int_0^{2\pi}\cos\theta\cdot ie^{i\theta}\,d\theta=i\int_0^{2\pi}\cos\theta(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)\,d\theta=i\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta-\int_0^{2\pi}\sin\theta\cos\theta\,d\theta$$

We have $\displaystyle\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^2\theta\,d\theta=\pi$ (using the half-angle formula $\cos^2\theta=\frac{1+\cos2\theta}2$, we get $\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{1+\cos2\theta}2d\theta=\pi+0=\pi$), and $\displaystyle\int_0^{2\pi}\sin\theta\cos\theta\,d\theta=\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}\sin2\theta\,d\theta=0$ (it is an integral over one period, so it is $0$). Therefore

$$\oint_{|z|=1}\operatorname{Re}(z)\,dz=i\pi-0=\pi i$$

Writing $f(z)=\operatorname{Re}(z)=x$ in the form $u+iv$, we have $u=x,\ v=0$. Checking the Cauchy–Riemann equations, we have $\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial x}=1$ and $\dfrac{\partial v}{\partial y}=0$, so $u_x=v_y$ (that is, $1=0$) fails to hold anywhere in the complex plane. Hence $f$ is nowhere holomorphic and does not satisfy the hypotheses of Cauchy's integral theorem (Theorem 25.2), so there is no contradiction with the theorem even though the integral is not $0$. (SymPy check: $\pi i$.)

Exercise 25.4: Finding an area with Green's theorem

Solution

Setting $f(x,y)=x$ in the second equation of Theorem 25.1 (Green's theorem), we have $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}=1$, so

$$\oint_C x\,dy=\iint_D 1\,dx\,dy=\text{(area of $D$)}$$

Also, setting $f(x,y)=y$ in the first equation, we have $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}=1$, so

$$\oint_C y\,dx=-\iint_D 1\,dx\,dy=-\text{(area of $D$)}$$

Therefore, subtracting the second equation from the first and dividing by two, we get

$$\frac12\Big(\oint_C x\,dy-\oint_C y\,dx\Big)=\frac12\Big(\text{area}-(-\text{area})\Big)=\text{area}$$

That is, the area of $D$ can be computed as $\displaystyle\frac12\oint_C(x\,dy-y\,dx)$. For the unit circle $C:x=\cos\theta,\ y=\sin\theta\ (0\le\theta\le2\pi)$, substituting $dx=-\sin\theta\,d\theta,\ dy=\cos\theta\,d\theta$, we get

$$\frac12\oint_C(x\,dy-y\,dx)=\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}\big[\cos\theta\cdot\cos\theta-\sin\theta\cdot(-\sin\theta)\big]d\theta=\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}(\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta)\,d\theta=\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}1\,d\theta=\frac12\cdot2\pi=\pi$$

This indeed agrees with the area $\pi$ of the unit disk. (SymPy check: $\pi$.)

Exercise 25.5: The branch point of the logarithm

Solution

Looking back at why the integral was $0$ in Formula 25.2 (the case $n\neq-1$), we see that the essential point was that the antiderivative $\dfrac{z^{n+1}}{n+1}$ of the integrand $z^n$ is a single-valued function (one whose value is uniquely determined for each $z$) on the whole complex plane except $z=0$. Even when $\theta$ changes from $0$ to $2\pi$ as we go once around the circle, $z=re^{i\cdot0}$ and $z=re^{i\cdot2\pi}$ are the same point, so the value of the antiderivative returns to exactly the same value, and as a result the definite integral (a difference of values of the antiderivative) is $0$.

But the function $\log z=\ln r+i\theta$ itself is a multivalued function: each time we go once around $z=0$ (increase $\theta$ by $2\pi$), its imaginary part keeps increasing by $2\pi$. Although $z=re^{i\cdot0}$ and $z=re^{i\cdot2\pi}$ are the same point in the complex plane, the values of $\log z$ are the different values $\ln r$ and $\ln r+2\pi i$. The point $z=0$ is the cause of this discrepancy, and it is called a branch point. Since $\log z$ is a function that “does not return to its original value after one loop” around $z=0$, the reasoning used for $z^n\ (n\neq-1)$, “go around once and everything is back as before, so the integral is $0$,” simply does not hold in the first place. The nonzero value $2\pi ri$ actually computed in Example 25.3 is exactly this “discrepancy after one loop” showing up in the form of an integral.

Exercise 25.6: Why we may shrink the path of integration

Solution

Consider two simple closed curves of different shapes, $C$ (the original path) and $C_r$ (a small circle centered at $\alpha$), each of which encloses just the one singularity $\alpha$ in its interior. Let $D$ be the domain obtained by removing the interior of $C_r$ from the interior of $C$. Then $D$ is a doubly connected domain (with one hole) having outer boundary $C$ and inner boundary $C_r$, and since the singularity $\alpha$ lies outside $D$ (inside $C_r$), the function $f$ is holomorphic on $D$ and its boundary.

Theorem 25.4 (application to multiply connected domains) showed the relation $\oint_C f\,dz=\oint_{C_1}f\,dz+\oint_{C_2}f\,dz$ for a triply connected domain with two holes, but the same proof (insert one bridging cut from the outer boundary to the boundary of a hole to make the domain simply connected, apply Cauchy's integral theorem, and drop the parts that cancel each other) can be used as it is when there is one hole; the equation becomes simpler by one bridging segment and one hole, and the proof gives the relation of Corollary 25.2:

$$\oint_C f(z)\,dz=\oint_{C_r}f(z)\,dz$$

That is, as long as the integrand $f$ is holomorphic throughout the domain between $C$ and $C_r$, the integrals over the two boundaries are exactly equal. In other words, however much and into whatever shape we deform $C$, as long as we do not jump over the singularity $\alpha$ (even if we finally shrink it all the way down to the small circle $C_r$), the value of the integral does not change. This is precisely why an integral such as $\oint_C\frac{dz}{z-\alpha}$ may be computed over a small circle centered at $\alpha$, which is easy to work with, instead of directly over the complicated curve $C$ (this is the argument actually used in the proof of Formula 25.3).

← Back to Chapter 25, “Complex Integration and Cauchy's Integral Theorem”

Chapter 26 Cauchy's Integral Formula

Exercise 26.1: Checking $\displaystyle\oint_{|z|=2}\frac{z+1}{z(z-1)}\,dz$ in two ways

Solution

(a) The method of partial fraction decomposition. Let $\dfrac{z+1}{z(z-1)}=\dfrac{A}{z}+\dfrac{B}{z-1}$. Putting the right-hand side over a common denominator, we get

$$ z+1=A(z-1)+Bz $$

(We cleared the denominators.) Substituting $z=0$ gives $0+1=A(0-1)+0$, so $1=-A$, that is, $A=-1$. Substituting $z=1$ gives $1+1=0+B\cdot1$, so $B=2$. Hence

$$ \frac{z+1}{z(z-1)}=-\frac1z+\frac{2}{z-1} $$

The interior of the circle $|z|=2$ contains both $z=0$ and $z=1$ ($|0|=0\lt2$, $|1|=1\lt2$). Using the contour integral formula (Section 25.5) $\oint_{|z|=2}\dfrac{1}{z-\alpha}dz=2\pi i$ (when $\alpha$ lies in the interior) twice, we obtain

$$ \oint_{|z|=2}\frac{z+1}{z(z-1)}\,dz=-1\cdot(2\pi i)+2\cdot(2\pi i)=2\pi i(-1+2)=2\pi i $$

(b) The method of Cauchy's integral formula. Near $z=0$, we view $\dfrac{z+1}{z(z-1)}=\dfrac{f_1(z)}{z-0}$ ($f_1(z):=\dfrac{z+1}{z-1}$, which is holomorphic near $z=0$). Since $f_1(0)=\dfrac{0+1}{0-1}=-1$, by Theorem 26.1 the contribution is $2\pi i\cdot(-1)=-2\pi i$. Near $z=1$, we view $\dfrac{z+1}{z(z-1)}=\dfrac{f_2(z)}{z-1}$ ($f_2(z):=\dfrac{z+1}{z}$). Since $f_2(1)=\dfrac{1+1}{1}=2$, the contribution is $2\pi i\cdot2=4\pi i$. Adding the two contributions gives

$$ \oint_{|z|=2}\frac{z+1}{z(z-1)}\,dz=-2\pi i+4\pi i=2\pi i $$

This agrees perfectly with the result $2\pi i$ of (a) (Python check: verify/univmath/ch26_cauchy_examples.py). Note also that the coefficients of the terms of the partial fraction decomposition ($A=-1,\ B=2$) coincide exactly with the values $f_1(0)=-1,\ f_2(1)=2$ computed by Cauchy's integral formula—this is no accident, because each coefficient of the partial fraction decomposition is nothing other than “the value obtained by evaluating at that singularity with Cauchy's integral formula.”

Exercise 26.2: The relation between Step 1 of the proof and the principle of path deformation

Solution

In Section 25.4, we rewrote the contour integral over a multiply connected domain with several holes (for example, a triply connected domain) as the sum of the integral over the outer boundary and the integrals over the small circles surrounding the individual holes. The key at that time was to draw one “bridging segment” connecting one hole to another, or a hole to the outer boundary, so that the whole could be regarded as a single simply connected domain, and then to apply Cauchy's integral theorem (if a function is holomorphic on a simply connected domain, its contour integral is $0$). The bridging segment is traversed twice, once going and once returning, in opposite directions, so the two integrals cancel exactly, and what remained was only the conclusion that “the integral along the outer boundary” equals “the sum of the integrals along the circles surrounding the inner holes.”

Step 1 of the proof of Theorem 26.1 (Figure 26.4) has exactly the same structure. Here the “hole” is the inside of the disk $|\zeta-z|\lt\rho$ of radius $\rho$ centered at the point $z$, and the integrand $\dfrac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}$ cannot be defined inside this hole (it is not holomorphic there), because its denominator becomes $0$ at $\zeta=z$. So we connect the outer boundary $\partial D$ and the circle $C_\rho$, which is the boundary of the hole, by a thin corridor (two bridging segments drawn very close together), as in Figure 26.4. Then the domain obtained from $D$ by removing the disk $|\zeta-z|\lt\rho$ can be regarded as a single simply connected domain if we cut it open along the corridor, and the integrand is holomorphic everywhere inside it. Therefore, by Cauchy's integral theorem, the integral over the entire path consisting of “one counterclockwise circuit of $\partial D$ + passage inward along the corridor + one clockwise circuit of $C_\rho$ (when the hole is viewed as part of the boundary of the simply connected domain, the direction is reversed) + passage outward along the corridor” is $0$. The inward and outward integrals along the corridor cancel, because they follow the same path in opposite directions, and what remains is

$$ \oint_{\partial D}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta-\oint_{C_\rho}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta=0 $$

(The sign of the $C_\rho$ term is negative because, viewed as part of the boundary of the simply connected domain, $C_\rho$ is traversed clockwise, that is, in the direction opposite to counterclockwise.) Rearranging this, we obtain exactly the equality used in Step 1 of the proof, $\displaystyle\oint_{\partial D}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta=\oint_{C_\rho}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta$. In other words, Step 1 of the proof is nothing other than an application of the argument for multiply connected domains in Section 25.4 to “the case with only one hole.”

Exercise 26.3: Behavior of the keyhole integral in the limit

Solution

We list once more the integrals along the four paths found in Section 26.2.5.

$$ \begin{aligned} \int_{C_1}\log z\,dz&=-2R\log R-i\pi R+2R\\ \int_{C_2}\log z\,dz&=R\log R-R-\varepsilon\log\varepsilon+\varepsilon+i\pi R-i\pi\varepsilon\\ \int_{C_3}\log z\,dz&=2\varepsilon\log\varepsilon+i\pi\varepsilon-2\varepsilon\\ \int_{C_4}\log z\,dz&=R\log R-R-\varepsilon\log\varepsilon+\varepsilon \end{aligned} $$

As $R\to\infty$, we have $R\log R\to\infty$ and $i\pi R\to\infty$ (its absolute value grows), so $C_1$ alone diverges, and $C_2$ alone also diverges (because of the $R\log R$ and $i\pi R$ terms). However, if we extract just the coefficient of $R\log R$, it is $-2$ for $C_1$, $+1$ for $C_2$, and $+1$ for $C_4$, and $-2+1+1=0$, so these cancel. Similarly, the coefficients of the terms in $R$ that do not involve a logarithm are $+2$ for $C_1$, $-1$ for $C_2$, and $-1$ for $C_4$, and $2-1-1=0$. The coefficients of the $i\pi R$ terms are $-1$ for $C_1$ and $+1$ for $C_2$, and $-1+1=0$. Therefore, in $C_1+C_2+C_4$, all the terms involving $R$ (including the divergent ones) cancel neatly, and no part depending on $R$ remains.

On the other hand, as $\varepsilon\to0$, we have $\varepsilon\log\varepsilon\to0$ (this can be shown by writing $\varepsilon\log\varepsilon=\dfrac{\log\varepsilon}{1/\varepsilon}$ and using l'Hôpital's rule, or by setting $t=1/\varepsilon\to\infty$ and noting that $-\log t/t\to0$), $\varepsilon\to0$, and $i\pi\varepsilon\to0$, so all the terms containing $\varepsilon$ tend to $0$. In particular, the integral $2\varepsilon\log\varepsilon+i\pi\varepsilon-2\varepsilon$ over $C_3$ (the small arc) converges as a whole to $0$ as $\varepsilon\to0$—as we shrink the small arc more and more, its contribution to the integral disappears.

In conclusion, in the limit $R\to\infty,\ \varepsilon\to0$, the divergent terms contained in $C_1,C_2,C_4$ (the terms in $R\log R$, $R$, and $i\pi R$) cancel exactly even before the limit is taken, the terms containing $\varepsilon$ tend to $0$ in the limit, and $C_3$ also vanishes entirely, so the total remains exactly $0$ at every stage (both before and after taking the limit). This is merely a restatement, from the viewpoint of the limit, of the fact seen in Section 26.2.5 that “the total is exactly $0$ for any $0\lt\varepsilon\lt R$.”

Exercise 26.4: Cauchy's integral formula cannot be used for a function that is not holomorphic, namely $\bar z$

Solution

Setting $z=e^{i\theta}$ ($0\le\theta\le2\pi$), we have $\bar z=\overline{e^{i\theta}}=e^{-i\theta}$ (Chapter 21: $\overline{e^{i\theta}}=e^{-i\theta}$) and $dz=ie^{i\theta}d\theta$. Therefore

$$ \oint_{|z|=1}\bar z\,dz=\int_0^{2\pi}e^{-i\theta}\cdot ie^{i\theta}\,d\theta=\int_0^{2\pi}i\,d\theta=i\Big[\theta\Big]_0^{2\pi}=2\pi i $$

(Since $e^{-i\theta}\cdot e^{i\theta}=e^{-i\theta+i\theta}=e^0=1$, the integrand is the constant $i$.)

Next, we confirm that $f(z)=\bar z$ is not holomorphic. Setting $z=x+iy$, we have $f(z)=\bar z=x-iy$, with real part $u(x,y)=x$ and imaginary part $v(x,y)=-y$. The Cauchy–Riemann equations (Chapter 24) require that $\pdiff{u}{x}=\pdiff{v}{y}$ and $\pdiff{u}{y}=-\pdiff{v}{x}$, but here $\pdiff{u}{x}=1$ and $\pdiff{v}{y}=-1$, and since $1\ne-1$, the first equation fails at every point. Therefore $f(z)=\bar z$ is not holomorphic at any point of the complex plane, and neither Cauchy's integral theorem of the previous chapter nor Cauchy's integral formula of this chapter can be applied, because the first assumption (that $f$ be holomorphic) is not satisfied.

Nevertheless, it is not a coincidence that the value of the integral came out to be exactly $2\pi i$. In general, let $D$ be the region enclosed by a simple closed curve $C$. Then $\bar z\,dz=(x-iy)(dx+i\,dy)=(x\,dx+y\,dy)+i(x\,dy-y\,dx)$, and applying Green's theorem (Section 26.1) to the real and imaginary parts separately, it is known that the integral of the real part, being of the form of an exact differential ($x\,dx+y\,dy=\dfrac12d(x^2+y^2)$), is $0$ along a closed curve, while the integral of the imaginary part is $\displaystyle\int_C(x\,dy-y\,dx)=2\times(\text{area of $D$})$. Therefore the formula $\displaystyle\oint_C\bar z\,dz=2i\times(\text{area})$ holds—a formula unrelated to holomorphy (it comes from Green's theorem alone). Since the area of the unit disk is $\pi$, we get $\oint_{|z|=1}\bar z\,dz=2i\pi=2\pi i$, which agrees exactly with the result of the direct computation. Note that the fact that “the numerical value of the answer looks the same as the answer given by Cauchy's integral formula ($2\pi i\cdot1$)” arises for a completely different reason (area, not holomorphy).

Exercise 26.5: Verification of Green's theorem ($f=x^2-y^2$, upper half-disk)

Solution

The arc part: Setting $x=r\cos\theta,\,y=r\sin\theta$ ($\theta:0\to\pi$), by the double-angle formula $\cos2\theta=\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta$ (high school mathematics) we have $f=x^2-y^2=r^2(\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta)=r^2\cos2\theta$. Since $dx=-r\sin\theta\,d\theta$,

$$ \int_{C_{\text{arc}}}f\,dx=\int_0^\pi r^2\cos2\theta\cdot(-r\sin\theta)\,d\theta=-r^3\int_0^\pi\sin\theta\cos2\theta\,d\theta $$

Using the product-to-sum formula $\sin A\cos B=\dfrac12\big(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)\big)$ (high school mathematics, with $A=\theta,\,B=2\theta$), we have $\sin\theta\cos2\theta=\dfrac12(\sin3\theta+\sin(-\theta))=\dfrac12(\sin3\theta-\sin\theta)$, so

$$ \int_0^\pi\sin\theta\cos2\theta\,d\theta=\frac12\int_0^\pi\sin3\theta\,d\theta-\frac12\int_0^\pi\sin\theta\,d\theta =\frac12\left[-\frac{\cos3\theta}{3}\right]_0^\pi-\frac12\Big[-\cos\theta\Big]_0^\pi $$

We have $\left[-\dfrac{\cos3\theta}{3}\right]_0^\pi=-\dfrac{\cos3\pi}{3}-\left(-\dfrac{\cos0}{3}\right)=-\dfrac{-1}{3}+\dfrac13=\dfrac13+\dfrac13=\dfrac23$ ($\cos3\pi=\cos\pi=-1$). Also, $\Big[-\cos\theta\Big]_0^\pi=-(-1)-(-1)=2$. Hence

$$ \int_0^\pi\sin\theta\cos2\theta\,d\theta=\frac12\cdot\frac23-\frac12\cdot2=\frac13-1=-\frac23 $$

Therefore $\displaystyle\int_{C_{\text{arc}}}f\,dx=-r^3\times\left(-\frac23\right)=\frac23r^3$. For the diameter part, on $y=0$ we have $f(x,0)=x^2-0^2=x^2$, which is exactly the same as the computation in Section 26.1.2, so $\displaystyle\int_{C_{\text{diam}}}f\,dx=\int_{-r}^r x^2\,dx=\dfrac23r^3$. Adding these up, the line integral over the boundary is

$$ \int_C f\,dx=\frac23r^3+\frac23r^3=\frac43r^3 $$

Next, the double-integral side. We have $\pdiff{f}{y}=\pdiff{}{y}(x^2-y^2)=-2y$. Using polar coordinates $x=r'\cos\theta,\,y=r'\sin\theta$ and $dx\,dy=r'\,dr'\,d\theta$, we get

$$ \iint_D\pdiff{f}{y}\,dx\,dy=\int_0^\pi\int_0^r(-2y)\cdot r'\,dr'\,d\theta=\int_0^\pi\int_0^r(-2r'\sin\theta)\cdot r'\,dr'\,d\theta =-2\left(\int_0^r r'^2\,dr'\right)\left(\int_0^\pi\sin\theta\,d\theta\right) $$ $$ =-2\cdot\frac{r^3}{3}\cdot2=-\frac43r^3 $$

Therefore $\displaystyle-\iint_D\pdiff{f}{y}\,dx\,dy=\frac43r^3$, which agrees with the left-hand side $\dfrac43r^3$ (Python check: verify/univmath/ch26_green_theorem.py). Note that, compared with the result for $f=x^2+y^2$ in Section 26.1.2 ($-\dfrac43r^3$), the sign is reversed and it becomes $+\dfrac43r^3$. This is because, although, as before, $f=x^2-y^2$ is unchanged under $y\to-y$ ($f\to f$; an even function), the sign of $y^2$ is reversed, so that $\pdiff{f}{y}=-2y$ (reversed from the $+2y$ of the case $x^2+y^2$), and this reversal is carried over directly to the sign of the double integral.

Exercise 26.6: The meaning of the mean value property

Solution

In Cauchy's integral formula $\displaystyle f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial D}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta$, we take the boundary $\partial D$ to be the circle itself, with center $z$ and radius $\rho$. A point on the circle can be parametrized as $\zeta=z+\rho e^{i\theta}$ ($\theta$ runs from $0$ to $2\pi$). Then $\zeta-z=\rho e^{i\theta}$ and $d\zeta=i\rho e^{i\theta}\,d\theta$ (we differentiated $\zeta$ with respect to $\theta$). Substituting these, we obtain

$$ f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{f(z+\rho e^{i\theta})}{\rho e^{i\theta}}\cdot i\rho e^{i\theta}\,d\theta $$

The $\rho e^{i\theta}$ in the denominator and the $\rho e^{i\theta}$ in the numerator cancel exactly, leaving only $i$, so

$$ f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_0^{2\pi}f(z+\rho e^{i\theta})\cdot i\,d\theta=\frac{i}{2\pi i}\int_0^{2\pi}f(z+\rho e^{i\theta})\,d\theta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}f(z+\rho e^{i\theta})\,d\theta $$

(using $i/i=1$). This is the mean value property. Put in terms that a high-school student can follow, it says: “If we keep measuring the values of $f$ while going once around the circle of radius $\rho$ centered at $z$, and average the measured values over the whole circuit, the average coincides exactly with the value $f(z)$ at the center point $z$.” For an ordinary (non-complex) function, there is no guarantee whatsoever that averaging the values here and there on a circle gives the value at the center—for example, if we average $f(x,y)=x$ (a function on the plane) over a circle centered at the origin, then by symmetry we indeed get $0=f(0,0)$, but that is only because this function happens to have a symmetry like that of an odd function. For holomorphic functions, on the other hand, this mean value property always holds, for any holomorphic function and for a circle of any radius. This expresses the fact that a holomorphic function is a special kind of function that is “very strongly bound by the information around it”—one cannot change its value at a single point arbitrarily.

Chapter 27 Orthogonality of Trigonometric Functions and Inner Products of Functions

Exercise 27.1: Computing an integral using the orthogonality formulas

Solution

Using the linearity of the integral (the integral of a sum is the sum of the integrals, and constant multiples can be taken outside), we expand the integrand.

$$ \int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\left(5\sin3x-2\cos7x+1\right)\sin3x\,dx =5\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sin3x\sin3x\,dx-2\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos7x\sin3x\,dx+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sin3x\,dx $$

The first term has the form $\sin mx\sin nx$ with $m=n=3$ (both are positive integers, and they are equal), so $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sin3x\sin3x\,dx=\pi$ (the case $m=n$ of Formula 27.5). The second term has the form $\cos mx\sin nx=\sin nx\cos mx$ (with $m=7,n=3$), so by Formula 27.3, $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos7x\sin3x\,dx=0$. The third term is the integral of a lone $\sin mx$ with $m=3\,(\neq0)$, so by Formula 27.1, $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sin3x\,dx=0$. Therefore,

$$ 5\cdot\pi-2\cdot0+0=5\pi $$

This is the answer (we verified, by expanding and integrating directly with SymPy, that the result agrees with $5\pi$: verify/univmath/ch27_examples.py).

Exercise 27.2: Does orthogonality also hold on the interval $[0,2\pi]$?

Solution

Carrying out the computation, we obtain $\displaystyle\int_0^{2\pi}\cos2x\cos3x\,dx=0$ (verified with SymPy; for reference, on the same interval $\displaystyle\int_0^{2\pi}\cos^22x\,dx=\pi$, which also agrees with the value on $[-\pi,\pi]$).

The reason it holds in general can be explained as follows. The function $\cos mx\cos nx$ ($m,n$ integers) can be written as products and sums of $\cos x,\cos2x,\dots$, and since $\sin x,\cos x$ both have period $2\pi$, $\cos mx\cos nx$ is itself a periodic function with period $2\pi$ (if $m,n$ are integers, then $\cos m(x+2\pi)=\cos(mx+2\pi m)=\cos mx$ and $\cos n(x+2\pi)=\cos nx$, so the product $\cos mx\cos nx$ is also invariant under $x\to x+2\pi$). In general, when a periodic function $h(x)$ with period $T$ is integrated over an interval of “length exactly one period ($T$),” the value does not depend on the position of the interval (where we start measuring) and is always the same—this is easy to accept if you picture the graph, since even if we shift the interval to the right by the period $T$, the shape of the waveform itself does not change at all, and the total of the “areas of the peaks and valleys” over one period does not change (rigorously, differentiating $\displaystyle\int_a^{a+T}h(x)\,dx$ with respect to $a$ gives $h(a+T)-h(a)=0$, which shows that the value of this integral is a constant independent of $a$). Both $[-\pi,\pi]$ and $[0,2\pi]$ are intervals of length $2\pi$ (exactly the same length as the period $2\pi$), so by this general fact the integral of $\cos mx\cos nx$ has the same value whether we take the interval to be $[-\pi,\pi]$ or $[0,2\pi]$. Therefore, as long as the interval of integration is an “interval of length $2\pi$,” the orthogonality formulas of this chapter (Formulas 27.1–27.5) hold wherever we take it, not only on $[-\pi,\pi]$.

Exercise 27.3: Inner products and norms of functions other than trigonometric functions

Solution

By the definition of the inner product (Definition 27.1),

$$ \langle f,g\rangle=\int_0^1e^x\cdot1\,dx=\Big[e^x\Big]_0^1=e-1\approx1.718 $$

(We used the integration formula for the exponential function from Chapter 5, $\int e^x\,dx=e^x+C$.) By the definition of the norm (Definition 27.2),

$$ \|f\|=\sqrt{\int_0^1(e^x)^2\,dx}=\sqrt{\int_0^1e^{2x}\,dx}=\sqrt{\left[\frac12e^{2x}\right]_0^1}=\sqrt{\frac{e^2-1}{2}}\approx1.787,\qquad \|g\|=\sqrt{\int_0^11^2\,dx}=\sqrt{1}=1 $$

Therefore $\|f\|\|g\|=\sqrt{\dfrac{e^2-1}{2}}\approx1.787$. Comparing this with $|\langle f,g\rangle|=e-1\approx1.718$, we have $1.718\le1.787$, so Schwarz's inequality (Theorem 27.1) $|\langle f,g\rangle|\le\|f\|\|g\|$ does indeed hold (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 27.4: Finding the norm from the coefficients of an orthogonal basis

Solution

The indices of $\cos x,\cos3x,\cos5x$ are all distinct positive integers, so by Formula 27.4 ($m\neq n$) they are mutually orthogonal, and by Formula 27.4 ($m=n$) we have $\|\cos x\|=\|\cos3x\|=\|\cos5x\|=\sqrt\pi$. When we expand the square of the norm according to the definition, all the cross terms (products of functions with different indices) integrate to $0$ by Formula 27.4 ($m\neq n$), and only the squared terms remain.

$$ \|y\|^2=\langle y,y\rangle=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(2\cos x+\cos3x-\cos5x)^2\,dx=2^2\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos^2x\,dx+1^2\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos^23x\,dx+(-1)^2\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos^25x\,dx $$ $$ =2^2\cdot\pi+1^2\cdot\pi+(-1)^2\cdot\pi=(4+1+1)\pi=6\pi $$

Hence $\|y\|=\sqrt{6\pi}$ (we verified with SymPy, by expanding and integrating $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(2\cos x+\cos3x-\cos5x)^2\,dx$ directly, that it agrees with $6\pi$). Using the general formula $\|y\|^2=\pi\sum_kc_k^2$ from Example 27.3 ($c_k$ being the coefficients $2,1,-1$) also immediately gives the same result, $\pi(2^2+1^2+(-1)^2)=6\pi$.

Exercise 27.5: An example where piecewise continuity fails

Solution

We examine the one-sided limits of the function in the problem at $x=0$.

$$ \lim_{x\to0+0}\frac1x=+\infty,\qquad\lim_{x\to0-0}\frac1x=-\infty $$

(As $x$ approaches $0$ from positive values, $1/x$ becomes arbitrarily large, and when the approach is from negative values, it becomes arbitrarily small, that is, arbitrarily large in the negative direction. Verified with SymPy.) Neither one-sided limit exists as a finite value (they diverge to $\pm\infty$).

Checking Definition 27.3 once more, condition (ii) requires that “at each point of discontinuity, the left-hand limit and the right-hand limit both exist (as finite values).” At $x=0$, neither the left-hand limit nor the right-hand limit exists as a finite value (they diverge), so this condition is not satisfied. Therefore $f(x)=1/x\,(x\neq0),\ f(0)=0$ is not piecewise continuous on the interval $[-1,1]$. Whereas the functions up through Exercise 27.4 were all piecewise continuous (being trigonometric functions, exponential functions, or polynomials), a function of the type $1/x$ is a typical example of one that falls outside the framework of piecewise continuity, because its denominator becomes $0$ at the origin and the function diverges.

Exercise 27.6: Numerical check of the triangle inequality

Solution

By Example 27.2, $\|f\|=\dfrac{\sqrt6}{3}\approx0.8165$ and $\|g\|=\dfrac{\sqrt{10}}{5}\approx0.6325$. First we compute $\|f\|+\|g\|$:

$$ \|f\|+\|g\|=\frac{\sqrt6}{3}+\frac{\sqrt{10}}{5}\approx0.8165+0.6325=1.4490 $$

Next we compute $\|f+g\|$ directly according to Definition 27.2. Since $(f+g)(x)=x+x^2$,

$$ \|f+g\|^2=\int_{-1}^1(x+x^2)^2\,dx=\int_{-1}^1\left(x^2+2x^3+x^4\right)dx $$

Since $2x^3$ is an odd function, its integral over the symmetric interval $[-1,1]$ is $0$ (for the same reason as in the alternative solution to Formula 27.3). The remaining two terms are

$$ \int_{-1}^1x^2\,dx=\left[\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_{-1}^1=\frac13-\left(-\frac13\right)=\frac23,\qquad \int_{-1}^1x^4\,dx=\left[\frac{x^5}{5}\right]_{-1}^1=\frac15-\left(-\frac15\right)=\frac25 $$

Therefore $\|f+g\|^2=\dfrac23+\dfrac25=\dfrac{10}{15}+\dfrac6{15}=\dfrac{16}{15}$, and hence

$$ \|f+g\|=\sqrt{\frac{16}{15}}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{15}}=\frac{4\sqrt{15}}{15}\approx1.0328 $$

(We verified with SymPy, by direct integration, that the values agree.) Comparing $\|f+g\|\approx1.0328$ with $\|f\|+\|g\|\approx1.4490$, we have $1.0328\le1.4490$, so Theorem 27.2 (the triangle inequality) $\|f+g\|\le\|f\|+\|g\|$ does indeed hold. Note that equality ($\|f+g\|=\|f\|+\|g\|$) holds only when $f,g$ point in the same “direction” (one is a constant multiple of the other, the constant being $0$ or greater)—in this case $f(x)=x$ and $g(x)=x^2$ are not proportional, so the inequality holds as a genuinely strict inequality ($\lt$).

← Back to Chapter 27, “Orthogonality of Trigonometric Functions and Inner Products of Functions”

Chapter 28 Definition of Fourier Series and Worked Examples

Exercise 28.1: Computing a complex Fourier series

Solution

We find the complex Fourier series of the function obtained by extending $f(x)=e^x\ (-\pi \lt x\le\pi)$ with period 2π. By Theorem 28.4 ($L=\pi$),

$$ C_k=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^x\,e^{-ikx}\,dx=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{(1-ik)x}\,dx $$

(we combined the exponentials using the law of exponents: $e^x\cdot e^{-ikx}=e^{x-ikx}=e^{(1-ik)x}$). Since $1-ik\ne0$ ($k$ is an integer and $i$ is the imaginary unit, so $1-ik=0$ holds only when $k=-i$, which cannot happen for a real integer $k$), the antiderivative formula for the exponential function, $\int e^{cx}dx=\frac1ce^{cx}$ ($c=1-ik$ is a complex constant), can be used as it stands, and we obtain

$$ C_k=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\frac{1}{1-ik}e^{(1-ik)x}\right]_{-\pi}^{\pi} =\frac{1}{2\pi(1-ik)}\Bigl(e^{(1-ik)\pi}-e^{-(1-ik)\pi}\Bigr) $$

Splitting the exponents into real and imaginary parts gives $e^{(1-ik)\pi}=e^{\pi}e^{-ik\pi}$ and $e^{-(1-ik)\pi}=e^{-\pi}e^{ik\pi}$. Here $k$ is an integer, so by Euler's formula $e^{\pm ik\pi}=\cos k\pi\pm i\sin k\pi=(-1)^k\pm i\cdot0=(-1)^k$ (since $\sin k\pi=0$, the value is the same for either sign). Therefore

$$ e^{(1-ik)\pi}-e^{-(1-ik)\pi}=(-1)^k e^{\pi}-(-1)^k e^{-\pi}=(-1)^k\bigl(e^{\pi}-e^{-\pi}\bigr)=2(-1)^k\sinh\pi $$

(we used the hyperbolic sine, $\sinh\pi=\frac{e^\pi-e^{-\pi}}2$). Hence

$$ C_k=\frac{2(-1)^k\sinh\pi}{2\pi(1-ik)}=\frac{(-1)^k\sinh\pi}{\pi(1-ik)} $$

Finally, as the hint directs, we make the denominator real. Multiplying the numerator and the denominator by $1+ik$ (the complex conjugate of $1-ik$) and using $(1-ik)(1+ik)=1-(ik)^2=1-i^2k^2=1+k^2$, we obtain

$$ C_k=\frac{(-1)^k\sinh\pi\,(1+ik)}{\pi(1-ik)(1+ik)}=\frac{(-1)^k(1+ik)\sinh\pi}{\pi(1+k^2)}\qquad(k\in\Z) $$

This expression can be used as it stands, because even when we substitute $k=0$ the denominator does not become $0$ ($1+0^2=1$). Indeed, substituting $k=0$ directly gives $C_0=\dfrac{\sinh\pi}{\pi}$, and this agrees exactly with the value $C_0=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^\pi e^x\,dx=\frac1{2\pi}(e^\pi-e^{-\pi})=\frac{\sinh\pi}{\pi}$ obtained by substituting $k=0$ directly into the defining formula. Therefore, regardless of the sign of $k$, we have the single formula

$$ f(x)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^k(1+ik)\sinh\pi}{\pi(1+k^2)}\,e^{ikx} $$

This is the answer (Python check: verify/univmath/ch28_explore4.py, ch28_explore5.py—we confirmed that $C_k$ obtained by direct integration with SymPy and the expression computed by hand above agree for all of $k=\pm1,\pm2,\pm3$. Numerically as well, for $k=1$ both agree with $C_1\approx-1.838-1.838i$).

Exercise 28.2: Fourier cosine series of period 2L

Solution

We find the Fourier cosine series of the function obtained by extending $f(x)=|x|\ (-3 \lt x\le3)$ with period 6 ($L=3$). Since $f(-x)=|-x|=|x|=f(x)$, $f$ is an even function, so by Theorem 28.3(I) we have $b_k=0$ from the outset, and only $\cos$ terms appear.

Computation of $a_0$. By Theorem 28.2,

$$ a_0=\frac1{2L}\int_{-L}^{L}f(x)\,dx=\frac16\int_{-3}^{3}|x|\,dx $$

Since the integrand $|x|$ is an even function, we can write $\int_{-3}^3=2\int_0^3$ (we double the area under a graph that is symmetric about the vertical axis), and since $|x|=x$ for $x\ge0$,

$$ a_0=\frac16\cdot2\int_0^{3}x\,dx=\frac13\left[\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^{3}=\frac13\cdot\frac92=\frac32 $$

Computation of $a_k\ (k\ge1)$. Similarly, the integrand $|x|\cos\frac{k\pi}{L}x$ is also an even function (even × even = even), so we can write $\int_{-3}^3=2\int_0^3$.

$$ a_k=\frac1L\int_{-L}^{L}f(x)\cos\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=\frac13\cdot2\int_0^{3}x\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x\,dx=\frac23\int_0^{3}x\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x\,dx $$

With $a=\dfrac{k\pi}{3}$, we use the general indefinite integral formula $\int x\cos ax\,dx=\frac1ax\sin ax+\frac1{a^2}\cos ax$, which was verified in the “Math Note” in Section 28.4.

$$ \int_0^{3}x\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x\,dx=\left[\frac{3}{k\pi}x\sin\frac{k\pi}{3}x+\frac{9}{k^2\pi^2}\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x\right]_0^{3} $$

(we substituted $\frac1a=\frac3{k\pi}$ and $\frac1{a^2}=\frac9{k^2\pi^2}$). Substituting $x=3$ and using $\frac{k\pi}{3}\cdot3=k\pi$, we have $\sin k\pi=0$ and $\cos k\pi=(-1)^k$ ($k$ is an integer), so the first term vanishes and only the second term remains. At $x=0$ as well, the first term is $0$ because $x=0$, and in the second term $\cos0=1$. Therefore

$$ \int_0^{3}x\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x\,dx=\left(0+\frac{9}{k^2\pi^2}(-1)^k\right)-\left(0+\frac{9}{k^2\pi^2}\right)=\frac{9}{k^2\pi^2}\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr) $$

Hence

$$ a_k=\frac23\cdot\frac{9}{k^2\pi^2}\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)=\frac{6\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{\pi^2k^2} $$

When $k$ is even, $(-1)^k-1=0$, so $a_k=0$. When $k$ is odd, $(-1)^k-1=-2$, so $a_k=\dfrac{-12}{\pi^2k^2}$. Thus the Fourier cosine series is

$$ f(x)=\frac32+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{6\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{\pi^2k^2}\cos\frac{k\pi}{3}x =\frac32-\frac{12}{\pi^2}\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{3}x+\frac19\cos\pi x+\frac1{25}\cos\frac{5\pi}{3}x+\cdots\right) $$

(Python check: verify/univmath/ch28_explore4.py, ch28_explore5.py—we confirmed $a_0=3/2$ and $a_k=6\{(-1)^k-1\}/(\pi^2k^2)$ for $k=1,\ldots,4$. We can also verify that this formula is, in the form $a_0=L/2$ and $a_k=\frac{2L}{\pi^2}\cdot\frac{(-1)^k-1}{k^2}$, the generalization ($L=\pi\to L=3$) of the result $a_k=2\{(-1)^k-1\}/(\pi k^2)$ for the “triangle wave $|x|$ of period 2π” seen in Example 28.3 and Example 28.6: substituting $L=\pi$ gives $a_0=\pi/2$ and $a_k=2\{(-1)^k-1\}/(\pi k^2)$, which returns exactly to the result of Example 28.6.)

Exercise 28.3: Proof that only cosine terms appear in the Fourier series of an even function

Solution

Let $f(x)$ be an even function of period $2L$ ($f(-x)=f(x)$ for all $x$). What we want to show is the following:

$$ b_k=\frac1L\int_{-L}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=0\qquad(\text{for all}\ k=1,2,\ldots) $$

We split the interval of integration $[-L,L]$ into the two intervals $[-L,0]$ and $[0,L]$ (splitting of the interval of integration).

$$ \int_{-L}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=\int_{-L}^{0}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx+\int_{0}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx $$

In the first term on the right-hand side, we make the substitution $x=-u$ ($dx=-du$). When $x=-L$ we have $u=L$, and when $x=0$ we have $u=0$, so the interval of integration becomes $u:L\to0$.

$$ \int_{-L}^{0}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=\int_{u=L}^{u=0}f(-u)\sin\!\left(-\frac{k\pi}{L}u\right)(-du) $$

Now, since $\sin(-\theta)=-\sin\theta$ (the sine function is odd), we have $\sin\!\left(-\frac{k\pi}{L}u\right)=-\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}u$. This minus sign and the minus sign of the $(-du)$ in the preceding integral multiply together and cancel exactly, so

$$ \int_{u=L}^{u=0}f(-u)\left(-\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}u\right)(-du)=\int_{u=L}^{u=0}f(-u)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}u\,du=-\int_{0}^{L}f(-u)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}u\,du $$

(the last equality uses the property of definite integrals that interchanging the upper and lower limits of integration reverses the sign, $\int_L^0=-\int_0^L$). Now, since $f$ is an even function, $f(-u)=f(u)$ holds for all $u$. Substituting this, we get

$$ \int_{-L}^{0}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=-\int_{0}^{L}f(u)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}u\,du $$

The symbol $u$ for the variable of integration can be written as anything without changing the value, as long as the values at the endpoints of the interval are correct (a definite integral does not depend on the name of the variable), so we may rewrite $u$ as $x$. Therefore

$$ \int_{-L}^{0}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=-\int_{0}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx $$

Substituting this back into the original splitting formula,

$$ \int_{-L}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=-\int_{0}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx+\int_{0}^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx=0 $$

Here the first and second terms on the right-hand side are exactly the same integral (differing only in sign), so they cancel exactly and the result is precisely $0$. Hence $b_k=\frac1L\cdot0=0$ holds for all $k=1,2,\ldots$. Therefore, the Fourier series of the even function $f(x)$ (Theorem 28.2) contains no $\sin$ terms at all and consists only of $\cos$ terms (and the constant term $a_0$); that is, it is the Fourier cosine series $f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_k\cos\frac{k\pi}{L}x$ (Theorem 28.3(I)), and this has now been shown directly at the level of the integral computation.

(Note that the same argument cannot be used for $a_k$. When $f$ is even, $f(x)\cos\frac{k\pi}{L}x$ is “even × even = even,” so even if we make the same substitution as above, what comes out is $+\int_0^L(\cdots)$ rather than $-\int_0^L(\cdots)$, and the nonvanishing value $2\int_0^L f(x)\cos\frac{k\pi}{L}x\,dx$ remains—this is the reason why $a_k$ does not become $0$ in general. For the case of an odd function ($f(-x)=-f(x)$), if we make a similar substitution, then conversely we get $a_k=0$ and $b_k\ne0$; you can verify this yourself by following the same computation.)

Exercise 28.4: Numerical experiment on the Fourier series of the square wave (Gibbs phenomenon)

Solution

We compute with Python (numpy) the partial sums $S_N(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1-(-1)^k}{2k}\sin kx$ of the Fourier sine series of the square wave $f(x)=-\pi/4\ (-\pi \lt x\le0),\ \pi/4\ (0 \lt x\le\pi)$ of Example 28.5 (verify/univmath/ch28_numeric.py).

(a) Convergence at the point of continuity $x=\pi/2$. Compared with $f(\pi/2)=\pi/4=0.785398\ldots$ (clear from the graph in Figure 28.4), the numerical results were as follows.

$N$5201001000
$S_N(\pi/2)$0.8666670.7604600.7803990.784898

As $N$ increases, $S_N(\pi/2)$ approaches the target value $\pi/4\approx0.785398$ monotonically, which confirms numerically that at $x=\pi/2$ (a point where $f$ is continuous) the Fourier series indeed converges to $f(\pi/2)$.

(b) Overshoot near the point of discontinuity $x=0$ (Gibbs phenomenon). Examining the maximum value of $S_N(x)$ over the range $x\in(0,\pi)$ and the position where it occurs, we find

$N$10502001000
Position of the maximum, $x$0.31410.06280.01570.0031
Maximum value $S_N$0.92860.92610.92600.9260
Ratio to the target value $\pi/4$1.18231.17911.17901.1790

As this table shows, when $N$ is increased the position of the maximum approaches $x=0$ more and more closely (it tends to $0$ in inverse proportion to $N$), but the maximum value itself does not converge to $\pi/4$; it stays at about $1.179$ times $\pi/4$ ($\approx0.9260$). This “overshoot,” which does not disappear even as $N\to\infty$, is the Gibbs phenomenon.

The theoretical value of this ratio is known to be given by the quantity called the Gibbs constant, $\dfrac2\pi\operatorname{Si}(\pi)\approx1.178979744$ ($\operatorname{Si}$ is the sine integral function $\operatorname{Si}(x)=\int_0^x\frac{\sin t}{t}dt$). We also confirmed that the value $1.178979744\ldots$ computed with Python's scipy.special.sici and the ratio obtained in the numerical experiment (for $N=1000$, computed to more digits than the table shows, $1.178980$) agree very well. Measuring the size of the overshoot in units of the size of the jump (the jump of $f$, $\pi/4-(-\pi/4)=\pi/2$), we get $(0.9260-0.7854)/(\pi/2)\approx0.090$, that is, about 9% of the jump, which also agrees with the known value for the Gibbs phenomenon.

Exercise 28.5: Working backward from complex coefficients to real coefficients (checked with the sawtooth wave)

Solution

The complex Fourier coefficients of the sawtooth wave $f(x)=x/2\ (-4 \lt x \lt4)$ (period 8) found in Example 28.7 were $C_k=\dfrac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi}\ (k\ne0)$. Using the formulas $a_k=C_k+C_{-k}$, $b_k=i(C_k-C_{-k})$ from the “Math Note” box (Section 28.3), we recompute the real Fourier coefficients.

First we find $C_{-k}$. Substituting $k\to-k$ into the expression for $C_k$, we get

$$ C_{-k}=\frac{2i(-1)^{-k}}{(-k)\pi}=-\frac{2i(-1)^{-k}}{k\pi} $$

Here, noting that $(-1)^{-k}=\dfrac{1}{(-1)^k}=(-1)^k$ (since $(-1)^k$ is one of the two values $\pm1$, its reciprocal equals the original value itself), we obtain

$$ C_{-k}=-\frac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi} $$

Computation of $a_k$.

$$ a_k=C_k+C_{-k}=\frac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi}+\left(-\frac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi}\right)=0 $$

This agrees exactly with $a_k=0$, which we knew from the start from Theorem 28.3(II) because $f(x)=x/2$ is an odd function (at the beginning of the solution of Example 28.7).

Computation of $b_k$.

$$ b_k=i\bigl(C_k-C_{-k}\bigr)=i\left(\frac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi}-\left(-\frac{2i(-1)^k}{k\pi}\right)\right)=i\cdot\frac{4i(-1)^k}{k\pi}=\frac{4i^2(-1)^k}{k\pi} $$

Using $i^2=-1$,

$$ b_k=\frac{-4(-1)^k}{k\pi}=\frac{4(-1)^{k+1}}{k\pi} $$

(the last equality is due to $-(-1)^k=(-1)^{k+1}$). This agrees exactly, without a single sign discrepancy, with the result $a_k=0,\ b_k=\dfrac{4(-1)^{k+1}}{k\pi}$ obtained directly as the real Fourier series in the main text of Example 28.7. We have confirmed with concrete values that once the complex Fourier coefficients $C_k$ are known, the real coefficients $a_k,b_k$ can be recovered mechanically, without redoing the integrals, using only the transformation formulas of the “Math Note.”

Exercise 28.6: Fourier sine series by odd extension

Solution

Extend $f(x)=x(\pi-x)\ (0 \lt x \lt \pi)$ to the interval $(-\pi,0)$ so that $f(x)=-f(-x)$ there (an odd function), and regard it as a periodic function with period $2\pi$. Because this is an odd extension, Theorem 28.3(II) gives $a_k=0$ from the start, and we get the Fourier sine series $f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_k\sin kx$. The coefficients are

$$ b_k=\frac1\pi\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(x)\sin kx\,dx=\frac2\pi\int_0^{\pi}x(\pi-x)\sin kx\,dx $$

(The integrand $f(x)\sin kx$ is “odd × odd = even,” so we used $\int_{-\pi}^\pi=2\int_0^\pi$. Also, on the interval $(0,\pi)$ we may use the original, unextended $f(x)=x(\pi-x)$ as it is.) We expand the integrand and split the integral into two.

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x(\pi-x)\sin kx\,dx=\pi\int_0^{\pi}x\sin kx\,dx-\int_0^{\pi}x^2\sin kx\,dx $$

First term: $\int_0^\pi x\sin kx\,dx$. By the same integration by parts as in Example 28.1 ($u=x,\ dv=\sin kx\,dx$, $v=-\frac1k\cos kx$),

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x\sin kx\,dx=\left[-\frac{x}{k}\cos kx\right]_0^{\pi}+\frac1k\int_0^{\pi}\cos kx\,dx=-\frac{\pi}{k}\cos k\pi+\frac1k\left[\frac1k\sin kx\right]_0^{\pi}=-\frac{\pi(-1)^k}{k}+0=-\frac{\pi(-1)^k}{k} $$

Second term: $\int_0^\pi x^2\sin kx\,dx$. We use integration by parts twice (in the same pattern as the two-fold integration by parts in Example 28.3). First, setting $u=x^2,\ dv=\sin kx\,dx$ ($v=-\frac1k\cos kx$), we get

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x^2\sin kx\,dx=\left[-\frac{x^2}{k}\cos kx\right]_0^{\pi}+\frac2k\int_0^{\pi}x\cos kx\,dx $$

The first term is $-\frac{\pi^2}{k}(-1)^k$ at $x=\pi$ and $0$ at $x=0$, so it equals $-\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}$. For the second term, $\int_0^\pi x\cos kx\,dx$, we use the indefinite integral formula $\int x\cos kx\,dx=\frac1kx\sin kx+\frac1{k^2}\cos kx$ from the “Math Note” in Section 28.4.

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x\cos kx\,dx=\left[\frac1kx\sin kx+\frac1{k^2}\cos kx\right]_0^{\pi}=\left(0+\frac{(-1)^k}{k^2}\right)-\left(0+\frac1{k^2}\right)=\frac{(-1)^k-1}{k^2} $$

(Since $\sin k\pi=\sin0=0$, the term $\frac1kx\sin kx$ vanishes at both the upper and lower limits.) Substituting this, we obtain

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x^2\sin kx\,dx=-\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}+\frac2k\cdot\frac{(-1)^k-1}{k^2}=-\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}+\frac{2\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{k^3} $$

Combining the two terms.

$$ \pi\int_0^{\pi}x\sin kx\,dx-\int_0^{\pi}x^2\sin kx\,dx =\pi\left(-\frac{\pi(-1)^k}{k}\right)-\left(-\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}+\frac{2\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{k^3}\right) $$ $$ =-\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}+\frac{\pi^2(-1)^k}{k}-\frac{2\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{k^3}=-\frac{2\bigl((-1)^k-1\bigr)}{k^3}=\frac{2\bigl(1-(-1)^k\bigr)}{k^3} $$

(Note that the $\pi^2(-1)^k/k$ terms cancel each other exactly—this is no accident: it is because $x(\pi-x)$ has a shape symmetric about $x=\pi/2$, which makes it a function engineered so that the highest-order divergent term vanishes.) Therefore

$$ b_k=\frac2\pi\cdot\frac{2\bigl(1-(-1)^k\bigr)}{k^3}=\frac{4\bigl(1-(-1)^k\bigr)}{\pi k^3} $$

When $k$ is even, $1-(-1)^k=0$, so $b_k=0$. When $k$ is odd, $1-(-1)^k=2$, so $b_k=\dfrac{8}{\pi k^3}$. Hence the Fourier sine series is

$$ f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{4\bigl(1-(-1)^k\bigr)}{\pi k^3}\sin kx=\frac8\pi\left(\sin x+\frac1{27}\sin3x+\frac1{125}\sin5x+\cdots\right)=\frac8\pi\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(2m+1)x}{(2m+1)^3} $$

(Python check: verify/univmath/ch28_explore4.py, ch28_explore5.py—verified that $b_k=4\{1-(-1)^k\}/(\pi k^3)$ for $k=1,\ldots,6$. The coefficients decay like $1/k^3$ because the function obtained by extending $x(\pi-x)$ as an odd function is itself continuous, and the discontinuity of its derivative is the cause of the jump—note that the coefficients decay even faster than for the square wave, which jumps at its discontinuities (Example 28.5; the coefficients decay like $1/k$), and for the triangle wave, which is continuous but whose derivative is discontinuous (Example 28.6; the coefficients decay like $1/k^2$).)
