Chapter 29 Convergence Theory of Fourier Series

Exercise 29.1: Comparing the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma with a direct computation

Solution

Prediction (from the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma). Since $f(x)=x^3$ is continuous on the closed interval $[-\pi,\pi]$, it is also piecewise continuous. Therefore part (I) of the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma (Theorem 29.1 of this chapter) applies directly, and we can predict that

$$ \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=0 $$

holds.

Direct computation. The integrand $x^3\sin(nx)$ is an odd function ($x^3$) × an odd function ($\sin nx$) = an even function, so we may write $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}=2\int_0^{\pi}$. Hence the Fourier sine coefficient is

$$ b_n=\frac1\pi\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=\frac2\pi\int_0^{\pi}x^3\sin(nx)\,dx $$

We find $\displaystyle\int_0^\pi x^3\sin(nx)\,dx$ by integrating by parts three times. First, $\int x\sin(nx)\,dx=-\dfrac{x}{n}\cos nx+\dfrac1{n^2}\sin nx$ (the formula in the “Math Note” of Chapter 28). Next, using this together with one more integration by parts with $u=x^2,\ dv=\cos(nx)\,dx$, we obtain

$$ \int x^2\cos(nx)\,dx=\frac{x^2}{n}\sin nx-\frac2n\int x\sin(nx)\,dx=\frac{x^2}{n}\sin nx+\frac{2x}{n^2}\cos nx-\frac{2}{n^3}\sin nx $$

(a computation of the same type as in Chapter 2, Section 29.2.3). Finally, setting $u=x^3,\ dv=\sin(nx)\,dx$ ($v=-\frac1n\cos nx$), we have

$$ \int x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=-\frac{x^3}{n}\cos nx+\frac3n\int x^2\cos(nx)\,dx $$

Substituting the $\int x^2\cos(nx)\,dx$ found above into this, we obtain the antiderivative

$$ \int x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=-\frac{x^3}{n}\cos nx+\frac{3x^2}{n^2}\sin nx+\frac{6x}{n^3}\cos nx-\frac{6}{n^4}\sin nx $$

We now evaluate this from $x=0$ to $x=\pi$. Since $n$ is an integer, $\sin(n\pi)=0$, and the two terms containing $\sin nx$ vanish at both the upper and lower limits. Applying $\cos(n\pi)=(-1)^n$ to the remaining $\cos nx$ terms, we get

$$ \int_0^{\pi}x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=\left(-\frac{\pi^3}{n}(-1)^n+\frac{6\pi}{n^3}(-1)^n\right)-0=(-1)^n\pi\cdot\frac{6-\pi^2n^2}{n^3} $$

Therefore,

$$ b_n=\frac2\pi\cdot(-1)^n\pi\cdot\frac{6-\pi^2n^2}{n^3}=\frac{2(-1)^n\bigl(6-\pi^2n^2\bigr)}{n^3}=\frac{2(-1)^{n+1}\bigl(\pi^2n^2-6\bigr)}{n^3} $$

Writing out a few concrete numerical values, we get $b_1=2\pi^2-12\approx7.739$, $b_2=\frac32-\pi^2\approx-8.370$, $b_3=\frac23\pi^2-\frac49\approx6.135$, $b_4\approx-4.747$, $b_5\approx3.852$ (Python check: verify/univmath/ch29_exercises.py—we computed $\int_{-\pi}^\pi x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=\pi b_n$ with SymPy and confirmed that it agrees with the closed form above).

Looking at the behavior as $n\to\infty$, we have $b_n=\dfrac{2(-1)^{n+1}\pi^2n^2}{n^3}-\dfrac{12(-1)^{n+1}}{n^3}=\dfrac{2(-1)^{n+1}\pi^2}{n}-\dfrac{12(-1)^{n+1}}{n^3}$, and the dominant term is $\dfrac{2\pi^2(-1)^{n+1}}{n}$ (its sign oscillates while its magnitude decreases on the order of $1/n$). Therefore the original integral satisfies $\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}x^3\sin(nx)\,dx=\pi b_n\sim\dfrac{2\pi^3(-1)^{n+1}}{n}\to0\ (n\to\infty)$, which does not contradict our initial prediction (convergence to $0$ by the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma). However, the rate of convergence remains only of order $1/n$—this is because, although $f(x)=x^3$ itself is continuous inside $(-\pi,\pi]$, extending it with period $2\pi$ produces a jump at $x=\pi$ ($=-\pi$), where $f(\pi)=\pi^3$ and $f(-\pi)=-\pi^3$ disagree (a discontinuity in the periodic boundary condition), and this is the same reason that the coefficients of $f(x)=x$ in Example 29.1 of this chapter (which likewise jumps at the endpoints) decay like $1/n$.

Exercise 29.2: A direct proof that the Dirichlet kernel is an even function

Solution

We want to show that $Q_n(-u)=Q_n(u)$. Substituting $u\to-u$ into the definition $Q_n(u)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=-n}^{n}e^{iku}$, we get

$$ Q_n(-u)=\sum_{k=-n}^{n}e^{ik(-u)}=\sum_{k=-n}^{n}e^{-iku} $$

Now we replace the summation index $k$ of this sum by $j:=-k$. As $k$ runs from $-n$ to $n$, $j=-k$ also runs over exactly the same range $\{-n,-n+1,\ldots,n\}$, from $n$ down to $-n$ (in the opposite direction)—that is, even if we flip the sign, the set of indices being summed over does not change at all. Moreover, since $e^{-iku}=e^{-i(-j)u}=e^{iju}$, we can rewrite the sum as

$$ \sum_{k=-n}^{n}e^{-iku}=\sum_{j=-n}^{n}e^{iju} $$

(This is a sum of finitely many terms, and we have merely relabeled the same terms with a different symbol, so rearranging the order of summation does not change the value.) If we rename the summation index on the right-hand side from $j$ back to $k$, it is exactly the definition of $Q_n(u)$. Therefore,

$$ Q_n(-u)=\sum_{j=-n}^{n}e^{iju}=Q_n(u) $$

Thus the claim is proved (Python check: verify/univmath/ch29_exercises.py—for each of $n=1,3,7$, we confirmed numerically at five randomly chosen points $u$ that $Q_n(-u)=Q_n(u)$ holds).

Exercise 29.3: Fourier's theorem at a discontinuity of the sawtooth wave

Solution

The sawtooth wave is the function $f(x)=\dfrac{x}{2}\ (-4\lt x\lt4)$ extended with period 8. The left-hand limit at $x=4$ can be taken directly as $x\to4-$ from inside the domain $(-4,4)$, so

$$ f(4-0)=\lim_{x\to4-}\frac{x}{2}=\frac{4}{2}=2 $$

On the other hand, to find the right-hand limit $f(4+0)$ we use periodicity. Since the period is 8, $f(x+8)=f(x)$ holds for all $x$; in particular $f(4+u)=f(4+u-8)=f(-4+u)$ (when $u$ is a small positive number). As $u\to0+$, $x=-4+u$ approaches $-4$ from inside the domain $(-4,4)$, so the expression $f(x)=x/2$ can be used as it stands, and we obtain

$$ f(4+0)=\lim_{u\to0+}f(4+u)=\lim_{u\to0+}f(-4+u)=\lim_{u\to0+}\frac{-4+u}{2}=-\frac{4}{2}=-2 $$

Since $f(4-0)=2\ne-2=f(4+0)$, $x=4$ is a point of discontinuity. By Fourier's theorem (Theorem 29.2), the value of the Fourier series at this point is

$$ \frac{f(4+0)+f(4-0)}{2}=\frac{-2+2}{2}=0 $$

(Direct check using partial sums: substituting $x=4$ into the real Fourier series $f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^\infty\frac{4(-1)^{k+1}}{k\pi}\sin\frac{k\pi}{4}x$ found in Example 28.7, we have $\sin\!\left(\frac{k\pi}{4}\cdot4\right)=\sin(k\pi)=0$ for every integer $k$, so the partial sum is already exactly $0$ at every finite stage $N$—Python check: using verify/univmath/ch29_exercises.py, we confirmed that the partial sum is exactly $0$ for each of $N=1,5,50$.)

Exercise 29.4: Determining the pointwise limit and testing for uniform convergence

Solution

(a) Pointwise limit. For $x=0$, $f_n(0)=n\cdot0\cdot e^0=0$ holds for all $n$. Fix $0\lt x\le1$ and consider $n\to\infty$. The exponential $e^{-nx^2}$ (since $x$ is a fixed positive number) approaches $0$ exponentially in $n$, and the speed of this decay overwhelmingly exceeds the growth of the factor $n$ in front (which grows only polynomially). Hence $n\,x\,e^{-nx^2}\to0$ (in keeping with the sense, learned in high school, that “an exponential function approaches $0$ faster than a polynomial does”; rigorously, setting $t=nx^2\to\infty$, one checks that $n\,e^{-nx^2}=\frac{t}{x}e^{-t}\to0$ ($te^{-t}\to0,\ t\to\infty$)). Therefore,

$$ f(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_n(x)=0\qquad(\text{for all }x\in[0,1]\text{}) $$

The limit function is identically $0$ and, moreover, continuous.

(b) Whether the convergence is uniform. The limit function $f(x)\equiv0$ is continuous, so the contrapositive of Theorem 29.3 (if the limit is discontinuous, the convergence is not uniform) cannot be used here—continuity of the limit does not guarantee uniform convergence, so we must evaluate the $\sup$ directly. Differentiating $f_n(x)=nxe^{-nx^2}$ with respect to $x$, we get

$$ f_n'(x)=n e^{-nx^2}+nx\cdot e^{-nx^2}\cdot(-2nx)=n e^{-nx^2}\bigl(1-2nx^2\bigr) $$

and $f_n'(x)=0$ holds (since $e^{-nx^2}\ne0$) exactly when $1-2nx^2=0$, that is, when $x=x^*:=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}$ (which does lie within the range $0\le x\le1$: if $n\ge1$, then $x^*\le1/\sqrt2\lt1$). Since $f_n'(x)\gt0$ for $x\lt x^*$ and $f_n'(x)\lt0$ for $x\gt x^*$, $x^*$ is the point that gives the maximum. The value there is

$$ f_n(x^*)=n\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\cdot e^{-n\cdot\frac{1}{2n}}=\frac{n}{\sqrt{2n}}\cdot e^{-1/2}=\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}}\cdot\frac1{\sqrt e}=\sqrt{\frac{n}{2e}} $$

Therefore,

$$ \sup_{0\le x\le1}\abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}=\sup_{0\le x\le1}f_n(x)=f_n(x^*)=\sqrt{\frac{n}{2e}}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\ \infty $$

so this $\sup$ not only fails to converge to $0$ but even diverges as $n\to\infty$. In the definition of uniform convergence (Definition 29.2), for any $\varepsilon\gt0$, from some $N$ onward the inequality $\abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}\lt\varepsilon$ must hold for all $x$; but since the $\sup$ diverges, no matter which $N$ we take, there necessarily exists an $x$ (concretely, near $x^*=1/\sqrt{2n}$) with $n\gt N$ and $f_n(x)\ge\varepsilon$. Hence $f_n(x)=nxe^{-nx^2}$ does not converge uniformly on $[0,1]$ (here again we have obtained an example that converges pointwise but not uniformly). (Python check: verify/univmath/ch29_exercises.py, ch29_pointwise_uniform.py—for $n=1,5,20,100,10000$, we confirmed how the values $f_n(x^*)=\sqrt{n/(2e)}$ actually diverge.)

Exercise 29.5: Showing that convergence is not uniform by constructing a counterexample with the $\varepsilon$-$N$ argument

Solution

In Exercise 29.4(b) we showed that $\displaystyle\sup_{0\le x\le1}f_n(x)=\sqrt{\dfrac{n}{2e}}$ and that this diverges to infinity as $n\to\infty$. Using this, we concretely construct the negation of the definition of uniform convergence (Definition 29.2).

First, fix one $\varepsilon\gt0$ (let us take $\varepsilon=1$ here—since the $\sup$ diverges to infinity, the same argument works whichever positive number $\varepsilon$ we choose). Next, suppose that an arbitrary natural number $N$ is given. What we want to show is that “there exists $x\in[0,1]$ for which $n\gt N$ and $\abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}\ge\varepsilon$.”

Since $\sqrt{n/(2e)}$ is monotonically increasing in $n$ and diverges as $n\to\infty$, for $\sqrt{n/(2e)}\ge1$ to hold it suffices that $n\ge2e\approx5.437$ (in terms of integers, $n\ge6$ is enough). To leave a little margin, we therefore choose

$$ n:=\max\{N+1,\ 7\} $$

(this way $n\gt N$, and also $n\ge7\gt2e$). For this $n$, if we take $x:=x^*=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}$, then by the computation in Exercise 29.4(b)

$$ \abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}=f_n(x^*)-0=\sqrt{\frac{n}{2e}}\ge\sqrt{\frac{7}{2e}}\approx1.135\ge1=\varepsilon $$

holds. (To give concrete numbers for $N=1,10,1000$: for $n=7,11,1001$ and $x^*\approx0.2673,0.2132,0.02235$ we get $f_n(x^*)\approx1.135,1.422,13.569$, and indeed every one of these is at least $\varepsilon=1$—Python check: verify/univmath/ch29_exercises.py.)

Thus, with $\varepsilon=1$ fixed, for every natural number $N$ we have actually found an $x\in[0,1]$ (namely $x=1/\sqrt{2n}$) for which $n\gt N$ and $\abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}\ge\varepsilon$. This is precisely the negation of Definition 29.2 of uniform convergence ($\exists\varepsilon\gt0$ s.t. $\forall N,\ \exists n\gt N,\ \exists x\in I$ s.t. $\abs{f_n(x)-f(x)}\ge\varepsilon$), and it constitutes a direct proof, based on the $\varepsilon$-$N$ argument, that $f_n(x)=nxe^{-nx^2}$ does not converge uniformly on $[0,1]$ (it is an argument of exactly the same type as the construction carried out for $f_n(x)=x^n$ in Example 29.6(b)-(ii)).

← Back to Chapter 29, “Convergence Theory of Fourier Series”

Chapter 30 The Fourier Transform

Exercise 30.1: The differentiation rule $\Four[f'(x)](k)=ik\,\Four[f(x)](k)$

Solution

General proof. We use integration by parts. Let $u=e^{-ikx}$ and $dv=f'(x)\,dx$; then $du=-ik\,e^{-ikx}\,dx$ and $v=f(x)$. By the integration by parts formula $\displaystyle\int u\,dv=[uv]-\int v\,du$,

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f'(x)\,e^{-ikx}\,dx=\Bigl[f(x)\,e^{-ikx}\Bigr]_{-\infty}^{\infty}-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\,(-ik\,e^{-ikx})\,dx $$

For the boundary term $\bigl[f(x)e^{-ikx}\bigr]_{-\infty}^{\infty}$ we have $\bigl|f(x)e^{-ikx}\bigr|=|f(x)|\times1=|f(x)|$ ($|e^{-ikx}|=1$ always holds), and by assumption $f(x)\to0$ as $x\to\pm\infty$, so the boundary term itself also tends to $0$. Therefore

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f'(x)e^{-ikx}\,dx=0+ik\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)e^{-ikx}\,dx=ik\,\Four[f(x)](k) $$

That is, we have shown that $\Four[f'(x)](k)=ik\,\Four[f(x)](k)$.

Concrete example: $f(x)=e^{-x^2}$ ($\alpha=1$). Here $f'(x)=-2x\,e^{-x^2}$. Let us compute the left-hand side directly. Completing the square in $-x^2-ikx$ (it suffices to follow the same procedure as in Section 30.4 of the main text with $\alpha=1$), we get $-x^2-ikx=-\left(x+i\dfrac{k}{2}\right)^2-\dfrac{k^2}{4}$, so

$$ \Four[f'(x)](k)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(-2x)e^{-x^2-ikx}\,dx=-2e^{-\frac{k^2}{4}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,e^{-\left(x+i\frac{k}{2}\right)^2}\,dx $$

Substituting $u:=x+i\dfrac{k}{2}$ ($x=u-i\dfrac{k}{2}$, $du=dx$), we obtain

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,e^{-\left(x+i\frac k2\right)^2}dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(u-i\frac k2\right)e^{-u^2}\,du=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u\,e^{-u^2}\,du-i\frac k2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-u^2}\,du $$

The integrand $u\,e^{-u^2}$ of the first term is an odd function (its sign reverses under $u\to-u$), so its integral over a symmetric range is $0$. The second term is exactly the Gaussian integral shown in the main text (the case $\alpha=1$), $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-u^2}du=\sqrt\pi$. Hence

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,e^{-\left(x+i\frac k2\right)^2}dx=0-i\frac k2\sqrt\pi=-\frac{ik\sqrt\pi}{2} $$

Therefore

$$ \Four[f'(x)](k)=-2e^{-\frac{k^2}{4}}\times\left(-\frac{ik\sqrt\pi}{2}\right)=ik\sqrt\pi\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{4}} $$

On the other hand, the right-hand side is $ik\,\Four[f(x)](k)=ik\times\sqrt\pi\,e^{-k^2/4}$ (obtained by substituting $\alpha=1$ into the general formula for the Fourier transform of a Gaussian function), and the two agree exactly (verified with SymPy as well: the difference between $F[f'](k)=i\sqrt\pi\,k\,e^{-k^2/4}$ and $ikF[f](k)=i\sqrt\pi\,k\,e^{-k^2/4}$ is identically $0$).

Exercise 30.2: Computing $\Four[x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}](k)$ by differentiation with respect to a parameter

Solution

We differentiate both sides of the general formula for the Fourier transform of a Gaussian function

$$ F(\alpha,k):=\Four[e^{-\alpha x^2}](k)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\alpha}}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}} $$

partially with respect to the parameter $\alpha$, which is independent of the integration variable $x$. For the left-hand side, assuming that the order of integration and differentiation can be interchanged,

$$ \frac{\partial F}{\partial\alpha}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha x^2}e^{-ikx}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\bigl(e^{-\alpha x^2}\bigr)e^{-ikx}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(-x^2)e^{-\alpha x^2}e^{-ikx}\,dx=-\Four[x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}](k) $$

(here $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}e^{-\alpha x^2}=-x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}$). For the right-hand side, using the quotient rule and the chain rule,

$$ \frac{\partial F}{\partial\alpha}=\sqrt\pi\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\Bigl(\alpha^{-1/2}e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}}\Bigr)=\sqrt\pi\left(-\frac12\alpha^{-3/2}e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}}+\alpha^{-1/2}e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}}\cdot\frac{k^2}{4\alpha^2}\right) $$

(we used $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}\Bigl(-\dfrac{k^2}{4\alpha}\Bigr)=\dfrac{k^2}{4\alpha^2}$). Factoring out the common factor $e^{-k^2/4\alpha}\alpha^{-3/2}$, we get

$$ \frac{\partial F}{\partial\alpha}=\sqrt\pi\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}}\,\alpha^{-3/2}\left(-\frac12+\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}\right)=\frac{\sqrt\pi\,(k^2-2\alpha)}{4\alpha^{5/2}}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}} $$

Therefore, since $\Four[x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}](k)=-\dfrac{\partial F}{\partial\alpha}$,

$$ \Four[x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}](k)=\frac{\sqrt\pi\,(2\alpha-k^2)}{4\alpha^{5/2}}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{4\alpha}} $$

This is the answer. The result of computing $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^2e^{-\alpha x^2}e^{-ikx}\,dx$ directly (verified with SymPy) is exactly the same expression, and the difference is identically $0$. A numerical check ($\alpha=1,k=2$) has also confirmed that the expression obtained by differentiating with respect to the parameter and the one obtained by direct integration agree, both giving the same value.

Exercise 30.3: The shift theorem $\Four[f(x-a)](k)=e^{-ika}\Four[f(x)](k)$

Solution

General proof. Substituting $u:=x-a$ in the defining integral ($x=u+a$, $dx=du$, and the interval of integration, $x:-\infty\to\infty$, corresponds to $u:-\infty\to\infty$), we obtain

$$ \Four[f(x-a)](k)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x-a)e^{-ikx}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(u)\,e^{-ik(u+a)}\,du=e^{-ika}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(u)e^{-iku}\,du=e^{-ika}\,\Four[f(x)](k) $$

(We split $e^{-ik(u+a)}=e^{-iku}e^{-ika}$ by the law of exponents and took the constant factor $e^{-ika}$, which depends only on $k$, outside the integral.) This proves the shift theorem.

Concrete example: $f(x)=e^{-\alpha|x|}$ shifted by $a$. It suffices to apply the general proof itself to $f(x)=e^{-\alpha|x|}$. By the computation above, the Fourier transform of $f(x-a)=e^{-\alpha|x-a|}$ is

$$ \Four[e^{-\alpha|x-a|}](k)=e^{-ika}\,\Four[e^{-\alpha|x|}](k)=e^{-ika}\cdot\frac{2\alpha}{\alpha^2+k^2} $$

which agrees exactly with the prediction of the shift theorem (note that in this case the procedure of the proof of the shift theorem is itself a “direct computation” for the specific function $f(x)=e^{-\alpha|x|}$—because we have merely applied a relation that holds for a general $f$ to a particular $f$. A numerical check, too, has confirmed that the two sides agree when specific numerical values are substituted for $\alpha,a,k$).

Exercise 30.4: The scaling theorem $\Four[f(cx)](k)=\dfrac1c\,\Four[f(x)]\!\left(\dfrac kc\right)\ (c\gt0)$

Solution

General proof. Substitute $u:=cx$ ($x=u/c$, $dx=du/c$) in the defining integral. Since $c\gt0$, as $x:-\infty\to\infty$ we have $u:-\infty\to\infty$, so the direction is unchanged.

$$ \Four[f(cx)](k)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(cx)e^{-ikx}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(u)\,e^{-ik(u/c)}\,\frac{du}{c}=\frac1c\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(u)\,e^{-i(k/c)u}\,du=\frac1c\,\Four[f(x)]\!\left(\frac kc\right) $$

This proves the scaling theorem.

Concrete example: $f(x)=e^{-x^2}$, $c=2$. Here $f(cx)=f(2x)=e^{-(2x)^2}=e^{-4x^2}$. For the left-hand side, substituting $\alpha=4$ directly into the general formula for the Fourier transform of a Gaussian function, we get

$$ \Four[e^{-4x^2}](k)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{4}}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{16}}=\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{16}} $$

For the right-hand side, applying the scaling theorem to $\Four[e^{-x^2}](k)=\sqrt\pi\,e^{-k^2/4}$ ($\alpha=1$), we get

$$ \frac1c\,\Four[e^{-x^2}]\!\left(\frac kc\right)=\frac12\times\sqrt\pi\,e^{-\frac{(k/2)^2}{4}}=\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2}\,e^{-\frac{k^2}{16}} $$

so the left-hand and right-hand sides agree completely (numerical check: for $k=1.3$, both are $\fallingdotseq0.79739$, in agreement).

Exercise 30.5: The duality $\Four[xf(x)](k)=i\dfrac{d}{dk}\Four[f(x)](k)$ between “multiplication by $x$” and “differentiation with respect to $k$”

Solution

General proof. We differentiate $F(k)=\Four[f(x)](k)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)e^{-ikx}\,dx$ with respect to $k$, assuming that the order of integration and differentiation can be interchanged. Since $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial k}e^{-ikx}=-ix\,e^{-ikx}$,

$$ \frac{dF}{dk}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\,(-ix)\,e^{-ikx}\,dx=-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}xf(x)e^{-ikx}\,dx=-i\,\Four[xf(x)](k) $$

Multiplying both sides by $i$ and using $i\times(-i)=-i^2=1$, we obtain $i\dfrac{dF}{dk}=\Four[xf(x)](k)$. This is the formula to be shown. (Comparing with the differentiation rule $\Four[f'](k)=ik\Four[f](k)$ of Exercise 30.1, we see that the operation “multiply by $x$” and the operation “differentiate with respect to $k$,” and likewise the operation “differentiate” and the operation “multiply by $k$,” are dual to each other, with their roles interchanged.)

Concrete example: $f(x)=e^{-2|x|}$ ($\alpha=2$). First we compute the right-hand side. Since $F(k)=\Four[e^{-2|x|}](k)=\dfrac{2\times2}{2^2+k^2}=\dfrac{4}{4+k^2}$,

$$ i\frac{dF}{dk}=i\times4\times\frac{-2k}{(4+k^2)^2}=\frac{-8ik}{(4+k^2)^2} $$

Next we compute the left-hand side $\Four[xe^{-2|x|}](k)$ directly, splitting the interval of integration into two, as we did for the two-sided exponential function in Section 30.5.

$$ \Four[xe^{-2|x|}](k)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}x\,e^{2x}e^{-ikx}\,dx+\int_{0}^{\infty}x\,e^{-2x}e^{-ikx}\,dx=\int_{-\infty}^{0}x\,e^{(2-ik)x}\,dx+\int_{0}^{\infty}x\,e^{-(2+ik)x}\,dx $$

Here we use the formula $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}t\,e^{-ct}\,dt=\frac{1}{c^2}$, which holds for a complex constant $c$ such that the real part of $c$ is positive, $\operatorname{Re}(c)\gt0$ (by integration by parts with $u=t,\ dv=e^{-ct}dt$, $\displaystyle\int_0^\infty t e^{-ct}dt=\Bigl[-\frac{t}{c}e^{-ct}\Bigr]_0^\infty+\frac1c\int_0^\infty e^{-ct}dt=0+\frac1c\cdot\frac1c=\frac1{c^2}$; the boundary term tends to $0$ as $t\to\infty$ because $\operatorname{Re}(c)\gt0$). The second term can be used as it stands with $c=2+ik$ ($\operatorname{Re}(c)=2\gt0$):

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}x\,e^{-(2+ik)x}\,dx=\frac{1}{(2+ik)^2} $$

For the first term, substituting $x=-t$ ($t:0\to\infty$, $dx=-dt$), we obtain

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{0}x\,e^{(2-ik)x}\,dx=\int_{\infty}^{0}(-t)\,e^{-(2-ik)t}\,(-dt)=-\int_0^\infty t\,e^{-(2-ik)t}\,dt=-\frac{1}{(2-ik)^2} $$

(we use the same formula with $c=2-ik$, $\operatorname{Re}(c)=2\gt0$; note also that the sign reversal occurs twice and a factor $-1$ is attached overall). Therefore,

$$ \Four[xe^{-2|x|}](k)=\frac{1}{(2+ik)^2}-\frac{1}{(2-ik)^2} $$

We combine these over a common denominator. The denominator is $(2+ik)^2(2-ik)^2=\bigl[(2+ik)(2-ik)\bigr]^2=(4+k^2)^2$ (since $(2+ik)(2-ik)=4-(ik)^2=4+k^2$). The numerator is $(2-ik)^2-(2+ik)^2=(4-4ik-k^2)-(4+4ik-k^2)=-8ik$. Hence

$$ \Four[xe^{-2|x|}](k)=\frac{-8ik}{(4+k^2)^2} $$

This agrees exactly with $i\dfrac{dF}{dk}=\dfrac{-8ik}{(4+k^2)^2}$, which we found for the right-hand side (verified with SymPy as well: the difference between $A-B:=\frac1{(2+ik)^2}-\frac1{(2-ik)^2}$ and $-8ik/(4+k^2)^2$ is identically $0$).

Exercise 30.6: Checking the parity theorem (the rectangular pulse with $a=0$)

Solution

Consider the rectangular pulse with $a=0$, $f(\alpha)=\dfrac{1}{2r}\ (-r\lt\alpha\lt r)$. First we check that it is an even function. As for $f(-\alpha)$, since $-r\lt\alpha\lt r\iff-r\lt-\alpha\lt r$ (we merely multiplied each side of the inequalities by $-1$, which reverses their direction, and then rearranged), $f(-\alpha)=\dfrac{1}{2r}=f(\alpha)$ holds exactly when $-\alpha$ also lies in the same interval $(-r,r)$, and outside the interval both are $0$. Therefore $f(-\alpha)=f(\alpha)$ for all $\alpha$; that is, $f$ is an even function.

By Example 30.2(a), its Fourier transform is given by

$$ F(k)=\Four[f(\alpha)](k)=e^{-ik\cdot0}\,\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr}=\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr} $$

Here we have substituted $a=0$ into the general formula $\Four[f(\alpha)](k)=e^{-ika}\sin(kr)/(kr)$.

Check that it is real. Both $\sin(kr)$ and $kr$ are real when $k,r$ are real, and the imaginary unit $i$ appears nowhere. Hence $F(k)=\sin(kr)/(kr)$ is real-valued (as long as the denominator is not $0$).

Check that it is even. Under $k\to-k$ we have $\sin(-kr)=-\sin(kr)$ (the sine function is odd), and the denominator becomes $(-k)r=-kr$, so

$$ F(-k)=\frac{\sin(-kr)}{(-k)r}=\frac{-\sin(kr)}{-kr}=\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr}=F(k) $$

Thus $F(-k)=F(k)$; that is, $F$ is also an even function.

From the above, we have directly confirmed that the Fourier transform $F(k)=\sin(kr)/(kr)$ of the even function $f(\alpha)$ is real and even. This agrees exactly with the prediction of Theorem 30.1(b) (if $f$ is a real-valued even function, then $F$ is real-valued and even), and means that we have been able to check, for a concrete rectangular pulse, a theorem that holds in general.

Chapter 31 Definition of the Laplace Transform and Basic Formulas

Exercise 31.1: Computing $\Lap[t^3]$ directly from the definition

Solution

Following the definition ($\Lap[f(t)]=\int_0^\infty f(t)e^{-st}dt$), we compute $\displaystyle\Lap[t^3]=\int_0^\infty t^3e^{-st}\,dt$ by repeating integration by parts three times. Let $u=-\frac1se^{-st}$ (an antiderivative of $e^{-st}$) and $v=t^3$ ($v'=3t^2$). Integrating by parts once gives

$$ \int_0^\infty t^3e^{-st}\,dt = \underbrace{\left[t^3\left(-\frac1se^{-st}\right)\right]_0^\infty}_{0} + \frac3s\int_0^\infty t^2e^{-st}\,dt $$

(Why the boundary term is $0$: at $t=0$, $t^3=0$. As $t\to\infty$, for $s>0$ we have $t^3e^{-st}\to0$—the exponential decays faster than the polynomial grows.) Applying integration by parts once more to the remaining integral ($v=t^2,v'=2t$), we get

$$ \int_0^\infty t^2e^{-st}\,dt = \underbrace{\left[t^2\left(-\frac1se^{-st}\right)\right]_0^\infty}_{0} + \frac2s\int_0^\infty te^{-st}\,dt $$

Applying integration by parts one more time ($v=t,v'=1$), we get

$$ \int_0^\infty te^{-st}\,dt = \underbrace{\left[t\left(-\frac1se^{-st}\right)\right]_0^\infty}_{0} + \frac1s\int_0^\infty e^{-st}\,dt = \frac1s\cdot\frac1s = \frac1{s^2} $$

Substituting these results one after another from the bottom up, we obtain

$$ \int_0^\infty t^2e^{-st}\,dt = \frac2s\cdot\frac1{s^2} = \frac2{s^3},\qquad \int_0^\infty t^3e^{-st}\,dt = \frac3s\cdot\frac2{s^3} = \frac6{s^4} $$

Therefore $\Lap[t^3]=\dfrac6{s^4}$ ($s>0$). This agrees with $3!/s^4=6/s^4$, obtained by substituting $n=3$ into Formula 31.3 ($\Lap[t^n]=n!/s^{n+1}$) (we have also verified that SymPy's improper integral gives the same value).

Exercise 31.2: Finding $\Lap[5e^{2t}-3t+7]$ by linearity

Solution

By linearity ($\Lap[c_1f+c_2g+c_3h]=c_1\Lap[f]+c_2\Lap[g]+c_3\Lap[h]$, which holds in the same way for three terms),

$$ \Lap[5e^{2t}-3t+7] = 5\Lap[e^{2t}] - 3\Lap[t] + 7\Lap[1] $$

We substitute the entries of the table of basic transforms for each term: $\Lap[e^{2t}]=\dfrac1{s-2}$ (substituting $\omega=2$ into the formula $\Lap[e^{\omega t}]=1/(s-\omega)$, for $s>2$), $\Lap[t]=\dfrac1{s^2}$, and $\Lap[1]=\dfrac1s$. Therefore,

$$ \Lap[5e^{2t}-3t+7] = \frac5{s-2} - \frac3{s^2} + \frac7s\qquad(s>2) $$

is the answer. The region of convergence is $s>2$ because, of the three terms, $\Lap[e^{2t}]$ imposes the most restrictive condition ($s>2$), and for the sum as a whole we must take the intersection of the conditions (we have also verified that SymPy's symbolic computation gives the same expression).

Exercise 31.3: Laplace transform of a function that switches from “1 first” to “0 later”

Solution

Let $g(t)$ denote the desired function: $g(t)=1$ ($0\le t\lt a$), $g(t)=0$ ($t\ge a$). Using the unit step function $u(t-a)$ (which is $0$ for $t\lt a$ and $1$ for $t\ge a$), we can write $g(t)=1-u(t-a)$—indeed, for $t\lt a$ we get $1-0=1$ and for $t\ge a$ we get $1-1=0$, which we can confirm agrees with the values of $g(t)$. Using linearity,

$$ \Lap[g(t)] = \Lap[1-u(t-a)] = \Lap[1] - \Lap[u(t-a)] $$

Substituting the entry $\Lap[1]=\dfrac1s$ of the table of basic transforms and the formula $\Lap[u(t-a)]=\dfrac{e^{-as}}{s}$ ($a\ge0$), we obtain

$$ \Lap[g(t)] = \frac1s - \frac{e^{-as}}{s} = \frac{1-e^{-as}}{s}\qquad(s>0) $$

which is the answer (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 31.4: Laplace transform of a sawtooth wave

Solution

We use the formula for the Laplace transform of a periodic function, $\Lap[f(t)]=\dfrac{F_0(s)}{1-e^{-sT}}$ ($F_0(s)=\int_0^Tf(t)e^{-st}dt$). First we find $F_0(s)=\displaystyle\int_0^T te^{-st}\,dt$. Integrating by parts with $u=-\frac1se^{-st}$ and $v=t$ ($v'=1$), we get

$$ \int_0^T te^{-st}\,dt = \left[t\left(-\frac1se^{-st}\right)\right]_0^T - \int_0^T\left(-\frac1se^{-st}\right)dt $$

The boundary term is $-\dfrac Ts e^{-sT}$ at $t=T$ and $0$ at $t=0$, so $\left[\,\cdot\,\right]_0^T=-\dfrac Ts e^{-sT}-0=-\dfrac Ts e^{-sT}$. The remaining integral is $\displaystyle\int_0^T\frac1se^{-st}\,dt=\frac1s\left[-\frac1se^{-st}\right]_0^T=\frac1{s^2}\bigl(1-e^{-sT}\bigr)$, so

$$ F_0(s) = -\frac Ts e^{-sT} + \frac1{s^2}\bigl(1-e^{-sT}\bigr) = \frac1{s^2} - e^{-sT}\left(\frac Ts+\frac1{s^2}\right) = \frac{1-e^{-sT}(1+sT)}{s^2} $$

Substituting this into the formula for periodic functions, we obtain

$$ \Lap[f(t)] = \frac{F_0(s)}{1-e^{-sT}} = \frac{1-e^{-sT}(1+sT)}{s^2\bigl(1-e^{-sT}\bigr)} $$

which is the answer. Multiplying the numerator and the denominator by $e^{sT}$ to rearrange it into a form with positive exponents, we can also write it in the equivalent form $\dfrac{e^{sT}-1-sT}{s^2\bigl(e^{sT}-1\bigr)}$ (for $T=1,s=2$, we have verified that the value of this expression agrees to 30 decimal places with the value obtained by directly integrating the first 60 periods numerically and adding them up).

Exercise 31.5: Finding $\Lap[\delta(t-3)\cos2t]$ by the sifting property of the delta function

Solution

Substituting into Definition 31.1 gives $\displaystyle\Lap[\delta(t-3)\cos2t]=\int_0^\infty \cos2t\cdot e^{-st}\cdot\delta(t-3)\,dt$. Since $a=3\ge0$, extending the interval of integration to $(-\infty,\infty)$ does not change the value (because $\delta(t-3)=0$ for $t\lt0$). Letting $f(t)=\cos2t\cdot e^{-st}$ and applying the sifting property (Definition 31.3), we pick out the value at $t=3$.

$$ \Lap[\delta(t-3)\cos2t] = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \cos2t\cdot e^{-st}\cdot\delta(t-3)\,dt = \cos(2\cdot3)\cdot e^{-3s} = \cos6\cdot e^{-3s} $$

is the answer ($\cos6$ is evaluated with the angle in radians, $\cos6\approx0.960$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 31.6: Determining whether a function is of exponential order $\alpha$

Solution

(a) $f(t)=t^{10}$. The limit

$$ \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t^{10}}{e^t} = \lim_{t\to\infty}t^{10}e^{-t} = 0 $$

holds (because the exponential function grows faster than a polynomial of any degree). Therefore there exists a sufficiently large $M$ (it suffices to take $M$ to be the maximum of $t^{10}e^{-t}$ over all $t\ge0$) such that $t^{10}\le Me^t$ holds. Hence $f(t)=t^{10}$ is a function of exponential order $1$.

(b) $f(t)=e^{t^3}$. Suppose it were of exponential order $\alpha$ ($\alpha$ finite). Then $|e^{t^3}|\le Me^{\alpha t}$, that is, $e^{t^3-\alpha t}\le M$, would have to hold for all $t\ge0$. But as $t\to\infty$, $t^3-\alpha t=t(t^2-\alpha)\to\infty$ (once $t$ becomes larger than $\sqrt\alpha$, $t^2-\alpha>0$ and $t(t^2-\alpha)$ grows without bound), so $e^{t^3-\alpha t}\to\infty$, and this inequality is violated no matter which $M$ we choose. Therefore $f(t)=e^{t^3}$ is not a function of exponential order $\alpha$ for any choice of $\alpha$.

Exercise 31.7: Expressing $\Lap[f''(t)]$ by using the derivative formula twice

Solution

Let $g(t):=f'(t)$ (we assume that $g$ is also continuous and of exponential order for $t\ge0$). Applying the derivative formula to $g$, we get

$$ \Lap[g'(t)] = s\Lap[g(t)] - g(0) $$

Here $g'(t)=f''(t)$ and $g(0)=f'(0)$, and for $\Lap[g(t)]=\Lap[f'(t)]$ we can use the result $\Lap[f'(t)]=sF(s)-f(0)$ of applying the derivative formula once more (to $f$ itself). Substituting all of these, we obtain

$$ \Lap[f''(t)] = s\Lap[f'(t)] - f'(0) = s\bigl(sF(s)-f(0)\bigr) - f'(0) = s^2F(s) - sf(0) - f'(0) $$

which is the general formula.

We check this with $f(t)=t^3$. We have $f(0)=0^3=0$, $f'(0)=0$ because $f'(t)=3t^2$, and $F(s)=\Lap[t^3]=\dfrac6{s^4}$ (the result of Exercise 31.1). Substituting into the general formula, we get

$$ \Lap[f''(t)] = s^2\cdot\frac6{s^4} - s\cdot0 - 0 = \frac6{s^2} $$

On the other hand, if we take the Laplace transform of $f''(t)=\dfrac{d}{dt}(3t^2)=6t$ directly, Formula 31.2 ($\Lap[t]=1/s^2$) and linearity give $\Lap[6t]=6\Lap[t]=\dfrac6{s^2}$, which agrees exactly with the value found from the general formula (verified with SymPy; we also checked separately that the case $f(t)=\cos\omega t$—with $f(0)=1,f'(0)=0$—agrees in the same way).

← Back to Chapter 31, “Definition of the Laplace Transform and Basic Formulas”

Chapter 32 The Gamma Function, the Beta Function, and the Laplace Transform

Exercise 32.1: Find $\Gamma(7/2)$

Solution

We use the recurrence relation $\Gamma(\alpha+1)=\alpha\Gamma(\alpha)$ repeatedly until we reach $\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt\pi$. Since $\dfrac72=\dfrac52+1$, substituting $\alpha=\dfrac52$ into the recurrence relation gives

$$ \Gamma\left(\frac72\right) = \frac52\,\Gamma\left(\frac52\right) $$

As found in Example 32.3 of the main text, $\Gamma\!\left(\dfrac52\right)=\dfrac34\sqrt\pi$. (To be safe, here is the route: $\Gamma\!\left(\frac52\right)=\frac32\Gamma\!\left(\frac32\right)$ and $\Gamma\!\left(\frac32\right)=\frac12\Gamma\!\left(\frac12\right)=\frac12\sqrt\pi$, and therefore $\Gamma\!\left(\frac52\right)=\frac32\cdot\frac12\sqrt\pi=\frac34\sqrt\pi$.) Substituting this, we obtain

$$ \Gamma\left(\frac72\right) = \frac52\cdot\frac34\sqrt\pi = \frac{15}{8}\sqrt\pi $$

Numerically, $\Gamma(7/2)=\dfrac{15}8\sqrt\pi\approx3.323351$ (verified with SymPy: gamma(Rational(7,2)) returns the same value $\frac{15\sqrt\pi}8$—verify/univmath/ch32_check.py). In general, the half-integer value $\Gamma\!\left(n+\frac12\right)$ always comes out as “a rational number $\times\sqrt\pi$”: each of the $n$ applications of the recurrence relation brings in one more factor of $2$ in the denominator and one more odd number in the numerator.

Exercise 32.2: Show that $\mathrm{erf}(x)+\mathrm{erfc}(x)=1$ using differentiation

Solution

Let $h(x)=\mathrm{erf}(x)+\mathrm{erfc}(x)$; we first find its derivative $h'(x)$.

Derivative of $\mathrm{erf}(x)$. By the fundamental theorem of calculus (Chapter 5), when the integral $\displaystyle\int_0^xe^{-u^2}du$ whose upper limit is the variable $x$ is differentiated with respect to $x$, we simply get the value $e^{-x^2}$ of the integrand with $u$ replaced by $x$. Therefore,

$$ \frac{d}{dx}\mathrm{erf}(x) = \frac2{\sqrt\pi}\cdot e^{-x^2} $$

Derivative of $\mathrm{erfc}(x)$. The function $\mathrm{erfc}(x)=\dfrac2{\sqrt\pi}\displaystyle\int_x^{\infty}e^{-u^2}du$ is an integral whose lower limit is the variable $x$. To convert it from this form, with the constant $\infty$ as the upper limit and a variable lower limit, into the familiar form with a variable upper limit and a constant lower limit, it suffices to reverse the interval of integration and change the sign.

$$ \int_x^{\infty}e^{-u^2}\,du = \int_0^{\infty}e^{-u^2}\,du - \int_0^{x}e^{-u^2}\,du $$

(The right-hand side is just what we get by transposing a term in the equation that splits the integral from $0$ to $\infty$ into the integrals from $0$ to $x$ and from $x$ to $\infty$.) The first term on the right, $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}e^{-u^2}du=\dfrac{\sqrt\pi}2$, is a constant independent of $x$, so it vanishes when differentiated with respect to $x$. The second term has the same form as the one we differentiated for $\mathrm{erf}$, so

$$ \frac{d}{dx}\int_x^{\infty}e^{-u^2}\,du = 0 - e^{-x^2} = -e^{-x^2} $$

Therefore,

$$ \frac{d}{dx}\mathrm{erfc}(x) = \frac2{\sqrt\pi}\cdot\left(-e^{-x^2}\right) = -\frac2{\sqrt\pi}e^{-x^2} $$

Adding the two.

$$ h'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\mathrm{erf}(x)+\frac{d}{dx}\mathrm{erfc}(x) = \frac2{\sqrt\pi}e^{-x^2}-\frac2{\sqrt\pi}e^{-x^2} = 0 $$

A function whose derivative is identically $0$ for all $x$ is a constant function. (This is a corollary of the mean value theorem: if $h$ were not constant, there would be two distinct points $a\ne b$ with $h(a)\ne h(b)$, and then, by the mean value theorem, there would exist a point somewhere between them at which $h'(c)=\dfrac{h(b)-h(a)}{b-a}\ne0$, contradicting $h'\equiv0$.) Therefore, $h(x)=\mathrm{erf}(x)+\mathrm{erfc}(x)$ equals some constant $C$.

The value of the constant $C$ may be evaluated at any $x$, so we choose $x=0$, which is easy to compute. We have $\mathrm{erf}(0)=\dfrac2{\sqrt\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^0e^{-u^2}du=\dfrac2{\sqrt\pi}\cdot0=0$ (because the interval of integration has width $0$). Also, as shown in Example 32.1(a) of the main text, $\mathrm{erfc}(0)=1$. Therefore,

$$ C = h(0) = \mathrm{erf}(0)+\mathrm{erfc}(0) = 0+1 = 1 $$

Hence $\mathrm{erf}(x)+\mathrm{erfc}(x)=1$ for every real number $x$. (In the main text we showed this at one stroke by splitting the interval of integration; here we have reached exactly the same conclusion by a different route, “the derivative is $0$ ⟹ the function is constant.” Both proofs are, in essence, the flip side of the fact that the error function and the complementary error function split the same Gaussian integral between them into exactly two parts.)

Exercise 32.3: Compute $\Lap[t^3]$ from the gamma function formula

Solution

Substituting $\alpha=3$ (an integer) into the general formula for the Laplace transform in terms of the gamma function, $\Lap[t^{\alpha}]=\dfrac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}{s^{\alpha+1}}$, we obtain

$$ \Lap[t^3] = \frac{\Gamma(3+1)}{s^{3+1}} = \frac{\Gamma(4)}{s^4} $$

Substituting $n=3$ into the formula $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$ gives $\Gamma(4)=\Gamma(3+1)=3!=3\times2\times1=6$. Therefore,

$$ \Lap[t^3] = \frac6{s^4} $$

On the other hand, substituting $n=3$ directly into the formula $\Lap[t^n]=\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$ of Chapter 31 gives $\Lap[t^3]=\dfrac{3!}{s^4}=\dfrac6{s^4}$, which agrees exactly with the computation via the gamma function (verified with SymPy: laplace_transform(t**3,t,s) gives the same result—verify/univmath/ch32_check.py). When $n$ is an integer, the gamma function formula merely requires reading $\Gamma(n+1)$ as $n!$, and it reduces directly to the formula of Chapter 31; this has been confirmed here as well.

Exercise 32.4: Find the relative errors of the simple and precise versions of Stirling's formula for $n=5$

Solution

The true value is $5!=120$.

Simple version. Substituting $n=5$ into $n^ne^{-n}$, we have $5^5=3125$ and $e^{-5}\approx0.00673794700$, so

$$ 5^5e^{-5} \approx 3125\times0.00673794700 \approx 21.0561 $$

The relative error with respect to the true value is

$$ \frac{|120-21.0561|}{120} = \frac{98.9439}{120} \approx 0.8245 = 82.45\% $$

Precise version. We substitute $n=5$ into $\sqrt{2\pi n}\,n^ne^{-n}$. Since $2\pi\times5=10\pi\approx31.4159$ and $\sqrt{31.4159}\approx5.60499$,

$$ \sqrt{2\pi\cdot5}\times21.0561 \approx 5.60499\times21.0561 \approx 118.019 $$

The relative error with respect to the true value is

$$ \frac{|120-118.019|}{120} = \frac{1.981}{120} \approx 0.01651 = 1.65\% $$

(Verified with Python—verify/univmath/ch32_check.py.) Compared with the relative errors in Example 32.6 ($n=10$; simple version $87.49\%$, precise version $0.83\%$), the errors for the smaller $n$, that is, $n=5$, are somewhat larger for both the simple and the precise versions—this is consistent with what we saw in Figure 32.5, namely that the error of the precise version decreases monotonically as $n$ grows (whereas that of the simple version, conversely, increases).

Exercise 32.5: Prove that $B(m,n)=B(n,m)$

Solution

In the definition $B(m,n)=\displaystyle\int_0^1x^{m-1}(1-x)^{n-1}dx$, we make the substitution $x=1-u$ (so that $dx=-du$). For the limits of integration, $x=0$ gives $u=1-0=1$ and $x=1$ gives $u=1-1=0$, so $u$ runs from $1$ to $0$ (the direction is reversed).

$$ B(m,n) = \int_{x=0}^{x=1}x^{m-1}(1-x)^{n-1}\,dx = \int_{u=1}^{u=0}(1-u)^{m-1}\cdot u^{n-1}\,(-du) $$

(Substituting $x=1-u$ gives $x^{m-1}=(1-u)^{m-1}$, and from $1-x=1-(1-u)=u$ we get $(1-x)^{n-1}=u^{n-1}$.) Interchanging the upper and lower limits of the integral reverses the sign once more, by the property $\displaystyle\int_1^0(\cdots)=-\int_0^1(\cdots)$ of definite integrals. Therefore, the sign reversal from $(-du)$ and the sign reversal from interchanging the upper and lower limits, two minus signs in total, exactly cancel each other, and we obtain

$$ B(m,n) = \int_0^1 (1-u)^{m-1}u^{n-1}\,du = \int_0^1 u^{n-1}(1-u)^{m-1}\,du $$

(We have merely changed the order of the factors in the product.) Now, rewriting the integration variable $u$ as $x$ does not change the value of a definite integral (a fact that we also used in the exercises of Chapter 28: a definite integral does not depend on the name of the variable). Therefore,

$$ B(m,n) = \int_0^1 x^{n-1}(1-x)^{m-1}\,dx $$

The right-hand side is exactly the defining expression with the roles of $m$ and $n$ interchanged, that is, $B(n,m)$ itself. Hence

$$ B(m,n) = B(n,m) $$

holds for all $m,n\gt0$ (what we intuitively expected in Section 32.4.2 of the main text from the symmetry in Figure 32.6 has now been rigorously proved here by substitution). Numerically as well, for example, for $(m,n)=(2,3)$ we have $B(2,3)=\frac1{12}$ (Example 32.7 of the main text), and for $(m,n)=(3,2)$ we have $B(3,2)=\int_0^1x^2(1-x)\,dx=\left[\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^4}4\right]_0^1=\frac13-\frac14=\frac1{12}$, so we can confirm that the two indeed agree (also verified with SymPy for several pairs $(m,n)$—verify/univmath/ch32_check.py).

Exercise 32.6: Compute $B(1,n)$ directly from the definition

Solution

Substituting $m=1$ into the definition gives $x^{m-1}=x^0=1$, so the factor involving $x$ disappears from the integrand.

$$ B(1,n) = \int_0^1 x^{1-1}(1-x)^{n-1}\,dx = \int_0^1(1-x)^{n-1}\,dx $$

We use the substitution $v=1-x$ ($dv=-dx$). Since $v=1$ when $x=0$ and $v=0$ when $x=1$,

$$ \int_0^1(1-x)^{n-1}\,dx = \int_1^0 v^{n-1}\,(-dv) = \int_0^1 v^{n-1}\,dv = \left[\frac{v^n}n\right]_0^1 = \frac1n-0 = \frac1n $$

Therefore, $B(1,n)=\dfrac1n$.

We compare this with the value computed from the theorem of the main text ($B(m,n)=\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)/\Gamma(m+n)$). Since $\Gamma(1)=1$ (Formula 32.2) and $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$ (Formula 32.5),

$$ B(1,n) = \frac{\Gamma(1)\Gamma(n)}{\Gamma(1+n)} = \frac{1\times(n-1)!}{n!} = \frac{(n-1)!}{n\times(n-1)!} = \frac1n $$

(We used $\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!$ and $\Gamma(n+1)=n!=n\times(n-1)!$.) The two agree exactly (verified with SymPy: $B(1,4)=1/4$ for $n=4$ by direct integration—verify/univmath/ch32_check.py). $B(1,n)$ is the particularly simple case “$m=1$” of the beta function, and as such both the direct computation from the definition and the computation via the gamma function can be carried out with equal ease.

← Back to Chapter 32, “The Gamma Function, the Beta Function, and the Laplace Transform”

Chapter 33 Basic Solution Methods for First-Order Ordinary Differential Equations

Exercise 33.1: Separable equation

Solution

The differential equation is $\dfrac{\dd y}{\dd x}=\dfrac{x^2}{y^2}$ ($y\neq0$). The right-hand side is the product of “a function of $x$ alone, $x^2$” and “a function of $y$ alone, $1/y^2$,” so the equation is separable. Multiplying both sides by $y^2$ gives

$$ y^2\,\dd y=x^2\,\dd x $$

Applying $\displaystyle\int$ to both sides gives

$$ \int y^2\,\dd y=\int x^2\,\dd x \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{y^3}{3}=\frac{x^3}{3}+C_1 $$

Multiplying both sides by 3 and letting $3C_1=C$, we obtain

$$ y^3=x^3+C $$

This is the general solution (solving for $y$ gives $y=\sqrt[3]{x^3+C}$). Check: differentiating $y=(x^3+C)^{1/3}$ with respect to $x$ gives $\diff yx=\dfrac13(x^3+C)^{-2/3}\cdot3x^2=\dfrac{x^2}{(x^3+C)^{2/3}}=\dfrac{x^2}{y^2}$. Indeed, the original equation is satisfied.

Exercise 33.2: Homogeneous-type equation

Solution

The differential equation is $\diff yx=\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{xy}$ ($x\gt0,\ y\gt0$). Dividing the numerator and denominator of the right-hand side by $x^2$ gives

$$ \frac{x^2+y^2}{xy}=\frac{1+(y/x)^2}{y/x} $$

so the right-hand side is a function of $y/x$ alone (a homogeneous-type equation). Letting $t=y/x$, that is, $y=xt$, we have $\diff yx=xt'+t$ (the product rule), and the equation becomes

$$ xt'+t=\frac{1+t^2}{t} $$

Subtracting $t$ from the right-hand side gives $\dfrac{1+t^2}{t}-t=\dfrac{1+t^2-t^2}{t}=\dfrac1t$, so

$$ x\diff tx=\frac1t \quad\Longrightarrow\quad t\,\dd t=\frac1x\,\dd x $$

Applying $\displaystyle\int$ to both sides gives

$$ \int t\,\dd t=\int\frac1x\,\dd x \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{t^2}{2}=\ln x+C_1\quad(x\gt0) $$

Multiplying both sides by 2 and letting $2C_1=C$, we obtain $t^2=2\ln x+C$. Substituting back $t=y/x$ gives

$$ \left(\frac yx\right)^{\!2}=2\ln x+C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad y^2=x^2(2\ln x+C) $$

This is the general solution. Check: differentiating both sides of $F(x,y)=y^2-x^2(2\ln x+C)=0$ with respect to $x$ (regarding $y$ as a function of $x$) gives $2y\diff yx-2x(2\ln x+C)-x^2\cdot\dfrac2x=0$, that is, $2y\diff yx=2x(2\ln x+C)+2x=2x\left(2\ln x+C+1\right)$. On the curve we have $2\ln x+C=y^2/x^2$, so $2y\diff yx=2x\left(\dfrac{y^2}{x^2}+1\right)=\dfrac{2(x^2+y^2)}{x}$. Dividing both sides by $2y$ gives $\diff yx=\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{xy}$, which indeed agrees with the original equation (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 33.3: The $f(ax+by+c)$ type

Solution

The differential equation is $\diff yx=\cos(x+y)$. This is the case $a=1,b=1,c=0$, so let $t=x+y$. Then $\diff tx=1+\diff yx$, and since $\diff yx=\cos(x+y)=\cos t$, we have

$$ \diff tx=1+\cos t $$

We reduce this to a separable equation. Using the half-angle formula $1+\cos t=2\cos^2\dfrac t2$ (from high school mathematics; a rearrangement of the double-angle formula $\cos t=2\cos^2\frac t2-1$), we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{2\cos^2(t/2)}\,\dd t=\dd x \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac12\sec^2\!\left(\frac t2\right)\dd t=\dd x $$

Applying $\displaystyle\int$ to both sides, and using $\displaystyle\int\sec^2u\,\dd u=\tan u+C$ (Chapter 3; the inverse operation of differentiating the tangent function) with $u=t/2$, we get

$$ \frac12\int\sec^2\!\left(\frac t2\right)\dd t=\int\dd x \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac12\cdot2\tan\!\left(\frac t2\right)=x+C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \tan\!\left(\frac t2\right)=x+C $$

Substituting back $t=x+y$ gives

$$ \tan\!\left(\frac{x+y}{2}\right)=x+C $$

This is the general solution. Check: differentiating both sides of $\Phi(x,y)=\tan\!\left(\dfrac{x+y}2\right)-x-C=0$ with respect to $x$ gives $\sec^2\!\left(\dfrac{x+y}2\right)\cdot\dfrac{1+y'}{2}-1=0$, that is, $y'=\dfrac{2}{\sec^2\left(\frac{x+y}2\right)}-1=2\cos^2\!\left(\dfrac{x+y}2\right)-1=\cos(x+y)$ (we used the double-angle formula $\cos\theta=2\cos^2\frac\theta2-1$ with $\theta=x+y$). This indeed agrees with the original equation.

Exercise 33.4: First-order linear differential equation

Solution

The differential equation is $\diff yx-y\tan x=\sin x$ ($-\frac\pi2\lt x\lt\frac\pi2$), a first-order linear differential equation with $p(x)=-\tan x$ and $g(x)=\sin x$. Since $\cos x\gt0$ for $-\frac\pi2\lt x\lt\frac\pi2$,

$$ \int p(x)\,\dd x=\int(-\tan x)\,\dd x=\ln(\cos x) $$

(the formula $\displaystyle\int\tan x\,\dd x=-\ln|\cos x|$ is from Chapter 5). Hence $e^{-\int p\,\dd x}=e^{-\ln(\cos x)}=\dfrac1{\cos x}=\sec x$ and $e^{\int p\,\dd x}=e^{\ln(\cos x)}=\cos x$. Substituting into the general-solution formula (Formula 33.2 of this chapter) gives

$$ y=\sec x\left\{\int\sin x\cdot\cos x\,\dd x+C\right\} $$

For the integral on the right-hand side, we use integration by substitution with $u=\sin x$, $\dd u=\cos x\,\dd x$ (covered in high school mathematics), which gives $\displaystyle\int\sin x\cos x\,\dd x=\int u\,\dd u=\dfrac{u^2}{2}=\dfrac{\sin^2x}2$. Hence,

$$ y=\sec x\left\{\frac{\sin^2x}2+C\right\}=\frac12\sin^2x\sec x+C\sec x=\frac12\sin x\cdot\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}+C\sec x=\frac12\sin x\tan x+C\sec x $$

This is the general solution. Check: differentiating $y=\dfrac12\sin x\tan x+C\sec x$ with the product rule gives $y'=\dfrac12\left(\cos x\tan x+\sin x\sec^2x\right)+C\sec x\tan x=\dfrac12\left(\sin x+\sin x\sec^2x\right)+C\sec x\tan x$. On the other hand, $y\tan x+\sin x=\dfrac12\sin x\tan^2x+C\sec x\tan x+\sin x$. Computing the difference of the two, we get $\dfrac12\sin x\sec^2x-\dfrac12\sin x\tan^2x+\dfrac12\sin x-\sin x=\dfrac12\sin x(\sec^2x-\tan^2x)-\dfrac12\sin x=\dfrac12\sin x\cdot1-\dfrac12\sin x=0$ (we used $\sec^2x-\tan^2x=1$). Hence $y'=y\tan x+\sin x$, that is, $y'-y\tan x=\sin x$ holds (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 33.5: Bernoulli's differential equation

Solution

The differential equation is $x\diff yx+y=y^2\ln x$ ($x\gt0$). Dividing both sides by $x$ gives

$$ \diff yx+\frac1x\,y=\frac{\ln x}{x}\,y^2 $$

so this is Bernoulli's differential equation with $p(x)=\dfrac1x$, $g(x)=\dfrac{\ln x}x$, and $n=2$. Let $u=y^{1-n}=y^{-1}$. Following the procedure of Formula 33.3 of this chapter, and setting $P(x)=(1-n)p(x)=(-1)\cdot\dfrac1x=-\dfrac1x$ and $Q(x)=(1-n)g(x)=(-1)\cdot\dfrac{\ln x}x=-\dfrac{\ln x}x$, we obtain the first-order linear differential equation for $u$

$$ u'-\frac1x\,u=-\frac{\ln x}{x} $$

We use Formula 33.2. Since $\displaystyle\int\left(-\frac1x\right)\dd x=-\ln x$ ($x\gt0$), we have $e^{-\int P\,\dd x}=e^{\ln x}=x$ and $e^{\int P\,\dd x}=e^{-\ln x}=\dfrac1x$. Hence,

$$ u=x\left\{\int\left(-\frac{\ln x}{x}\right)\cdot\frac1x\,\dd x+C\right\}=x\left\{-\int\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\,\dd x+C\right\} $$

We compute the integral on the right-hand side by integration by parts. Letting $f=\ln x$ and $\dd G=x^{-2}\dd x$, we have $\dd f=\dfrac1x\dd x$ and $G=-\dfrac1x$, so

$$ \int\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\,\dd x=\ln x\cdot\left(-\frac1x\right)-\int\left(-\frac1x\right)\cdot\frac1x\,\dd x=-\frac{\ln x}x+\int\frac1{x^2}\,\dd x=-\frac{\ln x}x-\frac1x+C_1 $$

Therefore $-\displaystyle\int\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\dd x=\dfrac{\ln x}x+\dfrac1x-C_1$, and substituting this into the above gives

$$ u=x\left\{\frac{\ln x}x+\frac1x+C\right\}=\ln x+1+Cx $$

(we have renamed $-C_1+C$ as the arbitrary constant $C$). Since $u=y^{-1}=1/y$,

$$ y=\frac{1}{\ln x+1+Cx} $$

This is the general solution (besides this, $y\equiv0$ is also a solution). Check: letting $u=\ln x+1+Cx$, we have $y=1/u$ and $\diff yx=-\dfrac{u'}{u^2}$. Since $u'=\dfrac1x+C$, we get $\diff yx=-\dfrac{1/x+C}{u^2}$. On the other hand,

$$ -\frac1x\,y+\frac{\ln x}xy^2=\frac1{u^2}\left(-\frac ux+\frac{\ln x}x\right)=\frac1{u^2}\cdot\frac{-(\ln x+1+Cx)+\ln x}{x}=\frac1{u^2}\cdot\frac{-1-Cx}{x}=-\frac{1/x+C}{u^2} $$

Thus $\diff yx=-\dfrac1xy+\dfrac{\ln x}xy^2$, that is, $x\diff yx+y=y^2\ln x$, holds (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 33.6: Riccati's differential equation

Solution

The differential equation is $\diff yx-y^2+2xy-(x^2+1)=0$. Matching it to the form $y'+P(x)y^2+Q(x)y+R(x)=0$ of the definition (Definition 33.9 of this chapter), we have $P(x)=-1,\ Q(x)=2x,\ R(x)=-(x^2+1)$. First we confirm that $y_1=x$ is a particular solution. Since $y_1'=1$ and $y_1^2=x^2$,

$$ y_1'+Py_1^2+Qy_1+R=1+(-1)x^2+2x\cdot x-(x^2+1)=1-x^2+2x^2-x^2-1=(1-1)+(-x^2+2x^2-x^2)=0 $$

Indeed, it is a particular solution. Next we compute $A(x)=2P(x)y_1(x)+Q(x)$.

$$ A(x)=2\cdot(-1)\cdot x+2x=-2x+2x=0 $$

Since $A(x)\equiv0$, Formula 33.4 of this chapter simplifies. From $\displaystyle\int A\,\dd x=0$ we get $e^{\int A\dd x}=e^{-\int A\dd x}=1$, so

$$ y=y_1+\left[1\cdot\left\{\int P(x)\cdot1\,\dd x+C_1\right\}\right]^{-1}=x+\left\{\int(-1)\,\dd x+C_1\right\}^{-1}=x+(-x+C_1)^{-1} $$

Since $-x+C_1=-(x-C_1)$, we have $(-x+C_1)^{-1}=-\dfrac1{x-C_1}$. Rewriting $C_1$ as $C$,

$$ y=x-\frac1{x-C} $$

This is the general solution ($C$ is an arbitrary constant. Renaming $-C$ as $C$ again, it can also be written as $y=x-\dfrac1{x+C}$). Check: letting $y=x-\dfrac1{x-C}$, we have $y'=1+\dfrac1{(x-C)^2}$. On the other hand,

$$ y^2-2xy+(x^2+1)=\left(x-\frac1{x-C}\right)^{\!2}-2x\left(x-\frac1{x-C}\right)+(x^2+1) $$

Expanding this, we get $x^2-\dfrac{2x}{x-C}+\dfrac1{(x-C)^2}-2x^2+\dfrac{2x}{x-C}+x^2+1=\dfrac1{(x-C)^2}+1$. Hence,

$$ y'-\bigl\{y^2-2xy+(x^2+1)\bigr\}=\left(1+\frac1{(x-C)^2}\right)-\left(\frac1{(x-C)^2}+1\right)=0 $$

that is, $y'-y^2+2xy-(x^2+1)=0$ holds (verified with SymPy).

← Back to Chapter 33, “Basic Solution Methods for First-Order Ordinary Differential Equations”

Chapter 34 Exact Differential Equations, Integrating Factors, and Singular Solutions

Exercise 34.1: Family of orthogonal curves for $x^2+y^2=2Cx$

Solution

Differentiating both sides of the family of curves $x^2+y^2=2Cx$ with respect to $x$ gives

$$ 2x+2yy'=2C $$

Dividing both sides by $2$ gives $x+yy'=C$. We eliminate $C$ by substituting this into the original equation $x^2+y^2=2Cx$. Since the original equation gives $C=\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{2x}$,

$$ x+yy'=\frac{x^2+y^2}{2x}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad yy'=\frac{x^2+y^2}{2x}-x=\frac{x^2+y^2-2x^2}{2x}=\frac{y^2-x^2}{2x} $$ $$ \therefore\ y'=\frac{y^2-x^2}{2xy} $$

This is the differential equation satisfied by the family of curves $x^2+y^2=2Cx$. To find the family of orthogonal curves, we replace $y'$ with $-1/y'$.

$$ -\frac1{y'}=\frac{y^2-x^2}{2xy}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{2xy}{x^2-y^2} $$

Dividing the numerator and the denominator of the right-hand side by $x^2$ gives $\dfrac{2(y/x)}{1-(y/x)^2}$, an expression in $y/x$ alone—that is, the equation is of homogeneous type (Chapter 33 of the main text). Letting $v=y/x$ ($y=vx$), we have $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=v+x\dfrac{dv}{dx}$, so

$$ v+x\frac{dv}{dx}=\frac{2v}{1-v^2}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x\frac{dv}{dx}=\frac{2v}{1-v^2}-v=\frac{2v-v(1-v^2)}{1-v^2}=\frac{v+v^3}{1-v^2}=\frac{v(1+v^2)}{1-v^2} $$

Separating variables, we obtain

$$ \frac{1-v^2}{v(1+v^2)}\,dv=\frac{dx}{x} $$

We decompose the left-hand side into partial fractions. Setting $\dfrac{1-v^2}{v(1+v^2)}=\dfrac{A}{v}+\dfrac{Bv+D}{1+v^2}$ and putting everything over a common denominator, we get $1-v^2=A(1+v^2)+(Bv+D)v$. Comparing the coefficients of $v^0$ gives $A=1$; comparing the coefficients of $v^2$ gives $A+B=-1$, so $B=-2$; and comparing the coefficients of $v^1$ gives $D=0$. Hence,

$$ \frac{1-v^2}{v(1+v^2)}=\frac1v-\frac{2v}{1+v^2} $$

(The same decomposition was also verified with SymPy's apart—verify/univmath/ch34_check.py.) Integrating both sides gives

$$ \int\left(\frac1v-\frac{2v}{1+v^2}\right)dv=\int\frac{dx}{x}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \ln|v|-\ln(1+v^2)=\ln|x|+K_0 $$

(Since the numerator of $\dfrac{2v}{1+v^2}$ is the derivative of the denominator, its integral is $\ln(1+v^2)$.) Combining the left-hand side by the properties of logarithms gives $\ln\left|\dfrac{v}{1+v^2}\right|=\ln|x|+K_0$, and exponentiating both sides gives

$$ \frac{v}{1+v^2}=Kx\qquad(K=\pm e^{K_0}) $$

Substituting back $v=y/x$, we obtain

$$ \frac{y/x}{1+y^2/x^2}=Kx\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{xy}{x^2+y^2}=Kx\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{y}{x^2+y^2}=K\quad(x\neq0\ \text{since we divided by}\ x\ \text{on both sides}) $$

Therefore $x^2+y^2=\dfrac{y}{K}$. Rewriting $\dfrac1K$ as $2K'$ (a constant), the family of orthogonal curves we seek is

$$ x^2+y^2=2K'y $$

Here $K'$ is an arbitrary constant. Whereas the original family of curves $x^2+y^2=2Cx$ was “the family of circles with centers on the $x$-axis that are tangent to the $y$-axis at the origin,” the family of orthogonal curves $x^2+y^2=2K'y$ is “the family of circles with centers on the $y$-axis that are tangent to the $x$-axis at the origin”—the roles of $x$ and $y$ have been exactly interchanged, a beautiful symmetry. (We verified with SymPy, by implicit differentiation, that $x^2+y^2=2K'y$ indeed satisfies $dy/dx=2xy/(x^2-y^2)$—verify/univmath/ch34_check.py.)

Exercise 34.2: Exact differential equation $(2xy+y^2+1)dx+(x^2+2xy)dy=0$

Solution

Let $P=2xy+y^2+1$ and $Q=x^2+2xy$.

$$ P_y=2x+2y,\qquad Q_x=2x+2y $$

Since $P_y=Q_x$ holds, the equation is an exact differential equation. We solve it by the undetermined-function method. Integrating $P$ with respect to $x$ (regarding $y$ as a constant) gives

$$ f(x,y)=\int(2xy+y^2+1)\,dx+g(y)=x^2y+xy^2+x+g(y) $$

(The integral of $2xy$ is $x^2y$, the integral of $y^2$ (treated as a constant) is $xy^2$, and the integral of $1$ is $x$.) Differentiating this partially with respect to $y$ gives

$$ f_y=x^2+2xy+g'(y) $$

This must equal $Q=x^2+2xy$, so $g'(y)=0$; that is, $g(y)$ is a constant (we may take it to be $0$). Therefore the general solution is

$$ x^2y+xy^2+x=C $$

As a check, differentiating $f=x^2y+xy^2+x$ partially with respect to $x$ gives $f_x=2xy+y^2+1=P$, and differentiating partially with respect to $y$ gives $f_y=x^2+2xy=Q$; both agree (verified with SymPy—verify/univmath/ch34_check.py).

Exercise 34.3: Integrating factor for $y\,dx+(2x+y^3)dy=0$

Solution

Letting $P=y$ and $Q=2x+y^3$, we have

$$ P_y=1,\qquad Q_x=2 $$

Since $P_y\neq Q_x$, the equation is not exact as it stands. So, to try an integrating factor of the form $\mu(x)$, we compute $\dfrac{P_y-Q_x}{Q}=\dfrac{1-2}{2x+y^3}=\dfrac{-1}{2x+y^3}$, but this is not a function of $x$ alone ($y$ remains). We therefore try the form $\mu(y)$.

$$ h(y)=\frac{P_y-Q_x}{P}=\frac{1-2}{y}=-\frac1y $$

This time $x$ has disappeared, and we have a function of $y$ alone. Hence an integrating factor of the form $\mu(y)$ exists, and by the formula $\mu=e^{-\int h(y)\,dy}$ in the main text,

$$ \mu=e^{-\int\left(-\frac1y\right)dy}=e^{\int\frac1y\,dy}=e^{\ln|y|}=|y| $$

(If we work in the range $y>0$, then $\mu=y$.) Multiplying both sides by $\mu=y$ gives

$$ y^2\,dx+(2xy+y^4)\,dy=0 $$

Indeed, $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y}(y^2)=2y$ and $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial x}(2xy+y^4)=2y$, so $P_y=Q_x$ has been restored (verified with SymPy). Solving by the undetermined-function method, we integrate $\mu P=y^2$ with respect to $x$ to get

$$ f(x,y)=xy^2+g(y) $$

Differentiating partially with respect to $y$ gives $f_y=2xy+g'(y)$, which must equal $\mu Q=2xy+y^4$, so $g'(y)=y^4$ and hence $g(y)=\dfrac{y^5}5$. Therefore the general solution is

$$ xy^2+\frac{y^5}5=C $$

Multiplying both sides by $5$ to clear the fraction, we can also write it as $5xy^2+y^5=C'$ ($C'=5C$). As a check, differentiating $f=xy^2+y^5/5$ gives $f_x=y^2=\mu P$ and $f_y=2xy+y^4=\mu Q$; both agree (verified with SymPy—verify/univmath/ch34_check.py).

Exercise 34.4: Clairaut's differential equation $y=xy'-\dfrac14(y')^2$

Solution

Let $p=y'$ and $f(p)=-\dfrac14p^2$. Following the procedure in the main text, we substitute into $y=Cx+f(C)$ to find that the general solution is

$$ y=Cx-\frac14C^2 $$

Next we find the singular solution. Since $f'(p)=-\dfrac12p$, the equation $x+f'(p)=0$ becomes

$$ x-\frac12p=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad p=2x $$

Substituting this into $y=xp-\dfrac14p^2$ gives

$$ y=x(2x)-\frac14(2x)^2=2x^2-\frac14\cdot4x^2=2x^2-x^2=x^2 $$

Therefore the singular solution is

$$ y=x^2 $$

which is a parabola. Let us use the discriminant to confirm that this parabola is tangent to all of the lines $y=Cx-\dfrac14C^2$ of the general solution. The $x$-coordinates of the intersection points of a line and the parabola are the solutions of the equation obtained by substituting $y=Cx-\dfrac14C^2$ into $y=x^2$,

$$ x^2=Cx-\frac14C^2\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x^2-Cx+\frac14C^2=0 $$

that is, of a quadratic equation in $x$. The left-hand side has exactly the form of the perfect square $\left(x-\dfrac{C}2\right)^2=x^2-Cx+\dfrac{C^2}4$, so the discriminant is

$$ D=(-C)^2-4\cdot1\cdot\frac{C^2}4=C^2-C^2=0 $$

A discriminant of $0$ means that the line $y=Cx-\frac14C^2$ and the parabola $y=x^2$ have exactly one point in common, a repeated root at $x=\dfrac{C}2$—that is, they are tangent. Since this holds for every $C$, the parabola $y=x^2$ is indeed an envelope tangent to all of the lines of the general solution (we verified with SymPy that substituting $p=2x$ into $x-p/2=0$ gives $0$, and that the discriminant is identically $0$—verify/univmath/ch34_check2.py).

Exercise 34.5: Lagrange's differential equation $y=x(y'+1)+(y')^2$

Solution

Let $p=y'$, $f(p)=p+1$, and $g(p)=p^2$ (the condition $f(p)\neq p$ is satisfied). Then $f'(p)=1$, $g'(p)=2p$, and $p-f(p)=p-(p+1)=-1$. Hence,

$$ P_0(p)=-\frac{f'(p)}{p-f(p)}=-\frac{1}{-1}=1,\qquad Q_0(p)=\frac{g'(p)}{p-f(p)}=\frac{2p}{-1}=-2p $$

The reduced equation in the main text was $\dfrac{dx}{dp}+P_0(p)x=Q_0(p)$, so here we have

$$ \frac{dx}{dp}+x=-2p $$

which is a first-order linear differential equation for $x=x(p)$. The integrating factor is $e^{\int1\,dp}=e^p$, so multiplying both sides by $e^p$ gives

$$ \frac{d}{dp}\big(xe^p\big)=e^p\frac{dx}{dp}+xe^p=-2pe^p $$

We integrate the right-hand side with respect to $p$. Since $\displaystyle\int pe^p\,dp=pe^p-e^p$ (by integration by parts: with $u=p,\ dv=e^p dp$, we have $\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du=pe^p-\int e^p dp=pe^p-e^p$),

$$ \int(-2p)e^p\,dp=-2(pe^p-e^p)=-2pe^p+2e^p $$

Therefore,

$$ xe^p=-2pe^p+2e^p+C\qquad\therefore\ x=-2p+2+Ce^{-p} $$

We substitute this into $y=x(p+1)+p^2$.

$$ y=\big(-2p+2+Ce^{-p}\big)(p+1)+p^2 $$

First we compute $(-2p+2)(p+1)=-2p^2-2p+2p+2=-2p^2+2$, and adding the remaining terms gives

$$ y=(-2p^2+2)+C(p+1)e^{-p}+p^2=-p^2+2+C(p+1)e^{-p} $$

Therefore the general solution can be expressed, using the parameter $p$, as

$$ x=-2p+2+Ce^{-p},\qquad y=-p^2+2+C(p+1)e^{-p} $$

(We verified with SymPy that $dy/dp=p\cdot dx/dp$ indeed holds, and that in the numerical examples $p=0.5,1.0,2.0$ the derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ (computed by numerical differentiation) agrees exactly with $p$—verify/univmath/ch34_check3_lagrange_fix.py.)

Exercise 34.6: Numerical computation with the logistic equation

Solution

We substitute the carrying capacity $N_\infty=10000$, $k_0=0.3$, and the initial population $N(0)=100$ into the solution formula for the logistic equation in the main text,

$$ N(t)=\frac{N_\infty}{1+\left(\dfrac{N_\infty}{N_0}-1\right)e^{-k_0t}} $$

Since $\dfrac{N_\infty}{N_0}-1=\dfrac{10000}{100}-1=100-1=99$, we obtain

$$ N(t)=\frac{10000}{1+99e^{-0.3t}} $$

(1) We substitute $t=5$. Since $e^{-0.3\times5}=e^{-1.5}\approx0.223130$,

$$ N(5)=\frac{10000}{1+99\times0.223130}\approx\frac{10000}{1+22.0899}\approx\frac{10000}{23.0899}\approx433.1 $$

That is, at $t=5$ the population is about $433$ individuals.

(2) We find $t$ such that $N(t)=9000$.

$$ 9000=\frac{10000}{1+99e^{-0.3t}}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 1+99e^{-0.3t}=\frac{10000}{9000}=\frac{10}9 $$ $$ 99e^{-0.3t}=\frac{10}9-1=\frac19\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad e^{-0.3t}=\frac1{9\times99}=\frac1{891}\approx0.0011223 $$

Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives $-0.3t=\ln\!\left(\dfrac1{891}\right)=-\ln(891)\approx-6.7923$, so

$$ t=\frac{6.7923}{0.3}\approx22.64 $$

That is, the population reaches $9000$ at $t\approx22.64$ (the unit of time depends on how the original problem is set up). (Verified numerically with Python: $N(5)=433.090\cdots$; the value $t=22.641\cdots$ is the one for which $N(t)=9000$; and putting this back into the formula, we indeed get $N(22.641\cdots)=9000.0$—verify/univmath/ch34_final_verify.py.)

← Back to Chapter 34, “Exact Differential Equations, Integrating Factors, and Singular Solutions”

Chapter 35 Fundamentals of Second-Order Linear Homogeneous Differential Equations

Exercise 35.1: The case $D=0$ (repeated root)

Solution

The coefficients of $y''+6y'+9y=0$ are $a=6,b=9$, so the characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2+6\lambda+9=0 $$

The left-hand side factors as $(\lambda+3)^2$ (since $\lambda^2+6\lambda+9=\lambda^2+2\cdot3\cdot\lambda+3^2$ has exactly the form of the perfect-square formula already covered in high school), so

$$ (\lambda+3)^2=0 \qquad\therefore\ \lambda_0=-3\ (\text{repeated root}) $$

Checking with the discriminant, we find $D=6^2-4\times9=36-36=0$, so this is indeed case [ii] of Section 35.5 (repeated root). By Formula 35.2 (Section 35.5.2), the general solution is

$$ Y=C_1e^{-3x}+C_2xe^{-3x} $$

As a check, let us verify this by direct substitution. For $y_1=e^{-3x}$ we have $y_1'=-3e^{-3x}$ and $y_1''=9e^{-3x}$, so $y_1''+6y_1'+9y_1=9e^{-3x}+6\times(-3e^{-3x})+9e^{-3x}=(9-18+9)e^{-3x}=0$. For $y_2=xe^{-3x}$, the product rule gives $y_2'=e^{-3x}+x\times(-3)e^{-3x}=(1-3x)e^{-3x}$ and $y_2''=-3e^{-3x}+(1-3x)\times(-3)e^{-3x}=(-3-3+9x)e^{-3x}=(9x-6)e^{-3x}$, so

$$ y_2''+6y_2'+9y_2=(9x-6)e^{-3x}+6(1-3x)e^{-3x}+9xe^{-3x}=\bigl\{(9x-6)+(6-18x)+9x\bigr\}e^{-3x}=0\cdot e^{-3x}=0 $$

Thus both functions are confirmed to be solutions (the coefficient of $x e^{-3x}$ simplifies to $9-18+9=0$, and the coefficient of $e^{-3x}$ simplifies to $-6+6=0$). Hence the general solution is $Y=C_1e^{-3x}+C_2xe^{-3x}=(C_1+C_2x)e^{-3x}$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 35.2: The case $D\lt0$ (complex roots)

Solution

The coefficients of $y''-2y'+10y=0$ are $a=-2,b=10$, so the characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2-2\lambda+10=0 $$

The discriminant is $D=(-2)^2-4\times10=4-40=-36\lt0$, so this is case [iii] of Section 35.5 (complex roots). By the quadratic formula,

$$ \lambda=\frac{2\pm\sqrt{-36}}{2}=\frac{2\pm6i}{2}=1\pm3i $$

so $\alpha=1,\beta=3$. By Formula 35.3 (Section 35.5.3), the general solution is

$$ Y=e^{x}(C_1\cos3x+C_2\sin3x) $$

As a check, substitute $y=e^{x}\cos3x$ directly. By the product rule,

$$ y'=e^{x}\cos3x-3e^{x}\sin3x,\qquad y''=(e^{x}\cos3x-3e^{x}\sin3x)-3(e^{x}\sin3x+3e^{x}\cos3x)=(e^x\cos3x-9e^x\cos3x)+(-3e^x\sin3x-3e^x\sin3x)=-8e^x\cos3x-6e^x\sin3x $$

(We differentiated the first derivative once more with the product rule and collected the terms in $\cos3x,\sin3x$ separately.) Therefore,

$$ y''-2y'+10y=(-8e^x\cos3x-6e^x\sin3x)-2(e^x\cos3x-3e^x\sin3x)+10e^x\cos3x=(-8-2+10)e^x\cos3x+(-6+6)e^x\sin3x=0 $$

so it is indeed a solution. In the same way, one can confirm that $y=e^{x}\sin3x$ also gives 0 (both were verified with SymPy). Hence the general solution is $Y=e^{x}(C_1\cos3x+C_2\sin3x)$.

Exercise 35.3: The equation of simple harmonic motion

Solution

The coefficients of $y''+\omega^2y=0$ ($\omega>0$) are $a=0,b=\omega^2$, so the characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2+\omega^2=0 \qquad\therefore\ \lambda^2=-\omega^2 $$

The discriminant is $D=0^2-4\omega^2=-4\omega^2$, and since $\omega>0$ (hence $\omega^2>0$) we have $D\lt0$, so this is case [iii] of Section 35.5 (complex roots). Solving $\lambda^2=-\omega^2$ gives $\lambda=\pm\sqrt{-\omega^2}=\pm\omega i$, so $\alpha=0,\beta=\omega$ (comparing with the form $\lambda=\alpha\pm\beta i$, the real part is $0$ and the imaginary part is $\pm\omega$). By Formula 35.3, the general solution is

$$ Y=e^{0\cdot x}(C_1\cos\omega x+C_2\sin\omega x)=C_1\cos\omega x+C_2\sin\omega x $$

(since $e^{0\cdot x}=1$, that factor disappears). This is a generalization of Example 35.9 (the special case $\omega=2$).

Regarding the physical meaning, as stated in the “Why?” at the end of Section 35.5.3, in the general solution $e^{\alpha x}(C_1\cos\beta x+C_2\sin\beta x)$ the quantity $\alpha$ expresses the degree to which the amplitude increases or decreases (damping and amplification), and $\beta$ expresses the speed of oscillation (the angular frequency). In this problem $\alpha=0$, so $e^{\alpha x}=e^{0}=1$, and the coefficient that represents the amplitude does not change at all—that is, even as time passes (if we reinterpret the variable $x$ as the time $t$), the amplitude neither grows nor shrinks, and the oscillation continues with constant magnitude. This corresponds to an idealized situation in which no effect that removes energy, such as friction or air resistance, acts at all, and it agrees with the statement made in the discussion of the physical meaning in Section 35.5.3 that “$\alpha=0$ corresponds to the ideal, frictionless ‘simple harmonic motion.’”

Exercise 35.4: The Wronskian of three solutions

Solution

By the definition of the hyperbolic functions (Chapter 2, Section 2.5, Definition 2.2), $\cosh x=\dfrac{e^x+e^{-x}}{2}$. Differentiating this twice gives

$$ (\cosh x)'=\frac{e^x-e^{-x}}{2}=\sinh x,\qquad (\cosh x)''=\frac{e^x+e^{-x}}{2}=\cosh x $$

(We used the fact that each time $e^{-x}$ is differentiated, the chain rule flips the sign.) Hence for $y_3=\cosh x$ we get $y_3''-y_3=\cosh x-\cosh x=0$, so $y_3$ is a solution of $y''-y=0$. That $y_1=e^x,y_2=e^{-x}$ are solutions was already shown in the check of Example 35.1 in this chapter (main text, Section 35.1.2). Therefore $y_1,y_2,y_3$ are all solutions of $y''-y=0$.

Next, we compute the $3\times3$ Wronskian (see the proof of Theorem 35.3).

$$ y_1=e^x,\ y_1'=e^x,\ y_1''=e^x,\qquad y_2=e^{-x},\ y_2'=-e^{-x},\ y_2''=e^{-x},\qquad y_3=\cosh x,\ y_3'=\sinh x,\ y_3''=\cosh x $$ $$ W(y_1,y_2,y_3)= \begin{vmatrix} e^x & e^{-x} & \cosh x\\ e^x & -e^{-x} & \sinh x\\ e^x & e^{-x} & \cosh x \end{vmatrix} $$

If we notice that the first and third rows of this determinant are exactly the same (both are $(e^x,\,e^{-x},\,\cosh x)$), we can see that it is 0 without computing anything (a property of determinants—a determinant with two identical rows is 0. This can also be derived from property (ii) in Section 8.5 of Chapter 8, “interchanging two rows reverses the sign”: interchanging two identical rows should not change the determinant, yet by that property the sign must flip, so $W=-W$, that is, $W=0$). Computing it explicitly by the cofactor expansion of Section 8.4 of Chapter 8 also gives

$$ W(y_1,y_2,y_3)=0 $$

(verified with SymPy).

This result is a concrete instance of Theorem 35.3 (a second-order linear homogeneous differential equation has exactly two linearly independent solutions, and no collection of three or more solutions can be linearly independent). In the proof of Theorem 35.3, using the fact that $y_1,y_2,y_3$ are solutions of the same equation ①, we showed by an elementary row operation on the determinant (adding constant multiples of the other rows to the third row) that the third row becomes all zeros—this is a general argument that holds for any three solutions. The present problem is a concrete instance of that general argument; moreover, since $y_3=\cosh x=\frac12y_1+\frac12y_2$ is already written as a linear combination of $y_1,y_2$ (that is, $y_1,y_2,y_3$ are linearly dependent from the start), the same conclusion is also obtained from the viewpoint of the columns of the determinant (if some column can be written as a linear combination of the other columns, the determinant is 0; Section 8.5 of Chapter 8). Both viewpoints agree in showing that $y_1,y_2,y_3$ cannot have “three independent directions.”

Exercise 35.5: Directly checking linear dependence

Solution

The definition of linear independence (the beginning of Section 35.2) was this: “When the only pair of constants for which $C_1y_1+C_2y_2=0$ (identically zero for all $x$) is $C_1=C_2=0$, the functions are said to be linearly independent.” For $y_1=x,\ y_2=3x$, the relation $y_2=3y_1$ holds. So take $C_1=-3,\ C_2=1$ (a pair that is not $0$); then

$$ C_1y_1+C_2y_2=(-3)\times x+1\times(3x)=-3x+3x=0 $$

which is identically 0 for all $x$. Since this holds for a pair other than $C_1=C_2=0$, by the definition $y_1,y_2$ are immediately seen to be linearly dependent.

Next we compute the Wronskian. Since $y_1'=1,\ y_2'=3$,

$$ W(y_1,y_2)=\begin{vmatrix}x & 3x\\ 1 & 3\end{vmatrix}=x\times3-3x\times1=3x-3x=0 $$

(the same procedure as in Example 35.3). Definition 35.2 asserts only that “if $W\neq0$, then the solutions are linearly independent,” so we cannot immediately conclude linear dependence from this result alone; but, as directly verified above, they are in fact linearly dependent, and the result $W=0$ is not in the least inconsistent with this fact (for linearly dependent solutions, it is generally known that the Wronskian is identically 0, as mentioned in the Math Note at the end of Section 35.2—Abel's formula. The present problem is a concrete example of this as well). (Verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 35.6: An initial value problem using Theorem 35.1

Solution

(a) The right-hand side of $y''-y=6$ is the constant $6$, so, as in Example 35.4, we guess that a particular solution $y_0$ is also a constant. Setting $y_0=k$ (a constant), we have $y_0'=0,\ y_0''=0$, so substituting into the equation gives

$$ y_0''-y_0=0-k=-k $$

For this to equal $6$ we need $-k=6$, that is, $k=-6$. Hence $y_0=-6$ is one particular solution. As shown in Example 35.1 (Section 35.1.2), the general solution of the corresponding homogeneous equation $y''-y=0$ was $Y=C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x}$. By Theorem 35.1 ($y=y_0+Y$), the required general solution is

$$ y=y_0+Y=-6+C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x} $$

Cross-check: since $y'=C_1e^x-C_2e^{-x}$ and $y''=C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x}$, we get $y''-y=(C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x})-(-6+C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x})=6$, so it indeed satisfies $y''-y=6$.

(b) Apply the initial conditions to the general solution in (a). First, using $y(0)=4$,

$$ y(0)=-6+C_1e^{0}+C_2e^{0}=-6+C_1+C_2=4 \qquad\therefore\ C_1+C_2=10 \quad\cdots(\ast) $$

Next, using $y'(0)=0$ in $y'=C_1e^x-C_2e^{-x}$,

$$ y'(0)=C_1e^{0}-C_2e^{0}=C_1-C_2=0 \qquad\therefore\ C_1=C_2 \quad\cdots(\ast\ast) $$

Solving $(\ast)$ and $(\ast\ast)$ simultaneously, we substitute $C_1=C_2$ into $(\ast)$ to get $2C_1=10$, so $C_1=C_2=5$. Therefore the solution of this initial value problem is

$$ y=-6+5e^x+5e^{-x} $$

Using the definition of the hyperbolic functions (Chapter 2, Section 2.5) $\cosh x=\dfrac{e^x+e^{-x}}{2}$, we can combine the terms as $5e^x+5e^{-x}=10\times\dfrac{e^x+e^{-x}}{2}=10\cosh x$, so the solution can also be written in the form

$$ y=-6+10\cosh x $$

(we verified that this agrees with the result of solving the initial value problem with SymPy's $\texttt{dsolve}$).

← Back to Chapter 35, “Fundamentals of Second-Order Linear Homogeneous Differential Equations”

Chapter 36 Second-Order Linear Nonhomogeneous Differential Equations and Euler's Equation

Exercise 36.1: Variation of parameters when resonance occurs ($y''+4y=8\sin(2x)$)

Solution

The characteristic equation of the homogeneous equation $y''+4y=0$ is $\lambda^2+4=0$, so $\lambda=\pm2i$ ($\alpha=0,\beta=2$), and hence the fundamental solutions are $y_1=\cos(2x),\ y_2=\sin(2x)$. The Wronskian is

$$ W(y_1,y_2)=y_1y_2'-y_2y_1'=\cos(2x)\cdot2\cos(2x)-\sin(2x)\cdot\big(-2\sin(2x)\big)=2\cos^2(2x)+2\sin^2(2x)=2 $$

(Since it is not $0$, they are indeed linearly independent.) Using $R(x)=8\sin(2x)$, we compute the $C_1'(x),C_2'(x)$ of Formula 36.1.

$$ C_1'(x)=-\frac{y_2R(x)}{W}=-\frac{\sin(2x)\cdot8\sin(2x)}{2}=-4\sin^2(2x)=-4\cdot\frac{1-\cos(4x)}{2}=-2+2\cos(4x) $$

(We used the half-angle formula $\sin^2\theta=\dfrac{1-\cos2\theta}{2}$ with $\theta=2x$.) Integrating, we obtain

$$ C_1(x)=\int\big(-2+2\cos(4x)\big)\dd x=-2x+\frac12\sin(4x) $$

Next,

$$ C_2'(x)=\frac{y_1R(x)}{W}=\frac{\cos(2x)\cdot8\sin(2x)}{2}=4\sin(2x)\cos(2x)=2\sin(4x) $$

(We used the double-angle formula $\sin(4x)=2\sin(2x)\cos(2x)$.) Integrating, we obtain

$$ C_2(x)=\int2\sin(4x)\,\dd x=-\frac12\cos(4x) $$

Substituting into Formula 36.1 (the variation-of-parameters formula), we get

$$ y_0=C_1(x)y_1+C_2(x)y_2=\left(-2x+\frac12\sin(4x)\right)\cos(2x)-\frac12\cos(4x)\sin(2x) $$ $$ =-2x\cos(2x)+\frac12\big(\sin(4x)\cos(2x)-\cos(4x)\sin(2x)\big)=-2x\cos(2x)+\frac12\sin(4x-2x)=-2x\cos(2x)+\frac12\sin(2x) $$

(We used the addition formula in reverse, $\sin A\cos B-\cos A\sin B=\sin(A-B)$, with $A=4x,B=2x$.) The term $\dfrac12\sin(2x)$ that appears at the end is a constant multiple of the fundamental solution $y_2=\sin(2x)$ of the homogeneous equation, so it can be absorbed into the arbitrary constant $C_2$ of the general solution. Therefore we may adopt $y_0=-2x\cos(2x)$ as a particular solution. Checking by direct substitution, for $y_p=-2x\cos(2x)$ we have

$$ y_p'=-2\cos(2x)+4x\sin(2x), \qquad y_p''=8\sin(2x)+8x\cos(2x) $$ $$ y_p''+4y_p=8\sin(2x)+8x\cos(2x)+4\cdot(-2x\cos(2x))=8\sin(2x)+8x\cos(2x)-8x\cos(2x)=8\sin(2x) $$

This indeed agrees with the right-hand side $8\sin(2x)$. Therefore the general solution is

$$ y=-2x\cos(2x)+C_1\cos(2x)+C_2\sin(2x) $$

(Verified with SymPy.) The term with an extra factor of $x$ appears in the particular solution because the angular frequency of the external force $8\sin(2x)$ happens to coincide exactly with the angular frequency of the original free oscillation ($\omega=2$) (resonance). In this case the amplitude grows without bound as time passes—this is the same mathematical mechanism as the phenomenon in which a swing's amplitude grows larger and larger when you keep pushing it at just the right timing.

Exercise 36.2: Reduction of order ($xy''-(x+1)y'+y=x^2e^x$, with only $y_1=e^x$ known)

Solution

Dividing both sides by $x$ ($\neq0$) to put the equation into the form $y''+P(x)y'+Q(x)y=R(x)$ that we have been working with in this chapter, we obtain

$$ y''-\left(1+\frac1x\right)y'+\frac1xy=xe^x, \qquad P(x)=-\left(1+\frac1x\right),\ \ Q(x)=\frac1x,\ \ R(x)=xe^x $$

Since $y_1=e^x$ ($y_1'=e^x$), computing the coefficients in Theorem 36.1 gives

$$ A(x)=\frac{2y_1'}{y_1}+P(x)=2+\left(-1-\frac1x\right)=1-\frac1x=\frac{x-1}{x}, \qquad B(x)=\frac{R(x)}{y_1}=\frac{xe^x}{e^x}=x $$

Letting $p=u'$, the equation for $u$ becomes the first-order linear equation

$$ p'+\left(1-\frac1x\right)p=x $$

The integrating factor is $\mu(x)=e^{\int(1-\frac1x)\dd x}=e^{x-\ln x}=\dfrac{e^x}{x}$ (Chapter 33). Multiplying both sides by $\mu(x)$, we get

$$ \left(p\cdot\frac{e^x}{x}\right)'=x\cdot\frac{e^x}{x}=e^x $$

Integrating both sides, we get $p\cdot\dfrac{e^x}{x}=e^x+C_1$, that is,

$$ p(x)=u'(x)=\left(e^x+C_1\right)\cdot\frac{x}{e^x}=x+C_1xe^{-x} $$

Integrating once more with respect to $x$ and using $\displaystyle\int x\,\dd x=\frac{x^2}2$ and $\displaystyle\int xe^{-x}\dd x=-(x+1)e^{-x}$ (integration by parts), we get

$$ u(x)=\frac{x^2}2-C_1(x+1)e^{-x}+C_2 $$

Substituting this into $y=u(x)y_1=u(x)e^x$, we obtain

$$ y=\left(\frac{x^2}2-C_1(x+1)e^{-x}+C_2\right)e^x=\frac{x^2}2e^x-C_1(x+1)+C_2e^x $$

Renaming the constants as $C_1\to-C_2,\ C_2\to C_1$, the general solution is

$$ y=\frac{x^2}2e^x+C_1e^x+C_2(x+1) $$

As a check, substituting the particular solution $y_p=\dfrac{x^2}2e^x$ directly into the equation, we have $y_p'=xe^x+\dfrac{x^2}2e^x$ and $y_p''=e^x+2xe^x+\dfrac{x^2}2e^x$, so

$$ xy_p''-(x+1)y_p'+y_p=x\left(e^x+2xe^x+\frac{x^2}2e^x\right)-(x+1)\left(xe^x+\frac{x^2}2e^x\right)+\frac{x^2}2e^x $$

Simplifying (verified with SymPy), this becomes $x^2e^x$, which indeed agrees with the right-hand side. Also, substituting $y_2=x+1$ into the homogeneous equation $xy''-(x+1)y'+y=0$, we have $y_2'=1,\ y_2''=0$, so $x\cdot0-(x+1)\cdot1+(x+1)=0$, and hence it is indeed another fundamental solution (together with $y_1=e^x$, the entire general solution of the nonhomogeneous equation has been found starting from just one fundamental solution).

Exercise 36.3: Euler's equation, distinct real roots ($x^2y''+7xy'+8y=0$)

Solution

Here $a=7,\ b=8$. By Formula 36.2, the characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2+(7-1)\lambda+8=\lambda^2+6\lambda+8=(\lambda+2)(\lambda+4)=0 $$

Hence $\lambda=-2,-4$ (distinct real roots). Therefore the general solution is

$$ y=C_1x^{-2}+C_2x^{-4} $$

(Substituting each of $x^{-2},x^{-4}$ into the equation gives $0$; verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 36.4: Euler's equation, a repeated root and an initial value problem ($x^2y''-3xy'+4y=0$)

Solution

Here $a=-3,\ b=4$. The characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2+(-3-1)\lambda+4=\lambda^2-4\lambda+4=(\lambda-2)^2=0 $$

This gives the repeated root $\lambda_0=2$. By Theorem 36.2, the general solution is

$$ y=C_1x^2+C_2x^2\ln x $$

We now apply the initial conditions. We have $y'(x)=2C_1x+C_2(2x\ln x+x)$ (product rule: $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd x}(x^2\ln x)=2x\ln x+x^2\cdot\frac1x=2x\ln x+x$). Substituting $x=1$ (note that $\ln1=0$), we get

$$ y(1)=C_1\cdot1+C_2\cdot1\cdot0=C_1=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad C_1=1 $$ $$ y'(1)=2C_1\cdot1+C_2(2\cdot1\cdot0+1)=2C_1+C_2=2+C_2=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad C_2=-1 $$

Therefore the required solution is

$$ y=x^2-x^2\ln x=x^2(1-\ln x) $$

As a check, $y(1)=1\cdot(1-0)=1$, and from $y'(x)=2x(1-\ln x)+x^2\cdot\left(-\frac1x\right)=2x-2x\ln x-x=x-2x\ln x$ we get $y'(1)=1-0=1$, so both initial conditions are satisfied. We have also verified that this agrees with the result of solving this initial value problem directly with SymPy's $\mathrm{dsolve}$.

Exercise 36.5: Euler's equation, complex roots ($x^2y''+xy'+4y=0$)

Solution

Here $a=1,\ b=4$. The characteristic equation is

$$ \lambda^2+(1-1)\lambda+4=\lambda^2+4=0 $$

Hence $\lambda=\pm2i$ ($\alpha=0,\ \beta=2$). By Theorem 36.3, the general solution is

$$ y=C_1\cos(2\ln x)+C_2\sin(2\ln x) $$

(Since $x^\alpha=x^0=1$, no coefficient appears in front. Substituting each of $\cos(2\ln x),\sin(2\ln x)$ into the equation gives $0$; verified with SymPy.)

Exercise 36.6: Finding a fundamental solution directly for a variable-coefficient equation (condition (I))

Solution
