Here $P(x)=x$, $Q(x)=-\left(\dfrac2{x^2}+2\right)$. We compute the expression $\lambda(\lambda-1)+\lambda xP(x)+x^2Q(x)$ in condition (I) of Theorem 36.4.

$$ \lambda xP(x)=\lambda x\cdot x=\lambda x^2, \qquad x^2Q(x)=x^2\cdot\left(-\frac2{x^2}-2\right)=-2-2x^2 $$

Substituting these, we get

$$ \lambda(\lambda-1)+\lambda x^2+(-2-2x^2)=(\lambda^2-\lambda-2)+x^2(\lambda-2) $$

For this to be identically $0$ regardless of $x$, the coefficient of $x^2$ and the constant term must each be $0$.

$$ \lambda-2=0 \qquad \text{and} \qquad \lambda^2-\lambda-2=0 $$

The first equation gives $\lambda=2$. Substituting this into the second equation gives $2^2-2-2=4-2-2=0$, which is indeed satisfied, so there is no contradiction. Therefore $\lambda=2$ is a constant satisfying condition (I), and by Theorem 36.4, $y=x^2$ is one of the fundamental solutions of this equation.

Checking by direct substitution, for $y=x^2$ we have $y'=2x,\ y''=2$, so

$$ y''+xy'-\left(\frac2{x^2}+2\right)y=2+x\cdot2x-\left(\frac2{x^2}+2\right)x^2=2+2x^2-2-2x^2=0 $$

Thus the equation is indeed satisfied (also verified with SymPy; the other fundamental solution can be found by applying the reduction-of-order method of Section 36.2 to $y_1=x^2$).

Exercise 36.7: An initial value problem for simple harmonic motion ($m=2,\ k=8$)

Solution

We have $\omega=\sqrt{k/m}=\sqrt{8/2}=\sqrt4=2$. By Theorem 36.6, the solution has the form $x(t)=A\cos(2t-\theta_0)$. Since $\dot x(t)=-2A\sin(2t-\theta_0)$,

$$ x(0)=A\cos(-\theta_0)=A\cos\theta_0=0, \qquad \dot x(0)=-2A\sin(-\theta_0)=2A\sin\theta_0=2 $$

From the first equation $A\cos\theta_0=0$ we get, since $A\neq0$, $\cos\theta_0=0$, that is, either $\theta_0=\dfrac\pi2$ or $\theta_0=-\dfrac\pi2$ (which is the same angle as $\dfrac{3\pi}2$). The second equation gives $A\sin\theta_0=1$. If we agree that the amplitude is positive ($A\gt0$), then we need $\sin\theta_0=1/A\gt0$, so $\sin\theta_0\gt0$; hence $\theta_0=\dfrac\pi2$ is determined (for which $\sin\theta_0=1$), and $A\sin\theta_0=A=1$ (if $\theta_0=-\dfrac\pi2$, then $\sin\theta_0=-1$, and $A\sin\theta_0=1$ cannot be satisfied with $A\gt0$). Therefore $A=1,\ \theta_0=\dfrac\pi2$, and

$$ x(t)=\cos\left(2t-\frac\pi2\right)=\cos(2t)\cos\frac\pi2+\sin(2t)\sin\frac\pi2=\sin(2t) $$

(We used the addition formula and $\cos\frac\pi2=0,\sin\frac\pi2=1$.) As a check, $x(0)=\sin0=0$, and from $\dot x(t)=2\cos(2t)$ we get $\dot x(0)=2\cos0=2$, so both initial conditions are satisfied. We have also verified that this agrees with the result of solving the initial value problem directly with SymPy's $\mathrm{dsolve}$.

← Back to Chapter 36, “Second-Order Linear Nonhomogeneous Differential Equations and Euler's Equation”

Chapter 37 Higher-Order Linear Differential Equations

Exercise 37.1: Four distinct real roots ($y''''-5y''+4y=0$)

Solution

The characteristic equation is $\lambda^4-5\lambda^2+4=0$. Letting $\mu=\lambda^2$, we get $\mu^2-5\mu+4=0$. We look for two numbers whose sum is $5$ and whose product is $4$, and find $1,4$, so this factors as $(\mu-1)(\mu-4)=0$. Substituting back $\mu=\lambda^2$, we have

$$ (\lambda^2-1)(\lambda^2-4)=0 $$

Factoring further gives $(\lambda-1)(\lambda+1)(\lambda-2)(\lambda+2)=0$, so we obtain $\lambda=1,-1,2,-2$, that is, four distinct real roots. By Theorem 37.3, the general solution is

$$ y=C_1e^{x}+C_2e^{-x}+C_3e^{2x}+C_4e^{-2x} $$

As a check, substitute $y'=C_1e^x-C_2e^{-x}+2C_3e^{2x}-2C_4e^{-2x}$, $y''=C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x}+4C_3e^{2x}+4C_4e^{-2x}$, and $y''''=C_1e^x+C_2e^{-x}+16C_3e^{2x}+16C_4e^{-2x}$ into the left-hand side $y''''-5y''+4y$ of the original equation. The coefficient of $e^x$ is $1-5+4=0$, the coefficient of $e^{-x}$ is likewise $1-5+4=0$, the coefficient of $e^{2x}$ is $16-20+4=0$, and the coefficient of $e^{-2x}$ is likewise $16-20+4=0$, so the expression is indeed identically $0$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 37.2: Triple root ($y'''-3y''+3y'-y=0$)

Solution

The characteristic equation is $\lambda^3-3\lambda^2+3\lambda-1=0$. Its coefficients $1,-3,3,-1$ coincide exactly with the binomial coefficients ${}_3\mathrm{C}_0,{}_3\mathrm{C}_1,{}_3\mathrm{C}_2,{}_3\mathrm{C}_3=1,3,3,1$ with alternating signs attached. Indeed, by the binomial theorem (already learned in high school),

$$ (\lambda-1)^3=\lambda^3-3\lambda^2\cdot1+3\lambda\cdot1^2-1^3=\lambda^3-3\lambda^2+3\lambda-1 $$

so the characteristic equation can be rewritten as

$$ (\lambda-1)^3=0 $$

Hence $\lambda=1$ is a triple root ($k=3$). By Theorem 37.4, the fundamental solutions corresponding to $\lambda_0=1$ are the three functions $e^x,xe^x,x^2e^x$ (exactly matching the order 3 of the equation), and the general solution is

$$ y=(C_1+C_2x+C_3x^2)e^x $$

As a check, we substitute $y=x^2e^x$ directly. By the product rule, $y'=(2x+x^2)e^x,\ y''=(2+4x+x^2)e^x,\ y'''=(6+6x+x^2)e^x$, so

$$ y'''-3y''+3y'-y=\big[(6+6x+x^2)-3(2+4x+x^2)+3(2x+x^2)-x^2\big]e^x $$

Collecting the terms inside the brackets by degree, we find that the coefficient of $x^2$ is $1-3+3-1=0$, the coefficient of $x^1$ is $6-12+6-0=0$, and the constant term is $6-6+0-0=0$, so the whole expression is $0$ (the same holds for $e^x,xe^x$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 37.3: Comparison with the second-order Euler equation ($x^2y''-3xy'+4y=0$)

Solution

This is the Euler equation with $n=2$, so we apply Formula 37.2 of Section 37.5 (the characteristic equation in terms of falling factorials) to the case $n=2$. Letting $y=x^\lambda$, we have $y'=\lambda x^{\lambda-1}$ and $y''=\lambda(\lambda-1)x^{\lambda-2}$, so substituting into the equation gives

$$ x^2\cdot\lambda(\lambda-1)x^{\lambda-2}-3x\cdot\lambda x^{\lambda-1}+4x^\lambda=\big[\lambda(\lambda-1)-3\lambda+4\big]x^\lambda=0 $$

Dividing by $x^\lambda\neq0$ ($x\gt0$), we obtain the characteristic equation

$$ \lambda(\lambda-1)-3\lambda+4=\lambda^2-\lambda-3\lambda+4=\lambda^2-4\lambda+4=(\lambda-2)^2=0 $$

This has the double root $\lambda=2$. By the rule of Section 37.5.3 (for a repeated root of an Euler equation, the solution is multiplied by powers of $\ln x$ instead of powers of $x$), the general solution is

$$ y=(C_1+C_2\ln x)\,x^2 $$

This result coincides exactly with what we get by substituting $\lambda_0=2$ into Theorem 36.2 in Chapter 36, Section 36.4 (the formula $y=C_1x^{\lambda_0}+C_2x^{\lambda_0}\ln x$ for the repeated-root case of the second-order Euler equation)—we have confirmed that specializing the general $n$th-order method learned in this chapter to $n=2$ reproduces the result for the second-order case exactly. As a check, we substitute $y=x^2\ln x$ directly. Since $y'=2x\ln x+x,\ y''=2\ln x+2+1=2\ln x+3$,

$$ x^2(2\ln x+3)-3x(2x\ln x+x)+4x^2\ln x=(2x^2\ln x+3x^2)-(6x^2\ln x+3x^2)+4x^2\ln x=(2-6+4)x^2\ln x+(3-3)x^2=0 $$

so it indeed satisfies the equation (the same holds for $x^2$; verified with SymPy).

Exercise 37.4: Wronskian of monomials ($W(1,x,x^2,x^3)$)

Solution

Following Definition 37.3, we form the $4\times4$ Wronskian. Each column consists of one of the functions $1,x,x^2,x^3$ together with its derivatives (of orders 0 through 3).

$$ W(1,x,x^2,x^3)= \begin{vmatrix} 1 & x & x^2 & x^3\\ 0 & 1 & 2x & 3x^2\\ 0 & 0 & 2 & 6x\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 6 \end{vmatrix} $$

(In the first column, $1$, all the derivatives are $0$ (those of order 1 and higher); in the second column, $x$ has first derivative $1$, and the derivatives of order 2 and higher are $0$; in the third column, $x^2$ has first derivative $2x$ and second derivative $2$, and the derivatives of order 3 and higher are $0$; in the fourth column, $x^3$ has first derivative $3x^2$, second derivative $6x$, and third derivative $6$.) This matrix is exactly an upper triangular matrix (all entries below the diagonal are $0$). The determinant of an upper triangular matrix equals the product of its diagonal entries (Chapter 8), so

$$ W(1,x,x^2,x^3)=1\times1\times2\times6=12 $$

This value does not contain $x$; it is a constant (since it is not $0$, $1,x,x^2,x^3$ are linearly independent for every $x$). Furthermore, the diagonal entries $1,1,2,6$ are equal to $0!,1!,2!,3!$, respectively ($0!=1,\ 1!=1,\ 2!=1\times2=2,\ 3!=1\times2\times3=6$). Therefore

$$ W(1,x,x^2,x^3)=0!\cdot1!\cdot2!\cdot3!=1\cdot1\cdot2\cdot6=12 $$

In general, if we compute the Wronskian of $1,x,x^2,\ldots,x^{n-1}$ in the same way, the $(j-1)$th derivative in the $j$th column ($y_j=x^{j-1}$, $j=1,\ldots,n$) is $(j-1)!$, and all derivatives of higher order are $0$. So again we get an upper triangular matrix, and $W(1,x,\ldots,x^{n-1})=0!\,1!\,2!\cdots(n-1)!$ (a product of factorials) is a constant (for the case $n=4$, a direct computation with SymPy confirmed that the value agrees with $12$).

Exercise 37.5: A sixth-order equation with both complex and real repeated roots

Solution

The characteristic equation $p(\lambda)=(\lambda^2+1)^2(\lambda-2)^2=0$ is already given in factored form. From $\lambda^2+1=0$ we get $\lambda=\pm i$, and since this factor is squared, each is a double root. Also, $\lambda-2=0$ gives $\lambda=2$, and since this factor is squared as well, it is a double root. The multiplicities add up to $2+2+2=6$, which matches exactly the order 6 of the equation.

We apply Theorem 37.4 to each root. From $\lambda=2$ (double, real) we get the two functions $e^{2x},xe^{2x}$. From $\lambda=\pm i$ (each double, a complex pair, $\alpha=0,\beta=1$) we first get $\cos x,\sin x$, obtained, just as in the case of simple roots, by converting to real form with Euler's formula; because the roots are double, these are each also multiplied by $x$, which adds $x\cos x,x\sin x$, so we obtain the four functions $\cos x,\sin x,x\cos x,x\sin x$ (we have merely applied, with $k=2$, the rule seen in Illustration 37.4 of Section 37.4, “for a repeated complex root, the functions multiplied by $x^j$ ($j=0,\ldots,k-1$) become fundamental solutions”). The total is $2+4=6$ functions, matching the order 6, and the general solution is

$$ y=(C_1+C_2x)\cos x+(C_3+C_4x)\sin x+(C_5+C_6x)e^{2x} $$

As a check, expanding $p(\lambda)$ gives

$$ p(\lambda)=\lambda^6-4\lambda^5+6\lambda^4-8\lambda^3+9\lambda^2-4\lambda+4 $$

so the original equation is $y^{(6)}-4y^{(5)}+6y''''-8y'''+9y''-4y'+4y=0$. We have verified with SymPy that substituting each of the six fundamental solutions $\cos x,\sin x,x\cos x,x\sin x,e^{2x},xe^{2x}$ of the general solution found above into this equation gives $0$ in every case.

Exercise 37.6 (application to materials science): Deflection of a beam under a uniformly distributed load

Solution

The right-hand side of the equation $EI\,y''''(x)=w_0$ ($w_0,E,I$ are constants) is a constant independent of $x$, so without even bringing up a test for exact differential equations as in Section 37.1, we see at once that each time we integrate both sides once with respect to $x$, the order of the derivative goes down by one. We divide both sides by $EI$ (a constant, $\neq0$) and then integrate four times with respect to $x$, in succession.

$$ y''''=\frac{w_0}{EI} \ \Longrightarrow\ y'''=\frac{w_0}{EI}x+A \ \Longrightarrow\ y''=\frac{w_0}{2EI}x^2+Ax+B $$ $$ \Longrightarrow\ y'=\frac{w_0}{6EI}x^3+\frac{A}{2}x^2+Bx+C \ \Longrightarrow\ y=\frac{w_0}{24EI}x^4+\frac{A}{6}x^3+\frac{B}{2}x^2+Cx+D $$

($A,B,C,D$ are integration constants. Since the equation is of fourth order, exactly four arbitrary constants appear—this can be understood naturally from the fact, stated in Section 37.2, that “the general solution of an $n$th-order homogeneous equation has $n$ arbitrary constants,” together with writing the solution of the nonhomogeneous equation as a sum with a particular solution: the part $\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}x^4$ is a particular solution for the right-hand side $w_0/EI$, and the remaining part $\dfrac{A}{6}x^3+\dfrac{B}{2}x^2+Cx+D$ is the general solution of the homogeneous equation $y''''=0$.) Now we apply the boundary conditions. First, $y(0)=0$:

$$ y(0)=D=0 \quad\therefore\ D=0 $$

Next, $y''(0)=0$:

$$ y''(0)=B=0 \quad\therefore\ B=0 $$

Using $B=D=0$, we have $y(x)=\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}x^4+\dfrac{A}{6}x^3+Cx$ and $y''(x)=\dfrac{w_0}{2EI}x^2+Ax$. We apply the remaining two boundary conditions $y(L)=0,\ y''(L)=0$.

$$ y''(L)=\frac{w_0}{2EI}L^2+AL=0 \quad\therefore\ A=-\frac{w_0L}{2EI} $$ $$ y(L)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}L^4+\frac{A}{6}L^3+CL=0 $$

Substituting $A=-\dfrac{w_0L}{2EI}$, we obtain

$$ \frac{w_0}{24EI}L^4-\frac{w_0L}{12EI}L^3+CL=\frac{w_0L^4}{24EI}-\frac{w_0L^4}{12EI}+CL=-\frac{w_0L^4}{24EI}+CL=0 $$

(We used $\dfrac1{24}-\dfrac1{12}=\dfrac1{24}-\dfrac2{24}=-\dfrac1{24}$.) Hence $CL=\dfrac{w_0L^4}{24EI}$, that is, $C=\dfrac{w_0L^3}{24EI}$. Summing up,

$$ y(x)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}x^4-\frac{w_0L}{12EI}x^3+\frac{w_0L^3}{24EI}x=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\big(x^4-2Lx^3+L^3x\big)=\frac{w_0\,x\big(L^3-2Lx^2+x^3\big)}{24EI} $$

Now we compute the deflection at the midpoint, $x=L/2$. Since $x^4=\dfrac{L^4}{16}$, $Lx^3=L\cdot\dfrac{L^3}{8}=\dfrac{L^4}{8}$, and $L^3x=\dfrac{L^4}{2}$,

$$ y\!\left(\frac L2\right)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\left(\frac{L^4}{16}-2\cdot\frac{L^4}{8}+\frac{L^4}{2}\right)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\cdot L^4\left(\frac1{16}-\frac14+\frac12\right)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\cdot L^4\cdot\frac{1-4+8}{16}=\frac{w_0L^4}{24EI}\cdot\frac5{16} $$

(Bringing to a common denominator: $\dfrac1{16}-\dfrac4{16}+\dfrac8{16}=\dfrac5{16}$.) Therefore

$$ y\!\left(\frac L2\right)=\frac{5w_0L^4}{384EI} $$

which agrees exactly with the formula $\dfrac{5w_0L^4}{384EI}$ known in mechanics of materials as “the formula for the deflection of a simply supported beam under a uniformly distributed load” (we verified with SymPy, by solving the boundary value problem directly, that the same result is obtained). The sign obtained, $y(L/2)\gt0$ when $w_0,E,I,L$ are all positive, corresponds to the magnitude of the deflection when $y$ is defined to be positive in the same direction as the load (downward).

← Back to Chapter 37, “Higher-Order Linear Differential Equations”

Chapter 38 The Operator Method

Exercise 38.1: Substitution formula for exponential functions (non-resonance)

Solution

The equation $y'-5y=e^{2x}$ has the form $\Phi(D)y=f(x)$ with $\Phi(D)=D-5$ (that is, $\Phi(z)=z-5$) and $f(x)=e^{2x}$ ($\alpha=2$). First we check that the exponent $\alpha=2$ of the forcing term does not coincide with the root ($\lambda=5$) of the characteristic equation $\Phi(\lambda)=\lambda-5=0$. Indeed,

$$ \Phi(2) = 2-5 = -3 \neq 0 $$

so no resonance occurs, and Formula 38.2 can be used as it stands. All we need to do is “replace $D$ in $\Phi(D)$ by $\alpha=2$ and divide,” so

$$ Y = \frac{1}{\Phi(D)}\big[e^{2x}\big] = \frac{1}{\Phi(2)}e^{2x} = \frac{1}{-3}e^{2x} = -\frac13 e^{2x} $$

is a particular solution. To check, we substitute $Y=-\dfrac13e^{2x}$ directly into the equation. Since $Y'=-\dfrac23e^{2x}$,

$$ Y'-5Y = -\frac23e^{2x} - 5\cdot\left(-\frac13e^{2x}\right) = -\frac23e^{2x}+\frac53e^{2x} = \frac33e^{2x} = e^{2x} $$

and so $y'-5y=e^{2x}$ is indeed satisfied.

Exercise 38.2: Resonance for an exponential function (double root)

Solution

Factoring $\Phi(D)=D^2-6D+9$ gives $\Phi(D)=(D-3)^2$ ($\Phi(z)=z^2-6z+9=(z-3)^2$). The forcing term is $e^{3x}$ ($\alpha=3$), and checking the characteristic equation $\Phi(\lambda)=(\lambda-3)^2=0$ shows that $\lambda=3$ is a double root and, moreover, coincides exactly with the exponent $\alpha=3$ of the forcing term. That is,

$$ \Phi(3) = (3-3)^2 = 0 $$

so the condition $\Phi(\alpha)\neq0$ of Formula 38.2 fails, and this is resonance. We therefore apply Formula 38.4 to the form $\Phi(D)=(D-\alpha)^n$ (with $\alpha=3$, $n=2$), which gives

$$ Y = \frac{1}{(D-3)^2}\big[e^{3x}\big] = \frac{x^2}{2!}e^{3x} = \frac{x^2}{2}e^{3x} $$

as a particular solution. To check, we compute the first and second derivatives of $Y=\dfrac{x^2}{2}e^{3x}$ with the product rule.

$$ Y' = xe^{3x} + \frac{x^2}{2}\cdot3e^{3x} = e^{3x}\left(x+\frac32x^2\right) $$ $$ Y'' = 3e^{3x}\left(x+\frac32x^2\right) + e^{3x}(1+3x) = e^{3x}\left(3x+\frac92x^2+1+3x\right) = e^{3x}\left(\frac92x^2+6x+1\right) $$

(The first term comes from differentiating $e^{3x}$, which multiplies it by three, and the second term comes from differentiating the contents of the parentheses, $x+\frac32x^2$, to get $1+3x$; this is the product rule.) Using these,

$$ Y''-6Y'+9Y = e^{3x}\left[\left(\frac92x^2+6x+1\right) - 6\left(x+\frac32x^2\right) + 9\cdot\frac12x^2\right] $$

Collecting the coefficient of $x^2$ inside the brackets gives $\dfrac92-9+\dfrac92=0$, collecting the coefficient of $x$ gives $6-6=0$, and only the constant term $1$ remains, so

$$ Y''-6Y'+9Y = e^{3x}\cdot1 = e^{3x} $$

and so $y''-6y'+9y=e^{3x}$ is indeed satisfied.

Exercise 38.3: Combining the shift theorem and resonance

Solution

We have $\Phi(D)=D^2-2D+1=(D-1)^2$, and the forcing term $f(x)=x^2e^{x}$ has the form “exponential function $e^{x}$” × “polynomial $x^2$.” So we use the shift theorem (Formula 38.3) with $\alpha=1$ and $g(x)=x^2$. First we compute $\Phi(D+1)$: replacing $D$ by $D+1$ gives

$$ \Phi(D+1) = \big((D+1)-1\big)^2 = D^2 $$

(the $-1$ and $+1$ cancel exactly). Therefore

$$ \frac{1}{(D-1)^2}\big[x^2e^{x}\big] = e^{x}\cdot\frac{1}{D^2}\big[x^2\big] $$

All that remains is to compute $\dfrac{1}{D^2}[x^2]$, that is, to integrate $x^2$ twice ($D^{-n}$ of Definition 38.1, with the constants of integration taken to be 0).

$$ \int x^2\,dx = \frac{x^3}{3},\qquad \int\frac{x^3}{3}\,dx = \frac{x^4}{12} $$

so $\dfrac{1}{D^2}[x^2]=\dfrac{x^4}{12}$, and the particular solution we seek is

$$ Y = \frac{x^4}{12}e^{x} $$

To check, we compute the derivatives of $Y=\dfrac{x^4}{12}e^{x}$ with the product rule.

$$ Y' = \frac{4x^3}{12}e^x + \frac{x^4}{12}e^x = e^x\left(\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^4}{12}\right) $$ $$ Y'' = e^x\left(\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^4}{12}\right) + e^x\left(x^2+\frac{x^3}{3}\right) = e^x\left(\frac{x^4}{12}+\frac{2x^3}{3}+x^2\right) $$

(In the second equality, differentiating $e^x$ leaves the first parenthesis as it is, and differentiating the contents of $\left(\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^4}{12}\right)$ adds one more term, $\left(x^2+\frac{x^3}{3}\right)$.) Using these,

$$ Y''-2Y'+Y = e^x\left[\left(\frac{x^4}{12}+\frac{2x^3}{3}+x^2\right) - 2\left(\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^4}{12}\right) + \frac{x^4}{12}\right] $$

Inside the brackets, the coefficient of $x^4$ is $\frac{1}{12}-\frac{2}{12}+\frac{1}{12}=0$ and the coefficient of $x^3$ is $\frac23-\frac23=0$; only the coefficient of $x^2$ remains, equal to $1$, so

$$ Y''-2Y'+Y = e^x\cdot x^2 = x^2e^x $$

and so $y''-2y'+y=x^2e^x$ is indeed satisfied.

Exercise 38.4: Substitution formula for trigonometric functions (non-resonance)

Solution

We have $\Phi(D^2)=D^2+9$ (that is, $\Phi(z)=z+9$), and the forcing term is $\cos2x$ (the angular frequency of the external force is $\alpha=2$). Checking the condition $\Phi(-\alpha^2)\neq0$ of Formula 38.5,

$$ \Phi(-\alpha^2) = \Phi(-4) = -4+9 = 5 \neq 0 $$

so no resonance occurs, and Formula 38.5 can be used. All we need to do is “replace $D^2$ in $\Phi(D^2)$ by $-\alpha^2=-4$ and divide,” so

$$ Y = \frac{1}{\Phi(D^2)}\cos2x = \frac{1}{\Phi(-4)}\cos2x = \frac15\cos2x $$

is a particular solution. To check, we substitute $Y=\dfrac15\cos2x$ into the equation. Since $Y''=-4\cdot\dfrac15\cos2x=-\dfrac45\cos2x$,

$$ Y''+9Y = -\frac45\cos2x + 9\cdot\frac15\cos2x = \left(-\frac45+\frac95\right)\cos2x = \cos2x $$

and so $y''+9y=\cos2x$ is indeed satisfied.

Exercise 38.5: Resonance for trigonometric functions

Solution

We have $\Phi(D^2)=D^2+4$ (that is, $\Phi(z)=z+4$), and the forcing term is $\sin2x$ (the angular frequency of the external force is $\alpha=2$). Checking the condition of Formula 38.5,

$$ \Phi(-\alpha^2) = \Phi(-4) = -4+4 = 0 $$

so the condition $\Phi(-\alpha^2)\neq0$ fails—this is the trigonometric version of resonance, in which the natural angular frequency ($\sqrt4=2$) of the system described by the equation itself coincides exactly with the angular frequency ($2$) of the external force. Hence Formula 38.5 cannot be used, and we use the resonance formula, Formula 38.6, with $\alpha=2$.

$$ Y = \frac{1}{D^2+4}\sin2x = -\frac{x}{2\cdot2}\cos2x = -\frac{x}{4}\cos2x $$

is a particular solution. To check, we compute the derivatives of $Y=-\dfrac{x}{4}\cos2x$ with the product rule.

$$ Y' = -\frac14\cos2x - \frac{x}{4}\cdot(-2\sin2x) = -\frac14\cos2x+\frac{x}{2}\sin2x $$ $$ Y'' = \frac12\sin2x + \frac12\sin2x + \frac{x}{2}\cdot2\cos2x = \sin2x + x\cos2x $$

(Differentiating the first term $-\frac14\cos2x$ gives $\frac12\sin2x$, and differentiating the second term $\frac{x}{2}\sin2x$ with the product rule gives $\frac12\sin2x+x\cos2x$; together they give $\sin2x+x\cos2x$.) Using these,

$$ Y''+4Y = \big(\sin2x+x\cos2x\big) + 4\left(-\frac{x}{4}\cos2x\right) = \sin2x + x\cos2x - x\cos2x = \sin2x $$

and so $y''+4y=\sin2x$ is indeed satisfied.

On the amplitude of the solution: the amplitude of the particular solution $Y=-\dfrac{x}{4}\cos2x$ (the height of its envelope) is $\left|\dfrac{x}{4}\right|$, which grows without bound as $x\to\infty$. Because the angular frequency of the external force coincides exactly with the natural angular frequency of the system itself, resonance occurs, and the amplitude keeps increasing in proportion to the time $x$ while the solution oscillates (the same mechanism as the phenomenon we saw in Section 38.8 and Figure 38.2).

Exercise 38.6: Commutativity of operators with constant coefficients (proof problem)

Solution

Let $\Phi_1(D)=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}a_iD^i$ and $\Phi_2(D)=\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}b_jD^j$. First we compute $\Phi_1(D)\big[\Phi_2(D)f(x)\big]$. Since $\Phi_2(D)f(x)=\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^n b_jD^jf(x)$ is a sum of finitely many terms, linearity (Theorem 38.1; that is, if we use repeatedly the property that $D$ preserves sums and constant multiples, it extends directly from a sum of two terms to sums and constant multiples of finitely many terms) gives

$$ \Phi_1(D)\Big[\sum_{j=0}^n b_jD^jf(x)\Big] = \sum_{i=0}^m a_iD^i\Big[\sum_{j=0}^n b_jD^jf(x)\Big] = \sum_{i=0}^m\sum_{j=0}^n a_ib_j\,D^i\big[D^jf(x)\big] $$

(We distributed the outer sum $\sum_i a_iD^i[\cdots]$ inside, and then distributed the inner sum $\sum_j b_jD^jf(x)$ term by term, again by linearity. Since $a_i,b_j$ are both constants, they may be taken outside $D^i$.) Now, using Theorem 38.1 ($D^i[D^jf(x)]=D^{i+j}[f(x)]$: different powers of $D$ commute with one another), we get

$$ \Phi_1(D)\big[\Phi_2(D)f(x)\big] = \sum_{i=0}^m\sum_{j=0}^n a_ib_j\,D^{i+j}\big[f(x)\big] $$

Carrying out exactly the same computation for $\Phi_2(D)\big[\Phi_1(D)f(x)\big]$ (with the roles of the indices $i,j$ interchanged), we obtain

$$ \Phi_2(D)\big[\Phi_1(D)f(x)\big] = \sum_{j=0}^n\sum_{i=0}^m b_ja_i\,D^{j+i}\big[f(x)\big] $$

Now compare the double sums $\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^m\sum_{j=0}^n a_ib_jD^{i+j}[f(x)]$ and $\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^n\sum_{i=0}^m b_ja_iD^{j+i}[f(x)]$. Both are “sums over all combinations of $i=0,\dots,m$ and $j=0,\dots,n$”; the range over which we add (the range over which $i,j$ vary) is exactly the same, and the content of each term also agrees completely, because $a_ib_j=b_ja_i$ (products of real numbers commute) and $i+j=j+i$ (sums of integers commute). Changing the order of summation (whether we sum over $i$ first or over $j$ first) does not change the result of a sum of finitely many real numbers, so

$$ \sum_{i=0}^m\sum_{j=0}^n a_ib_j\,D^{i+j}\big[f(x)\big] = \sum_{j=0}^n\sum_{i=0}^m b_ja_i\,D^{j+i}\big[f(x)\big] $$

holds. Combining the above,

$$ \Phi_1(D)\big[\Phi_2(D)f(x)\big] = \Phi_2(D)\big[\Phi_1(D)f(x)\big] $$

has been shown.

∎

Check with a concrete example. We check numerically in the concrete case $\Phi_1(D)=D^2+3$, $\Phi_2(D)=D^3-2D$, $f(x)=\sin2x+x^3$. Using Python (SymPy), we computed the result of first computing $\Phi_2(D)f(x)=D^3[f]-2D[f]$ and then applying $\Phi_1(D)$, and the result of first computing $\Phi_1(D)f(x)=D^2[f]+3f$ and then applying $\Phi_2(D)$; we expanded both and confirmed that they agree (see the verification script). This provides supporting evidence, from a concrete example, for the correctness of the general proof.

Exercise 38.7: Basic computation with the inverse operator $D^{-1}$

Solution

By Definition 38.1, $\dfrac1D[y]=\displaystyle\int y\,dx$ (with the constant of integration taken to be 0). Integrating the polynomial $3x^2-2x+1$ term by term gives

$$ \frac{1}{D}\big[3x^2-2x+1\big] = \int(3x^2-2x+1)\,dx = 3\cdot\frac{x^3}{3} - 2\cdot\frac{x^2}{2} + x = x^3-x^2+x $$

(with the constant of integration taken to be 0). Hence the answer is $x^3-x^2+x$. To confirm it, we differentiate this answer with $D$, which gives

$$ D\big[x^3-x^2+x\big] = 3x^2-2x+1 $$

and we indeed recover the original $3x^2-2x+1$. This computation also confirms that $D$ and $D^{-1}$ ($=1/D$) are inverse operations of each other.

Chapter 39 The Power Series Method and Frobenius Theory

Exercise 39.1: Radius of convergence of $\cos x$

Solution

The Maclaurin expansion of $\cos x$ is

$$ \cos x = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^k}{(2k)!}x^{2k} = 1-\frac{x^2}{2!}+\frac{x^4}{4!}-\frac{x^6}{6!}+\cdots $$

Here all coefficients of odd degree are $0$, and the coefficient of the even-degree term $x^{2k}$ is $a_{2k}=\dfrac{(-1)^k}{(2k)!}$. In its basic form, d'Alembert's ratio test considers the ratio of the coefficients of adjacent degrees, but in order to follow only the coefficients that are not $0$, we regard $x^2$ as a single variable and compute the ratio of $a_{2k}$ to $a_{2k+2}$, the “adjacent coefficients that are not $0$.”

$$ \left|\frac{a_{2k}}{a_{2k+2}}\right| = \left|\frac{(-1)^k/(2k)!}{(-1)^{k+1}/(2k+2)!}\right| = \frac{(2k+2)!}{(2k)!} = (2k+1)(2k+2) $$

(Since $(2k+2)!=(2k+2)(2k+1)\cdot(2k)!$, the factor $(2k)!$ cancels and gives this form.) As $k\to\infty$, this value

$$ \lim_{k\to\infty}(2k+1)(2k+2) = \infty $$

grows without bound. Therefore the radius of convergence is $R=\infty$; that is, the Maclaurin expansion of $\cos x$ converges for every real number $x$ (in agreement with the conclusion stated in Section 39.2.1 of this chapter). (Verified with SymPy that $(2k+1)(2k+2)\to\infty$.)

Exercise 39.2: Series solution of $y''-xy'-y=0$

Solution

Writing the equation in the form of Eq. ①, we have $P(x)=-x,\ Q(x)=-1$, and both are polynomials (analytic in a special sense), hence analytic at every real number. In particular, $x=0$ is an ordinary point, so the solution has the form $y=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_kx^k$. Differentiating term by term, we obtain

$$ y'=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}ka_kx^{k-1},\qquad y''=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}k(k-1)a_kx^{k-2} $$

Shifting the index of the sum for $y''$ by $k\to k+2$ (replacing $k$ by $k+2$ in $k(k-1)a_kx^{k-2}$ makes the lower limit of the sum start from $k=0$ and turns $x^{k-2}$ into $x^k$), we can rewrite it as

$$ y''=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+2)(k+1)a_{k+2}x^{k} $$

On the other hand, $xy'=x\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}ka_kx^{k-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}ka_kx^{k}$, and since the $k=0$ term of this sum is $0\cdot a_0x^0=0$, the value does not change if we rewrite the lower limit as $k=0$. Also, $y$ is already $\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_kx^k$ as it stands. Substituting these into the equation $y''-xy'-y=0$, we obtain

$$ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+2)(k+1)a_{k+2}x^{k} - \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}ka_kx^{k} - \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_kx^{k} = 0 \ \Longrightarrow\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigl\{(k+2)(k+1)a_{k+2}-(k+1)a_k\bigr\}x^{k}=0 $$

(we combined $-ka_k-a_k=-(k+1)a_k$). This is an identity in $x$, so the coefficient of each power must itself be $0$. Dividing both sides by $k+1\neq0$ (which is automatic since $k\ge0$), we obtain the recurrence relation

$$ a_{k+2}=\frac{a_k}{k+2} \qquad (k=0,1,2,\ldots) $$

Taking $a_0=1,a_1=0$, all the odd-degree coefficients are $0$ because they start from $a_1=0$, and the even-degree coefficients are determined as

$$ a_2=\frac{a_0}{2}=\frac12,\qquad a_4=\frac{a_2}{4}=\frac18,\qquad a_6=\frac{a_4}{6}=\frac1{48} $$

Therefore, the terms through $x^6$ are

$$ y=1+\frac{x^2}{2}+\frac{x^4}{8}+\frac{x^6}{48}+\cdots $$

(Verified with SymPy, by substituting the series directly into the equation, that the recurrence relation $a_{k+2}=a_k/(k+2)$ holds and that the four terms above are correct.)

Exercise 39.3: Classifying a Bessel-type equation of half-integer order

Solution

In the form of Eq. ①, we have $P(x)=\dfrac1x,\ Q(x)=1-\dfrac1{4x^2}$. Since $P(x)=1/x$ is not defined at $x=0$ (the denominator is $0$), it is not analytic there, and $x=0$ is a singular point. So we examine $xP(x),x^2Q(x)$.

$$ xP(x)=x\cdot\frac1x=1,\qquad x^2Q(x)=x^2\left(1-\frac1{4x^2}\right)=x^2-\frac14 $$

Here $xP(x)=1$ is a constant function and $x^2Q(x)=x^2-\dfrac14$ is a polynomial, and both are analytic at $x=0$. Therefore $x=0$ is a regular singular point. Reading off the coefficients of the Maclaurin expansions, we have $a_0=1$ (all the other $a_i=0$), and since $x^2Q(x)=-\dfrac14+0\cdot x+1\cdot x^2$, we have $b_0=-\dfrac14$. The indicial equation is

$$ \lambda(\lambda-1)+a_0\lambda+b_0=\lambda(\lambda-1)+\lambda-\frac14=\lambda^2-\frac14=0 $$

and we obtain $\lambda=\pm\dfrac12$. (This equation in fact coincides with the case $n=1/2$ of Bessel's differential equation, which we study from the next chapter onward, and it is known that the half-integer exponents $\lambda=\pm1/2$ appear. We verified with SymPy that these are the roots of the indicial equation.)

Exercise 39.4: Complex roots of the indicial equation

Solution

For $P(x)=\dfrac1x,\ Q(x)=\dfrac1{x^2}$, we have $xP(x)=1,\ x^2Q(x)=1$ (both are constant functions), so $a_0=1,b_0=1$. Substituting into the indicial equation $\lambda^2+(a_0-1)\lambda+b_0=0$ (Section 39.4.1 of this chapter), we obtain

$$ \lambda^2+(a_0-1)\lambda+b_0=\lambda^2+(1-1)\lambda+1=\lambda^2+1=0 $$

and solving this gives

$$ \lambda^2=-1 \ \Longrightarrow\ \lambda=\pm i $$

(where $i$ is the imaginary unit). Thus the roots of the indicial equation are not necessarily real; they can also be complex numbers. In this case, we need to redefine complex powers by means of the logarithm, as in $x^{\pm i}=e^{\pm i\ln x}$ ($x\gt0$), and use Euler's formula to rewrite the solutions as real-valued fundamental solutions (the detailed treatment of this is based on the same idea as the procedure for complex roots of Euler's differential equation in Chapter 36—$x^{i\beta}=e^{i\beta\ln x}=\cos(\beta\ln x)+i\sin(\beta\ln x)$—but because it goes beyond the framework of Frobenius theory, we do not go into it any further in this book). (Verified with SymPy that the roots of $\lambda^2+1=0$ are $\pm i$.)

Exercise 39.5: Re-verifying the error in $\log(1+x)$

Solution

Let $f(x)=\log(1+x)$. We differentiate it successively.

$$ f'(x)=(1+x)^{-1},\qquad f''(x)=-(1+x)^{-2},\qquad f'''(x)=2(1+x)^{-3},\qquad f^{(4)}(x)=-6(1+x)^{-4} $$

(Each time we differentiate $f'$, the exponent goes down by one and the corresponding coefficient is multiplied in front—$f''=-1\cdot(1+x)^{-2}$, $f'''=-1\cdot(-2)(1+x)^{-3}=2(1+x)^{-3}$, $f^{(4)}=2\cdot(-3)(1+x)^{-4}=-6(1+x)^{-4}$, and so on.) Substituting $x=0$, we get

$$ f^{(4)}(0)=-6\cdot1^{-4}=-6 $$

By the formula for the coefficients of the Maclaurin expansion, $a_k=f^{(k)}(0)/k!$ (Section 39.2 of this chapter), we obtain

$$ a_4=\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{4!}=\frac{-6}{24}=-\frac14 $$

Therefore the correct fourth term (the $x^4$ term) of the expansion of $\log(1+x)$ is $a_4x^4=-\dfrac14x^4$, and as stated in the Caution box (box warn) in Section 39.2.1 of this chapter, we have confirmed, also by direct computation from the fourth derivative, that the “$+\dfrac{x^4}4$” in the original notes is a sign error made in writing. (Verified with SymPy that $f^{(4)}(0)=-6$.)

Exercise 39.6: What if $\lambda_1-\lambda_2$ were not an integer?

Solution

Keeping $P(x)=\dfrac3x$ as it is and changing the other coefficient to $Q(x)=-\dfrac2{x^2}$, we get $xP(x)=3$ (unchanged) and $x^2Q(x)=x^2\left(-\dfrac2{x^2}\right)=-2$, so $a_0=3,\ b_0=-2$. The indicial equation is

$$ \lambda^2+(a_0-1)\lambda+b_0=\lambda^2+2\lambda-2=0 $$

and by the quadratic formula we obtain

$$ \lambda=\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{4+8}}{2}=\frac{-2\pm2\sqrt3}{2}=-1\pm\sqrt3 $$

That is, $\lambda_1=-1+\sqrt3\approx0.73,\ \lambda_2=-1-\sqrt3\approx-2.73$, and the difference is

$$ \lambda_1-\lambda_2=2\sqrt3\approx3.46 $$

which is not an integer. Therefore this falls under case (i) of Theorem 39.1 of this chapter. In Example 39.8, because $\lambda_1-\lambda_2=4$ (an integer), the recurrence relation built from the smaller root $\lambda_2$ ran into “resonance” at $k=4$ (the expression in the braces became exactly $0$), so we could not find $y_2$ as a plain series, and we had to rely on the method for the case where one fundamental solution is already known ($y_2=u(x)y_1$, reduction of order).

However, when the difference is not an integer, as in the present case, this resonance never occurs. This is because the coefficient $\{(\lambda_2+k)(\lambda_2+k-1)+a_0(\lambda_2+k)+b_0\}$ of the recurrence relation built from $\lambda_2$ becomes $0$ only when $\lambda_2+k$ coincides with the other root $\lambda_1$ of the indicial equation, that is, only when $k=\lambda_1-\lambda_2$. In the present case, $\lambda_1-\lambda_2=2\sqrt3$ is not an integer, so for no positive integer $k$ can $\lambda_2+k=\lambda_1$ ever hold. Therefore, whichever of $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ we start from, the recurrence relation can be solved all the way without a hitch, and we obtain the two independent Frobenius series

$$ y_1=x^{\lambda_1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_kx^k=x^{-1+\sqrt3},\qquad y_2=x^{\lambda_2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}b_kx^k=x^{-1-\sqrt3} $$

as they stand (the present equation is of Euler type, with all coefficients other than $a_0,b_0$ equal to $0$, so, just as in Example 39.5, the series terminates with its leading term alone, and $y_1=x^{-1+\sqrt3},\ y_2=x^{-1-\sqrt3}$ themselves serve as fundamental solutions). In other words, the extra effort of “relying on reduction of order” that was needed in Example 39.8 is unnecessary, and Theorem 39.1(i) of Section 39.4 can be applied as it stands. (Verified with SymPy that the coefficient of the recurrence relation on the $\lambda=-1-\sqrt3$ side does not become $0$ for any of $k=1,\ldots,5$; that is, resonance does not occur.)

← Back to Chapter 39, “The Power Series Method and Frobenius Theory”

Chapter 40 Legendre's Differential Equation and Legendre Polynomials

Exercise 40.1: Deriving $P_2(x)$ by solving the case $n=2$ with the series method

Solution

Recall the recurrence relation for the coefficients of Legendre's differential equation, written in a form that works for both even and odd $k$:

$$ a_{k+2}=-\frac{(n-k)(n+k+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}a_k\qquad(k=0,1,2,\ldots) $$

Substitute $n=2$ and $a_0=1$, and compute in the order $k=0,2,4,\ldots$.

$k=0$: $a_2=-\dfrac{(2-0)(2+1)}{1\cdot2}a_0=-\dfrac{2\cdot3}{2}\cdot1=-3$.

$k=2$: $a_4=-\dfrac{(2-2)(2+3)}{3\cdot4}a_2=-\dfrac{0\cdot5}{12}\cdot(-3)=0$ (this is the moment when the numerator factor $(n-k)=(2-2)=0$ appears). Therefore $a_4=a_6=\cdots=0$ as well; all the later coefficients likewise become $0$.

It follows that $U_2(x)=a_0+a_2x^2+a_4x^4+\cdots=1-3x^2$ (a polynomial of degree 2 that ends at $x^2$).

Next, following the definition, we normalize by the value at $x=1$.

$$ U_2(1)=1-3\cdot1^2=1-3=-2 $$

Therefore we obtain

$$ P_2(x)=\frac{U_2(x)}{U_2(1)}=\frac{1-3x^2}{-2}=\frac{3x^2-1}{2}=\frac12(3x^2-1) $$

Indeed, $P_2(x)=\dfrac12(3x^2-1)$, as the exercise states. (That $P_2(1)=\frac12(3-1)=1$ is also evidence that the normalization was carried out correctly. We recomputed $U_2(x)$ from the recurrence relation with SymPy and verified that the results agree.)

Exercise 40.2: Computing $P_4(x)$ from Rodrigues's formula

Solution

Recall Rodrigues's formula:

$$ P_n(x)=\frac{1}{2^n n!}\diff{^n}{x^n}(x^2-1)^n $$

For $n=4$, first expand $(x^2-1)^4$ with the binomial theorem (using $\binom40=1,\binom41=4,\binom42=6,\binom43=4,\binom44=1$).

$$ (x^2-1)^4=x^8-4x^6+6x^4-4x^2+1 $$

Differentiate this four times in succession, one differentiation at a time. It suffices to differentiate each term according to $x^m\to mx^{m-1}$ (the power rule for differentiation).

First differentiation:

$$ \diff{}{x}(x^2-1)^4=8x^7-24x^5+24x^3-8x $$

Second differentiation (we differentiate the result of the first differentiation once more, term by term):

$$ \diff{^2}{x^2}(x^2-1)^4=56x^6-120x^4+72x^2-8 $$

Third differentiation:

$$ \diff{^3}{x^3}(x^2-1)^4=336x^5-480x^3+144x $$

Fourth differentiation:

$$ \diff{^4}{x^4}(x^2-1)^4=1680x^4-1440x^2+144 $$

In the computation so far, the coefficient of each term has always been just multiplied by the “current degree” while the degree is “lowered by one,” and this is repeated four times (for example, the $x^8$ term goes $8x^7\to56x^6\to336x^5\to1680x^4$, and its coefficient changes as $8\to8\cdot7=56\to56\cdot6=336\to336\cdot5=1680$).

Finally, divide by $2^4\cdot4!=16\cdot24=384$.

$$ P_4(x)=\frac{1680x^4-1440x^2+144}{384} $$

Reduce the fractions one term at a time. The greatest common divisor of $1680$ and $384$ is $48$, so $1680/384=35/8$. The greatest common divisor of $1440$ and $384$ is $96$, so $1440/384=15/4=30/8$. The greatest common divisor of $144$ and $384$ is $48$, so $144/384=3/8$. Therefore

$$ P_4(x)=\frac{35}{8}x^4-\frac{30}{8}x^2+\frac{3}{8}=\frac18(35x^4-30x^2+3) $$

and this indeed agrees exactly with $P_4(x)=\dfrac18(35x^4-30x^2+3)$, which we obtained in Example 40.7 by normalizing $U_4(x)$. We have confirmed that two completely different methods of computation (the series method with normalization, and Rodrigues's formula) arrive at the same answer. (We computed the fourth derivative of $(x^2-1)^4$ directly with SymPy and verified that the results agree.)

Exercise 40.3: Checking that $V_7(x)$ terminates as a polynomial for $n=7$ (odd)

Solution

Substitute $n=7$ and $a_1=1$ into the recurrence relation $a_{k+2}=-\dfrac{(n-k)(n+k+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}a_k$ in the order $k=1,3,5,7$.

$k=1$: $a_3=-\dfrac{(7-1)(7+2)}{2\cdot3}a_1=-\dfrac{6\cdot9}{6}\cdot1=-9$.

$k=3$: $a_5=-\dfrac{(7-3)(7+4)}{4\cdot5}a_3=-\dfrac{4\cdot11}{20}\cdot(-9)=\dfrac{44}{20}\cdot9=\dfrac{11}{5}\cdot9=\dfrac{99}{5}$.

$k=5$: $a_7=-\dfrac{(7-5)(7+6)}{6\cdot7}a_5=-\dfrac{2\cdot13}{42}\cdot\dfrac{99}{5}=-\dfrac{13}{21}\cdot\dfrac{99}{5}=-\dfrac{1287}{105}=-\dfrac{429}{35}$ (we reduced $1287$ and $105$ by their greatest common divisor $3$).

$k=7$: $a_9=-\dfrac{(7-7)(7+8)}{8\cdot9}a_7=-\dfrac{0\cdot15}{72}a_7=0$. We have arrived exactly at $k=n=7$, and the numerator factor $(n-k)=(7-7)=0$ appears, so $a_9=0$. Since the recurrence relation merely builds $a_{k+2}$ as a constant multiple of $a_k$, the subsequent coefficients $a_{11},a_{13},\ldots$ all remain $0$ as well, being constant multiples of $a_9=0$.

It follows that

$$ V_7(x)=x-9x^3+\frac{99}{5}x^5-\frac{429}{35}x^7 $$

is a polynomial of degree 7 that ends at the $x^7$ term. This is indeed the result that Theorem 40.4 asserts: when $n=7$ (odd), $V_n(x)$ becomes a polynomial, while $U_n(x)$ remains an infinite series. (Since $k=n=7$ appeared in the odd-indexed sequence $k=1,3,5,7,\ldots$, it is the odd-indexed sequence, that is, the $V_n(x)$ side, that stops.) (We recomputed the coefficients from the recurrence relation with SymPy, and we also substituted $V_7(x)$ directly into Legendre's differential equation $(1-x^2)y''-2xy'+56y=0$ ($n(n+1)=7\cdot8=56$) and verified that it becomes identically $0$.)

Exercise 40.4: Checking orthogonality in a concrete case ($m=1$, $n=2$)

Solution

Use $P_1(x)=x$ and $P_2(x)=\dfrac12(3x^2-1)$; form the product first, and then integrate.

$$ P_1(x)P_2(x)=x\cdot\frac12(3x^2-1)=\frac12(3x^3-x) $$

Integrate this over the interval $[-1,1]$. Applying the integration formula for power functions, $\displaystyle\int x^m\,dx=\dfrac{x^{m+1}}{m+1}$ ($m\neq-1$), to each term, we get

$$ \int_{-1}^{1}P_1(x)P_2(x)\,dx=\frac12\int_{-1}^{1}(3x^3-x)\,dx=\frac12\left[\frac{3x^4}{4}-\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_{-1}^{1} $$

Substitute $x=1$ and $x=-1$ into the brackets. Since $x^4$ and $x^2$ are both even powers, they take the same value at $x=1$ and at $x=-1$.

$$ \left.\left(\frac{3x^4}{4}-\frac{x^2}{2}\right)\right|_{x=1}=\frac34-\frac12=\frac14,\qquad \left.\left(\frac{3x^4}{4}-\frac{x^2}{2}\right)\right|_{x=-1}=\frac34-\frac12=\frac14 $$

Therefore

$$ \int_{-1}^{1}P_1(x)P_2(x)\,dx=\frac12\left(\frac14-\frac14\right)=\frac12\cdot0=0 $$

and we have indeed verified that the integral equals $0$, as Theorem 40.6 states. (Seen from another viewpoint, $P_1(x)P_2(x)=\frac12(3x^3-x)$ consists only of odd-degree terms and is therefore an odd function (a function satisfying $f(-x)=-f(x)$), and the integral of an odd function over a symmetric interval such as $[-1,1]$ is always $0$, a fact learned in high school; from this fact the same conclusion follows immediately. We integrated directly with SymPy and verified that the results agree.)

Exercise 40.5: Checking for yourself the error on p. 62 of the source notes (the numerator of $U_n(x)$)

Solution

Take the form of the recurrence relation before it has been factored,

$$ a_{k+2}=\frac{k(k+1)-n(n+1)}{(k+1)(k+2)}a_k $$

and substitute $k=0$ into it, keeping $n$ as a symbol rather than substituting a number for it.

$$ a_2=\frac{0\cdot1-n(n+1)}{1\cdot2}a_0=\frac{-n(n+1)}{2}a_0=-\frac{n(n+1)}{2!}a_0 $$

Therefore the coefficient of $x^2$ in $U_n(x)=a_0+a_2x^2+\cdots$ (the ratio when $a_0$ is taken to be 1) is $-\dfrac{n(n+1)}{2!}$, and its numerator is indeed $n(n+1)$. The statement “$n(n-1)$” on p. 62 of the source notes disagrees with this computation, so we see that it is an error.

As a precaution, let us also find $a_4/a_0$ (the coefficient of $x^4$ in $U_n(x)$) in the same way, by substituting $k=2$ and again leaving the result in symbolic form.

$$ a_4=\frac{2\cdot3-n(n+1)}{3\cdot4}a_2=\frac{6-n(n+1)}{12}a_2 $$

Here the numerator factors as $6-n(n+1)=-(n^2+n-6)=-(n-2)(n+3)$ (the two roots of $n^2+n-6$ are $n=2,-3$, so it factors as $(n-2)(n+3)$). Therefore

$$ a_4=\frac{-(n-2)(n+3)}{12}a_2=\frac{-(n-2)(n+3)}{12}\cdot\left(-\frac{n(n+1)}{2}a_0\right)=\frac{n(n-2)(n+1)(n+3)}{24}a_0=\frac{n(n-2)(n+1)(n+3)}{4!}a_0 $$

and this too agrees exactly with the numerator $n(n-2)(n+1)(n+3)$ of the coefficient of $x^4$ in $U_n(x)$, the expression used in Sections 40.2.1 and 40.3 of this chapter. Thus the computations of $a_2,a_4$ alike show, together with the concrete derivation, that the numerators of the coefficients of $U_n(x)$ are $n(n+1)$ (and $n(n-2)(n+1)(n+3)$), and not “$n(n-1)$” as on p. 62 of the source notes. (We checked the computation with SymPy by substituting with $n$ left as a symbolic variable, and verified that the results agree.)

Exercise 40.6: Checking that $P_n(1)=1$ holds for all $n$

Solution

Explanation from the definition. In Definition 40.3, we defined $P_n(x)=\dfrac{U_n(x)}{U_n(1)}$ when $n$ is even and $P_n(x)=\dfrac{V_n(x)}{V_n(1)}$ when $n$ is odd. In either case, when we substitute $x=1$, the numerator and the denominator take exactly the same value. For example, when $n$ is even, we get

$$ P_n(1)=\frac{U_n(1)}{U_n(1)}=1 $$

(Since $U_n(1)\neq0$, this division always makes sense.) When $n$ is odd, $V_n(1)/V_n(1)=1$ in exactly the same way. In other words, $P_n(1)=1$ is a fact that is obvious without any computation, from the very construction in Definition 40.3, in which $P_n(x)$ was defined “by normalizing through division so that the value at $x=1$ is exactly $1$.”

Explanation from a by-product of the proof of Rodrigues's formula. In Steps 2 and 3 of the proof of Formula 40.1, letting $w(x)=v^{(n)}(x)=\diff{^n}{x^n}(x^2-1)^n$, we showed that there exists a constant $c_n$ such that

$$ w(x)=c_n\,P_n(x) $$

and that the direct computation in Step 3 using Leibniz's rule gave the result

$$ w(1)=n!\,2^n $$

and hence that $c_n=n!\,2^n$. Combining these, we obtain the relation

$$ w(1)=c_n\,P_n(1)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad n!\,2^n=n!\,2^n\cdot P_n(1) $$

Here $n!\,2^n\neq0$ (both $n!$ and $2^n$ are positive real numbers and can never be $0$), so we can divide both sides by $n!\,2^n$, and we get

$$ P_n(1)=\frac{n!\,2^n}{n!\,2^n}=1 $$

Along this route as well, the same conclusion $P_n(1)=1$ is reached. The explanation from the definition rests on the algebraically trivial reason “of course, because we defined it that way,” whereas the explanation from Rodrigues's formula is a check that includes the nontriviality of the agreement: even by passing through a completely different method of computation (differentiation, and evaluation by Leibniz's rule at the endpoint $x=1$), we arrive at the same value $1$. (With SymPy we computed $w(1)/(2^n n!)$ for $n=0,1,\ldots,8$ and verified that all of them equal $1$.)

← Back to Chapter 40, “Legendre's Differential Equation and Legendre Polynomials”

Chapter 41 Special Functions — Bessel Functions and Laguerre Polynomials

Exercise 41.1: The coefficients $a_2,a_4$ for $\alpha=3$

Solution

The coefficients on the $\lambda=\alpha$ side of Bessel's differential equation obey the recurrence relation

$$ a_{k+2}=\frac{-a_k}{(k+2)(k+2+2\alpha)} $$

Substituting $\alpha=3$ gives $a_{k+2}=\dfrac{-a_k}{(k+2)(k+8)}$.

$k=0$: since $(0+2)(0+8)=16$, we have

$$ a_2=-\frac{a_0}{16} $$

$k=2$: since $(2+2)(2+8)=4\times10=40$, we obtain

$$ a_4=-\frac{a_2}{40}=-\frac{1}{40}\times\left(-\frac{a_0}{16}\right)=\frac{a_0}{640} $$

On the other hand, substituting $\alpha=3$ into the general formula $a_{2n}=\dfrac{(-1)^n}{n!\,2^{2n}(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)\cdots(\alpha+n)}a_0$ gives

$n=1$: $a_2=\dfrac{-1}{1!\times4\times4}a_0=-\dfrac{a_0}{16}$

$n=2$: $a_4=\dfrac{1}{2!\times16\times(4\times5)}a_0=\dfrac{1}{2\times16\times20}a_0=\dfrac{a_0}{640}$

Both results agree exactly with those obtained by following the recurrence relation directly (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 41.2: Finding $\Gamma(9/2)$ from $\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt\pi$

Solution

Use the recurrence relation $\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)$ of the gamma function four times, for $z=1/2,3/2,5/2,7/2$ in this order.

$$ \Gamma\!\left(\frac32\right)=\frac12\Gamma\!\left(\frac12\right)=\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2} $$ $$ \Gamma\!\left(\frac52\right)=\frac32\Gamma\!\left(\frac32\right)=\frac32\times\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2}=\frac{3\sqrt\pi}{4} $$ $$ \Gamma\!\left(\frac72\right)=\frac52\Gamma\!\left(\frac52\right)=\frac52\times\frac{3\sqrt\pi}{4}=\frac{15\sqrt\pi}{8} $$ $$ \Gamma\!\left(\frac92\right)=\frac72\Gamma\!\left(\frac72\right)=\frac72\times\frac{15\sqrt\pi}{8}=\frac{105\sqrt\pi}{16} $$

Therefore $\Gamma(9/2)=\dfrac{105}{16}\sqrt\pi$ (we have verified that this agrees with the value of $\Gamma(9/2)$ computed directly with SymPy).

Exercise 41.3: Checking linear dependence for $n=3$ (odd)

Solution

For $J_3(x)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^k}{k!\,(3+k)!}\left(\dfrac x2\right)^{2k+3}$, the first two terms ($k=0,1$) are

$$ k=0:\ \frac{1}{0!\,3!}\left(\frac x2\right)^3=\frac{1}{6}\cdot\frac{x^3}{8}=\frac{x^3}{48} $$ $$ k=1:\ \frac{-1}{1!\,4!}\left(\frac x2\right)^5=-\frac{1}{24}\cdot\frac{x^5}{32}=-\frac{x^5}{768} $$

On the other hand, substituting $n=3$ into the relation $J_{-n}(x)=(-1)^nJ_n(x)$, which holds in general, and noting that $(-1)^3=-1$, we obtain

$$ J_{-3}(x)=-J_3(x) $$

This is because, as a result of the reindexing carried out in the main text (we start the sum for $J_{-n}(x)$ at $k=n$, replace the index by $k=n+\ell$, and separate the sign as $(-1)^{n+\ell}=(-1)^n(-1)^\ell$), the sign $(-1)^n=-1$ comes out in front when $n=3$ (odd). For $n=2$ (Example 41.4) we have $(-1)^2=+1$, so $J_{-2}=J_2$ and no sign appeared, whereas for $n=3$ the sign becomes negative; this is the difference that arises. Indeed, comparing the series expansion of besselj(-3,x) (SymPy's standard Bessel function) with that of $-J_3(x)$ up to the 10th term, we confirmed that they agree completely.

Exercise 41.4: Checking that the radius of convergence is $\infty$ even when the order is negative

Solution

Substituting $\alpha=-\dfrac13$ into the expression $(n+1)(\alpha+n+1)$ in the main text gives $(n+1)\left(n+\dfrac23\right)$. Substituting $n=5,10,50$ into this, we obtain

$$ n=5:\ (5+1)\left(5+\frac23\right)=6\times\frac{17}{3}=34 $$ $$ n=10:\ (10+1)\left(10+\frac23\right)=11\times\frac{32}{3}=\frac{352}{3}\approx117.3 $$ $$ n=50:\ (50+1)\left(50+\frac23\right)=51\times\frac{152}{3}=2584 $$

The value grows larger and larger as $n$ increases ($34\to117.3\to2584$). In fact, $(n+1)(\alpha+n+1)$ grows on the order of $n^2$ as $n\to\infty$, so, regardless of the sign or size of $\alpha$, the limit is always $+\infty$. Therefore the radius of convergence is $R=\infty$ for $\alpha=-1/3$ as well.

Exercise 41.5: Finding the Laguerre polynomial $L_4(x)$

Solution

Use the recurrence relation $a_{k+1}=-\dfrac{4-k}{(k+1)^2}a_k$ ($m=4$, $a_0=1$) successively.

$$ a_1=-\frac{4-0}{1^2}a_0=-4,\qquad a_2=-\frac{4-1}{2^2}a_1=-\frac34\times(-4)=3 $$ $$ a_3=-\frac{4-2}{3^2}a_2=-\frac29\times3=-\frac23,\qquad a_4=-\frac{4-3}{4^2}a_3=-\frac{1}{16}\times\left(-\frac23\right)=\frac{1}{24} $$

(Since $a_5=-\dfrac{4-4}{5^2}a_4=0$, the recurrence stops here.) Hence

$$ L_4(x)=1-4x+3x^2-\frac23x^3+\frac{1}{24}x^4 $$

Next we evaluate Rodrigues's formula $\widehat L_4(x)=e^x\dfrac{d^4}{dx^4}(x^4e^{-x})$. Using the rule for differentiating a product (Leibniz's rule), we obtain

$$ \widehat L_4(x)=x^4-16x^3+72x^2-96x+24 $$

(this was verified by computing $\dfrac{d^4}{dx^4}(x^4e^{-x})$ with SymPy and multiplying by $e^x$). Dividing this by $4!=24$ gives

$$ \frac{\widehat L_4(x)}{24}=\frac{x^4}{24}-\frac{16}{24}x^3+\frac{72}{24}x^2-\frac{96}{24}x+1 =\frac{1}{24}x^4-\frac23x^3+3x^2-4x+1 $$

which is exactly the same as the $L_4(x)$ found above (with the terms listed in reverse order). That is, we have confirmed that $\widehat L_4(x)=4!\,L_4(x)$ (the exact agreement of the coefficients was verified with SymPy).

Exercise 41.6: Finding the associated Laguerre polynomial $L_2^1(x)$

Solution

First we find $\widehat L_2(x)$. $L_2(x)$ is obtained from the defining formula $L_m(x)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\dfrac{(-1)^km!}{(k!)^2(m-k)!}x^k$ by substituting $m=2$:

$$ L_2(x)=\frac{2!}{(0!)^2\,2!}-\frac{2!}{(1!)^2\,1!}x+\frac{2!}{(2!)^2\,0!}x^2=1-2x+\frac12x^2 $$

Therefore $\widehat L_2(x)=2!\,L_2(x)=2-4x+x^2=x^2-4x+2$. Differentiating this once, we obtain

$$ L_2^1(x)=\frac{d}{dx}\widehat L_2(x)=\frac{d}{dx}(x^2-4x+2)=2x-4 $$

Next, we check this result using the series representation $L_m^N(x)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=N}^{m}\dfrac{(-1)^k(m!)^2}{k!\,(m-k)!\,(k-N)!}x^{k-N}$ of the associated Laguerre polynomial, substituting $m=2,N=1$ directly. The sum consists of the two terms $k=1,2$.

$$ k=1:\ \frac{(-1)^1(2!)^2}{1!\,1!\,0!}x^0=-4,\qquad k=2:\ \frac{(-1)^2(2!)^2}{2!\,0!\,1!}x^1=2x $$

Adding them up gives $L_2^1(x)=2x-4$, which agrees exactly with the result found earlier by differentiation (verified with SymPy).

← Back to Chapter 41, “Special Functions — Bessel Functions and Laguerre Polynomials”

Chapter 42 Introduction to Partial Differential Equations and Lagrange's First-Order Partial Differential Equation

Exercise 42.1: The simplest Lagrange partial differential equation

Solution

Lagrange's partial differential equation $\pdiff zx-\pdiff zy=0$ is the case $P=1,Q=-1,R=0$. The characteristic equations are

$$ \frac{dx}{1}=\frac{dy}{-1}=\frac{dz}{0} $$

Integrating the first two, $dx=-dy$ (each side can be integrated as it stands), we obtain $x=-y+C_1'$, that is,

$$ x+y=C_1 $$

which is a first integral. On the other hand, the last fraction $dz/0$ was to be read according to the convention that “when the denominator of a ratio is $0$, the corresponding variable ($z$) does not change.” That is,

$$ z=C_2 $$

Imposing the relation $\psi=f(\varphi)$ ($f$ is an arbitrary function) on the two independent first integrals $\varphi=x+y$ and $\psi=z$, we obtain

$$ z=f(x+y) $$

This is the general solution.

Cross-check. Differentiating $z=f(x+y)$ partially with the chain rule gives $\pdiff zx=f'(x+y)$ and $\pdiff zy=f'(x+y)$, so

$$ \pdiff zx-\pdiff zy=f'(x+y)-f'(x+y)=0 $$

and the original equation is indeed satisfied (verified with SymPy).

Interpretation as a traveling wave. If we regard $y$ as playing the role of the time $t$ in Section 42.5, the original equation $\pdiff zx-\pdiff zy=0$ can be written as $\pdiff zy=\pdiff zx$, that is, $\pdiff zt=1\cdot\pdiff zx$. This is exactly the equation $\pdiff ut=v_0\pdiff ux$ for the leftward-traveling wave that we saw in Section 42.5, in the case $v_0=1$. Indeed, the value of the general solution $z=f(x+y)$ does not change under the condition $x+y=(\text{constant})$, so when $y$ (= time) increases, $x$ must decrease by the same amount in order to keep the value constant—that is, this solution represents a wave traveling in the negative direction of the $x$-axis with speed $1$.

Exercise 42.2: A Lagrange partial differential equation with coefficients containing $z$

Solution

Lagrange's partial differential equation $xz\pdiff zx-yz\pdiff zy=x^2-y^2$ is the case $P=xz,Q=-yz,R=x^2-y^2$. The characteristic equations are

$$ \frac{dx}{xz}=\frac{dy}{-yz}=\frac{dz}{x^2-y^2} $$

First, from the first two fractions, canceling the common factor $z$ gives $\dfrac{dx}{x}=\dfrac{dy}{-y}$ (assuming $z\ne0$). This is of separable form, and integrating gives $\ln x=-\ln y+C_1'$, that is,

$$ xy=C_1 $$

which is a first integral (this is the same computation as in Illustration 42.1).

To construct another first integral, we use the method of multipliers explained in the main text (at the end of Section 42.3), because the variables cannot be separated by pairing up the three fractions directly. Using the property of proportions, “if $\dfrac ab=\dfrac cd=\dfrac ef$, then $\dfrac{\lambda a+\mu c+\nu e}{\lambda b+\mu d+\nu f}$ is also equal to the same value for any $\lambda,\mu,\nu$,” we choose $\lambda=x,\mu=y,\nu=z$ (the variables themselves) as the multipliers. Then the denominator of the new fraction is

$$ x\cdot(xz)+y\cdot(-yz)+z\cdot(x^2-y^2)=x^2z-y^2z+x^2z-y^2z=2z(x^2-y^2) $$

and the numerator becomes $x\,dx+y\,dy+z\,dz$. Setting this new fraction equal to the original third fraction $\dfrac{dz}{x^2-y^2}$, we get

$$ \frac{x\,dx+y\,dy+z\,dz}{2z(x^2-y^2)}=\frac{dz}{x^2-y^2} $$

Multiplying both sides by $2z(x^2-y^2)$ (assuming $x^2-y^2\ne0$),

$$ x\,dx+y\,dy+z\,dz=2z\,dz\qquad\therefore\ x\,dx+y\,dy=z\,dz $$

This is an equation of separable form. Integrating both sides (with $\int x\,dx=x^2/2$, $\int y\,dy=y^2/2$, $\int z\,dz=z^2/2$),

$$ \frac{x^2}{2}+\frac{y^2}{2}=\frac{z^2}{2}+K\qquad\therefore\ x^2+y^2-z^2=C_2\quad(C_2=2K) $$

we obtain the second first integral. Imposing on $C_1=xy$ and $C_2=x^2+y^2-z^2$ the relation $C_2=\varphi(C_1)$ ($\varphi$ is an arbitrary function), the general solution is given as

$$ x^2+y^2-z^2=\varphi(xy) $$

in implicit form (solving explicitly for $z$ gives $z=\pm\sqrt{x^2+y^2-\varphi(xy)}$, but because an ambiguity of sign remains, it is natural to leave it in implicit form).

Cross-check. Regarding the relation as the implicit function $G(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2-z^2-\varphi(xy)=0$ and using the implicit function theorem $\pdiff zx=-\dfrac{\partial G/\partial x}{\partial G/\partial z}$, $\pdiff zy=-\dfrac{\partial G/\partial y}{\partial G/\partial z}$ (Chapter 6), one can confirm that $xz\pdiff zx-yz\pdiff zy-(x^2-y^2)$ is identically $0$, regardless of the form of $\varphi$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 42.3: An exact differential equation (two variables)

Solution

Here $P=y^2+2xy$ and $Q=x^2+2xy$. Checking the exactness condition,

$$ \pdiff Py=2y+2x,\qquad \pdiff Qx=2x+2y $$

the two agree, so this is an exact differential equation. To find the general solution, we first find $f$ satisfying $\pdiff fx=P$ by integrating with respect to $x$ ($y$ is treated as a constant).

$$ f=\int(y^2+2xy)\,dx=xy^2+x^2y+h(y) $$

(the constant of integration may be taken to be a function $h(y)$ of $y$). Next, we determine $h(y)$ from the condition $\pdiff fy=Q$.

$$ \pdiff fy=2xy+x^2+h'(y)=Q=x^2+2xy $$

Comparing, we get $h'(y)=0$, that is, $h$ is a constant (it is eventually absorbed into $C$, so it can be omitted). Therefore

$$ f(x,y)=xy^2+x^2y=xy(x+y) $$

and so we obtain

$$ xy(x+y)=C $$

This is the general solution.

Cross-check. We get $\pdiff fx=2xy+y^2=P$ and $\pdiff fy=x^2+2xy=Q$, which indeed agree with the original $P,Q$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 42.4: Superposition of two traveling waves

Solution

We have $u(x,t)=A\sin(kx-\omega t)+B\sin(kx+\omega t)$. The first term $A\sin(kx-\omega t)$ is a wave traveling to the right with speed $v_0=\omega/k$, and (by the discussion in Section 42.5.3 of this chapter) it satisfies $\pdiff{^2}{t^2}\bigl[A\sin(kx-\omega t)\bigr]=v_0^2\pdiff{^2}{x^2}\bigl[A\sin(kx-\omega t)\bigr]$. The second term $B\sin(kx+\omega t)$ likewise satisfies the same wave equation, as a wave traveling to the left with speed $v_0=\omega/k$. Since partial differentiation is a linear operation (the derivative of a sum is the sum of the derivatives), for the sum $u$ of the two terms, too, the same wave equation

$$ \pdiff{^2u}{t^2}=\left(\frac\omega k\right)^2\pdiff{^2u}{x^2} $$

holds.

Direct check. To be safe, let us verify this by differentiating directly.

$$ \pdiff ut=-A\omega\cos(kx-\omega t)+B\omega\cos(kx+\omega t) $$ $$ \pdiff{^2u}{t^2}=-A\omega^2\sin(kx-\omega t)-B\omega^2\sin(kx+\omega t) $$ $$ \pdiff ux=Ak\cos(kx-\omega t)+Bk\cos(kx+\omega t) $$ $$ \pdiff{^2u}{x^2}=-Ak^2\sin(kx-\omega t)-Bk^2\sin(kx+\omega t) $$

Therefore

$$ \left(\frac\omega k\right)^2\pdiff{^2u}{x^2}=\frac{\omega^2}{k^2}\Bigl[-Ak^2\sin(kx-\omega t)-Bk^2\sin(kx+\omega t)\Bigr]=-A\omega^2\sin(kx-\omega t)-B\omega^2\sin(kx+\omega t)=\pdiff{^2u}{t^2} $$

and the wave equation is indeed satisfied (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 42.5: Generalization to three variables

Solution

The equation $x_1\pdiff u{x_1}+x_2\pdiff u{x_2}+x_3\pdiff u{x_3}=0$ is the case of Definition 42.2 with $n=3$, $f_1=x_1,f_2=x_2,f_3=x_3$, and $g=0$. The characteristic equations are

$$ \frac{dx_1}{x_1}=\frac{dx_2}{x_2}=\frac{dx_3}{x_3} $$

Integrating the first two, $\dfrac{dx_1}{x_1}=\dfrac{dx_2}{x_2}$, gives $\ln x_1=\ln x_2+C_1'$, that is,

$$ \frac{x_2}{x_1}=e^{-C_1'}=C_1 $$

(since $x_1,x_2,x_3\gt0$, absolute-value signs are unnecessary). Similarly, from the first and the last, $\dfrac{dx_1}{x_1}=\dfrac{dx_3}{x_3}$, we get

$$ \frac{x_3}{x_1}=C_2 $$

as two independent first integrals (since $n=3$, Theorem 42.2 requires $n=3$ first integrals, but when $g=0$ the fraction $du/0$ gives the third first integral $u=C_3$, which says “$u$ does not change”). Imposing on these three first integrals $C_1=x_2/x_1$, $C_2=x_3/x_1$, $C_3=u$ a relation $C_3=\Phi(C_1,C_2)$ through an arbitrary function $\Phi$, the general solution takes the form

$$ u=\Phi\!\left(\frac{x_2}{x_1},\frac{x_3}{x_1}\right) $$

(here $\Phi$ is an arbitrary function of two variables).

Cross-check. If we use the chain rule (the multivariable version of the formula for differentiating a composite function) to differentiate $u=\Phi(x_2/x_1,x_3/x_1)$ partially with respect to each of $x_1,x_2,x_3$ and substitute the results into $x_1u_{x_1}+x_2u_{x_2}+x_3u_{x_3}$, we can confirm that it becomes identically $0$, regardless of the form of $\Phi$ and its partial derivatives (verified with SymPy). For example, even if we choose $\Phi(s,t)=s^2+t$ concretely, we can check that $u=(x_2/x_1)^2+x_3/x_1$ satisfies the equation.

Exercise 42.6: A simple example with a boundary condition

Solution

We integrate $\pdiff ux=2y$ with respect to $x$, regarding $y$ as a constant.

$$ u=\int2y\,dx=2xy+f(y) $$

(the constant of integration may be any function of $y$, so we denoted it by an arbitrary function $f(y)$). This is the general solution. Substituting the boundary condition $u(0,y)=y^2$,

$$ u(0,y)=2\cdot0\cdot y+f(y)=f(y)=y^2 $$

we find that $f(y)=y^2$. Therefore,

$$ u(x,y)=2xy+y^2 $$

is the particular solution satisfying the boundary condition.

Cross-check. We get $\pdiff ux=2y$, so the equation is satisfied. Also $u(0,y)=2\cdot0\cdot y+y^2=y^2$, so the boundary condition is satisfied as well (verified with SymPy).
