Chapter 43 The Wave Equation and the Heat Equation — Derivation and Separation of Variables

Exercise 43.1: Dimension of the speed in the wave equation

Solution

The unit of the tension $T$ is the unit of force, $\mathrm N$, and from the equation of motion $F=ma$ (mass × acceleration) this is $\mathrm N=\mathrm{kg}\cdot\mathrm{m/s^2}$. The unit of the linear density $\rho$ is $\mathrm{kg/m}$, since it is mass per unit length. The unit of the quantity $T/\rho$ inside $v=\sqrt{T/\rho}$ can simply be computed as a fraction.

$$ \frac{[T]}{[\rho]}=\frac{\mathrm{kg\cdot m/s^2}}{\mathrm{kg/m}}=\mathrm{kg\cdot m/s^2}\times\frac{\mathrm m}{\mathrm{kg}}=\frac{\mathrm m^2}{\mathrm s^2} $$

(The $\mathrm{kg}$ cancels between the numerator and the denominator, and the factors of $\mathrm m$ multiply to give $\mathrm m^2$.) Therefore the unit of $v=\sqrt{T/\rho}$ is

$$ [v]=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm m^2}{\mathrm s^2}}=\frac{\mathrm m}{\mathrm s} $$

and indeed this is the unit of speed ($\mathrm{m/s}$). This is also a condition required for the two sides of the wave equation $\pdiff{^2u}{x^2}=\dfrac1{v^2}\pdiff{^2u}{t^2}$ to have the same dimension: the dimension of the left-hand side is $[u]/\mathrm m^2$, and that of the right-hand side is $(\mathrm s^2/\mathrm m^2)\times([u]/\mathrm s^2)=[u]/\mathrm m^2$, and the fact that the unit of $v$ is $\mathrm{m/s}$ guarantees this agreement (verified with SymPy by treating the base units $\mathrm{kg,m,s}$ as symbols, which gives $[T/\rho]=\mathrm m^2/\mathrm s^2$).

Exercise 43.2: Dimension of the thermal diffusivity in the heat equation

Solution

Write the given units in the form of fractions.

$$ [k]=\frac{\mathrm{kg\cdot m}}{\mathrm{s^3\cdot K}},\qquad[\rho]=\frac{\mathrm{kg}}{\mathrm{m^3}},\qquad[C]=\frac{\mathrm{m^2}}{\mathrm{s^2\cdot K}} $$

First compute $[\rho][C]$, which appears in the denominator.

$$ [\rho][C]=\frac{\mathrm{kg}}{\mathrm{m^3}}\times\frac{\mathrm{m^2}}{\mathrm{s^2\cdot K}}=\frac{\mathrm{kg}}{\mathrm{m\cdot s^2\cdot K}} $$

(One factor of $\mathrm m$ remains from $\mathrm{m^2}$ and $\mathrm{m^3}$.) Next, compute the unit of $a=k/(\rho C)$.

$$ [a]=\frac{[k]}{[\rho][C]}=\frac{\mathrm{kg\cdot m}/(\mathrm{s^3\cdot K})}{\mathrm{kg}/(\mathrm{m\cdot s^2\cdot K})}=\frac{\mathrm{kg\cdot m}}{\mathrm{s^3\cdot K}}\times\frac{\mathrm{m\cdot s^2\cdot K}}{\mathrm{kg}}=\frac{\mathrm{m^2}}{\mathrm s} $$

(The $\mathrm{kg}$ and the $\mathrm K$ each cancel, and $\mathrm s^3/\mathrm s^2=\mathrm s$ remains in the denominator.) Therefore the unit of the thermal diffusivity $a$ is $\mathrm{m^2/s}$. This can also be confirmed from the fact that the dimension $[u]/\mathrm s$ of the left-hand side of the heat equation $\pdiff ut=a\pdiff{^2u}{x^2}$ agrees with the dimension $(\mathrm m^2/\mathrm s)\times([u]/\mathrm m^2)=[u]/\mathrm s$ of the right-hand side (verified with SymPy by treating the base units as symbols, which gives $[a]=\mathrm m^2/\mathrm s$).

Exercise 43.3: The wave equation when the initial condition is a sine wave of double frequency

Solution

The general solution is $u(x,t)=\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^\infty D_m\sin\dfrac{m\pi}Lx\cos\dfrac{m\pi}Lvt$. The initial condition $u(x,0)=h\sin\dfrac{2\pi x}L$ is itself the eigenfunction $\sin(2\pi x/L)$ for $m=2$. Using the orthogonality of the sine functions

$$ \int_0^L\sin\frac{2\pi x}L\sin\frac{m\pi x}Ldx=\frac L2\delta_{2m} $$

(this equals $L/2$ only when $m=2$, and $0$ otherwise) in the formula for the Fourier coefficients, we obtain

$$ D_m=\frac2L\int_0^Lh\sin\frac{2\pi x}L\sin\frac{m\pi x}Ldx=\begin{cases}h&(m=2)\\0&(m\ne2)\end{cases} $$

Therefore only the $m=2$ term of the series survives, and

$$ u(x,t)=h\sin\frac{2\pi x}L\cos\frac{2\pi vt}L $$

is the required solution. Compared with Example 43.4 (the case $m=1$), there is one more node (a point that does not oscillate, where $\sin$ is $0$) in the spatial direction, at $x=L/2$, and the angular frequency in the time direction is also doubled, from $\pi v/L$ to $2\pi v/L$—this is a concrete instance of the general relation that when the wavelength is halved, the frequency doubles (verified with SymPy: direct integration confirms that $D_2=h$ for $m=2$ and $D_m=0$ for $m=1,3,4,5$).

Exercise 43.4: The heat equation when the initial temperature is uniform

Solution

The general solution is $U(x,t)=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n\,e^{-\frac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2}at}\sin\dfrac{n\pi}Lx$. Substituting $U(x,0)=T_0$ (a constant) into the formula for the Fourier sine coefficients, we obtain

$$ b_n=\frac2L\int_0^LT_0\sin\frac{n\pi}Lx\,dx=\frac2L\left[-\frac L{n\pi}T_0\cos\frac{n\pi}Lx\right]_0^L=\frac{2T_0}{n\pi}\bigl(1-\cos n\pi\bigr) $$

Since $\cos n\pi=(-1)^n$, we have $1-\cos n\pi=1-(-1)^n$, which is $0$ when $n$ is even and $2$ when odd. Therefore

$$ b_n=\begin{cases}\dfrac{4T_0}{n\pi}&(n\text{ odd})\\[4pt]0&(n\text{ even})\end{cases} $$

and the required solution is

$$ U(x,t)=\sum_{n=1,3,5,\ldots}^\infty\frac{4T_0}{n\pi}e^{-\frac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2}at}\sin\frac{n\pi}Lx $$

Here the initial condition is the constant value $T_0$ (at both ends, $U(0,0)=U(L,0)=T_0\ne0$), whereas the boundary conditions impose $U(0,t)=U(L,t)=0$ (for $t\gt0$ both ends are forced to be $0$); hence at the instant $t=0^+$ an abrupt change (a situation close to a discontinuity) occurs near both ends. For this reason, if the series is truncated after finitely many terms, the Gibbs phenomenon (overshoot) appears near both ends, just as in the case of the rectangular pulse in Section 43.6. However, for $t\gt0$ the exponential decay begins immediately, and as time passes the distribution becomes smoother (verified with SymPy: direct integration confirms that $b_n=4T_0/(n\pi)$ for $n=1,3,5,7$ and $b_n=0$ for $n=2,4,6,8$).

Exercise 43.5: The two-dimensional wave equation for a different normal mode

Solution

The general solution is $u(x,y,t)=\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}D_{mn}\sin m\pi x\sin n\pi y\cos v\sqrt{m^2+n^2}\,\pi t$. The initial condition $u(x,y,0)=\sin\pi x\sin2\pi y$ is itself the eigenfunction for $m=1,n=2$, so by the orthogonality of the double Fourier sine series,

$$ D_{mn}=\begin{cases}1&(m,n)=(1,2)\\0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases} $$

Noting that $m^2+n^2=1^2+2^2=5$, we find that the required solution is

$$ u(x,y,t)=\sin\pi x\sin2\pi y\cos\bigl(v\sqrt5\,\pi t\bigr) $$

Compared with Example 43.5 ($(m,n)=(1,1)$, angular frequency $v\sqrt2\,\pi$), the angular frequency is $\sqrt5/\sqrt2\approx1.58$ times as large—as the waves in the $y$ direction become “finer” ($n$ goes from $1$ to $2$), the frequency of the whole motion rises as well (verified with SymPy: direct integration confirms that $b_{12}=1$ for $(m,n)=(1,2)$, while all other $(m,n)\,(\le3)$ give $0$).

Exercise 43.6: Laplace's equation when the boundary condition is a quadratic function

Solution

The form of the general solution is the same as in Illustration 43.5 and Example 43.8: $u(x,y)=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n\sinh\!\left(\dfrac{n\pi}L(L-x)\right)\sin\dfrac{n\pi}Ly$. From the boundary condition $u(0,y)=y(L-y)$, the coefficients are determined by

$$ b_n\sinh(n\pi)=\frac2L\int_0^Ly(L-y)\sin\frac{n\pi}Ly\,dy $$

The integral on the right-hand side is computed by writing $y(L-y)=Ly-y^2$ and applying integration by parts twice (the same technique as in Sections 43.4 and 43.5). Finding an antiderivative and substituting the endpoints $y=0,L$ (note that $y(L-y)$ is $0$ at each of $y=0,L$), we obtain

$$ \int_0^Ly(L-y)\sin\frac{n\pi}Ly\,dy=\frac{2L^3\bigl(1-(-1)^n\bigr)}{n^3\pi^3} $$

Therefore

$$ b_n\sinh(n\pi)=\frac2L\cdot\frac{2L^3\bigl(1-(-1)^n\bigr)}{n^3\pi^3}=\frac{4L^2\bigl(1-(-1)^n\bigr)}{n^3\pi^3} $$

Since $1-(-1)^n$ is $2$ when $n$ is odd and $0$ when even,

$$ b_n=\begin{cases}\dfrac{8L^2}{n^3\pi^3\sinh(n\pi)}&(n\text{ odd})\\[4pt]0&(n\text{ even})\end{cases} $$

and the required solution is

$$ u(x,y)=\sum_{n=1,3,5,\ldots}^\infty\frac{8L^2}{n^3\pi^3\sinh(n\pi)}\sinh\!\left(\frac{n\pi}L(L-x)\right)\sin\frac{n\pi}Ly $$

The fact that the boundary function $y(L-y)$ is a parabola that is symmetric about the axis $y=L/2$ is the reason that only the odd-order terms appear in the Fourier series (the same as the symmetry argument seen in Illustration 43.4 and Example 43.8). Moreover, since the denominator contains both $n^3$ and $\sinh(n\pi)$, the series of Exercise 43.6 converges even faster than that of Example 43.8—this reflects the general property of Fourier analysis that the smoother the boundary condition ($y(L-y)$ is of class $C^\infty$), the faster the Fourier coefficients tend to $0$ (verified with SymPy: $(2/L)\int_0^Ly(L-y)\sin(n\pi y/L)dy=4L^2(1-(-1)^n)/(n^3\pi^3)$).

← Back to Chapter 43, “The Wave Equation and the Heat Equation — Derivation and Separation of Variables”

Chapter 44 Coordinate Transformations and the Laplacian

Exercise 44.1: $\nabla^2(r^2)$ in polar coordinates

Solution

Since $u=r^2$ does not contain $\theta$, we have $u_\theta=u_{\theta\theta}=0$. For $r$, $u_r=2r$ and $u_{rr}=2$. The Laplacian in polar coordinates is

$$ \nabla^2u=u_{rr}+\frac1r u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta} $$

Substituting these derivatives into it, we obtain

$$ \nabla^2u=2+\frac1r\cdot2r+\frac{1}{r^2}\cdot0=2+2=4 $$

On the other hand, in Cartesian coordinates $u=r^2=x^2+y^2$, so from $u_x=2x,\ u_{xx}=2$ and $u_y=2y,\ u_{yy}=2$ we get

$$ \nabla^2u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=2+2=4 $$

Thus we confirm that the same value $4$ is obtained whichever coordinate system we compute in (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 44.2: Harmonicity of $u=z$ in spherical coordinates

Solution

Since $u=r\cos\theta$ does not contain $\varphi$, we have $u_\varphi=u_{\varphi\varphi}=0$. For $r$, $u_r=\cos\theta$ and $u_{rr}=0$. For $\theta$, $u_\theta=-r\sin\theta$ and $u_{\theta\theta}=-r\cos\theta$. The Laplacian in spherical coordinates is

$$ \nabla^2u=u_{rr}+\frac2r u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}+\frac{\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}u_\theta+\frac{1}{r^2\sin^2\theta}u_{\varphi\varphi} $$

Substituting these derivatives into it, we obtain

$$ \nabla^2u=0+\frac2r\cos\theta+\frac{1}{r^2}(-r\cos\theta)+\frac{\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}(-r\sin\theta)+0 =\frac{2\cos\theta}{r}-\frac{\cos\theta}{r}-\frac{\cos\theta}{r}=0 $$

so the function indeed satisfies Laplace's equation. In Cartesian coordinates, $u=z$ and $u_{xx}=u_{yy}=u_{zz}=0$, so $\nabla^2u=0$ is obvious (verified with SymPy). Thus, even through spherical coordinates, a coordinate system that looks complicated at first sight, we have confirmed the same simple fact (by taking a detour).

Exercise 44.3: Reproduce the reason for the cylindrical-coordinate Laplacian

Solution

Cylindrical coordinates are given by the transformation $x=r\cos\theta,\ y=r\sin\theta,\ z=z$. The following two facts deserve attention here.

(i) $r,\theta$ do not depend on $z$ at all. Since $z$ appears nowhere in the expressions $r=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ and $\theta=\tan^{-1}(y/x)$, we have $\partial r/\partial z=0$ and $\partial\theta/\partial z=0$.

(ii) $z$ does not depend on $x,y$ at all. The relation $z=z$ itself means that $z$ is another coordinate, independent of $x,y$.

Because of these two facts, when we differentiate $u(x,y,z)=u\bigl(r(x,y),\theta(x,y),z\bigr)$ partially with respect to $x$ or $y$, $z$ is treated as a constant that does not change at all along the way, so the computation of $u_{xx},u_{yy}$ carried out in Section 44.2 remains valid as it stands (whether or not $z$ is present, if we regard $z$ as a mere parameter, the same chain-rule computation holds as it is). Conversely, when we differentiate $u$ partially with respect to $z$, we merely treat $r,\theta$ (and hence $x,y$) as constants, and this never mixes with the partial derivatives in the $r,\theta$ directions (since $r_z=\theta_z=0$, the chain rule gives $u_z=u_r r_z+u_\theta\theta_z+u_z\cdot1=u_z$, and $u_z$ contains no information at all from the $r,\theta$ directions).

In other words, in general, for a function such as $u(x,y,z)=f(x,y)+g(z)$, in which the part depending on $x,y$ and the part depending on $z$ are completely separated, we have

$$ \pdiff{^2u}{x^2}+\pdiff{^2u}{y^2}+\pdiff{^2u}{z^2}=\bigl(f_{xx}+f_{yy}\bigr)+g''(z) $$

As this shows, the second derivatives of the two parts are simply added without mixing (one may also think that mixed partial derivatives of $f,g$ such as $\partial^2/\partial x\partial z$ do not exist in the first place, that is, they are automatically $0$). For cylindrical coordinates the reason is essentially the same: of the three-dimensional Laplacian $u_{xx}+u_{yy}+u_{zz}$ in Cartesian coordinates, only the part $u_{xx}+u_{yy}$ is replaced wholesale by the polar-coordinate formula (Formula 44.1), while the part $u_{zz}$ remains as it is, unchanged (we have also verified with SymPy that this additivity holds for general functions of the form $f(x,y)+g(z)$).

Exercise 44.4: Complete the direct method for the spherical-coordinate Laplacian

Solution

We use the first-order partial derivatives found with the formulas in the main text:

$$ r_x=\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\qquad \theta_x=\frac{\cos\theta\cos\varphi}{r},\qquad \varphi_x=-\frac{\sin\varphi}{r\sin\theta} $$

First, differentiate $u_x=u_r r_x+u_\theta\theta_x+u_\varphi\varphi_x$ once more with respect to $x$. This time it is the chain rule for three variables, so applying the product rule to each of the three terms gives

$$ u_{xx}=\bigl(u_{rx}r_x+u_r r_{xx}\bigr)+\bigl(u_{\theta x}\theta_x+u_\theta\theta_{xx}\bigr)+\bigl(u_{\varphi x}\varphi_x+u_\varphi\varphi_{xx}\bigr) $$

where, regarding $u_r,u_\theta,u_\varphi$ as functions of $r,\theta,\varphi$ (in the same way as for $u$ itself) and applying the chain rule, we obtain for $u_{rx},u_{\theta x},u_{\varphi x}$, respectively,

$$ u_{rx}=u_{rr}r_x+u_{r\theta}\theta_x+u_{r\varphi}\varphi_x,\quad u_{\theta x}=u_{\theta r}r_x+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_x+u_{\theta\varphi}\varphi_x,\quad u_{\varphi x}=u_{\varphi r}r_x+u_{\varphi\theta}\theta_x+u_{\varphi\varphi}\varphi_x $$

(by Schwarz's theorem, $u_{r\theta}=u_{\theta r}$, $u_{r\varphi}=u_{\varphi r}$, $u_{\theta\varphi}=u_{\varphi\theta}$). Substituting all of these and rearranging, we obtain

$$ u_{xx}=u_{rr}r_x^2+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_x^2+u_{\varphi\varphi}\varphi_x^2+2u_{r\theta}r_x\theta_x+2u_{r\varphi}r_x\varphi_x+2u_{\theta\varphi}\theta_x\varphi_x+u_r r_{xx}+u_\theta\theta_{xx}+u_\varphi\varphi_{xx} $$

The result takes this form (the pattern is the same as in the expansion of a quadratic form; one may think of it as the formula for the two-variable case in Section 44.2 with the terms involving $\varphi$ added). All that remains is to compute the squares and products of $r_x,\theta_x,\varphi_x$ and the second-order derivatives $r_{xx},\theta_{xx},\varphi_{xx}$, and to substitute them. The second-order derivatives are obtained by the same procedure as in the main text (differentiating $r_x=\sin\theta\cos\varphi$ and the like once more with respect to $x$, this time noting that $\theta,\varphi$ are functions of $x$), and after simplification we have

$$ r_{xx}=\frac{1-\sin^2\theta\cos^2\varphi}{r},\qquad \theta_{xx}=\frac{(\sin^2\varphi-2\sin^2\theta\cos^2\varphi)\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta},\qquad \varphi_{xx}=\frac{\sin2\varphi}{r^2\sin^2\theta} $$

(verified with SymPy to agree with the second partial derivatives computed directly from Cartesian coordinates). Substituting all of these together with $r_x,\theta_x,\varphi_x$, and simplifying as in $2r_x\theta_x=\dfrac{2\sin\theta\cos\theta\cos^2\varphi}{r}$, $2r_x\varphi_x=-\dfrac{\sin2\varphi}{r}$, $2\theta_x\varphi_x=-\dfrac{\sin2\varphi\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}$, we finally obtain

$$ \begin{aligned} u_{xx}={}&\sin^2\theta\cos^2\varphi\,u_{rr}+\frac{\cos^2\theta\cos^2\varphi}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}+\frac{\sin^2\varphi}{r^2\sin^2\theta}u_{\varphi\varphi}\\ &+\frac{2\sin\theta\cos\theta\cos^2\varphi}{r}u_{r\theta}-\frac{\sin2\varphi}{r}u_{r\varphi}-\frac{\sin2\varphi\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}u_{\theta\varphi}\\ &+\frac{1-\sin^2\theta\cos^2\varphi}{r}u_r+\frac{(\sin^2\varphi-2\sin^2\theta\cos^2\varphi)\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}u_\theta+\frac{\sin2\varphi}{r^2\sin^2\theta}u_\varphi \end{aligned} $$

This is the complete expression for $u_{xx}$ (verified with SymPy that this expression agrees algebraically with the one assembled mechanically from the chain rule). At this point all nine kinds of second- and first-order partial derivatives have already appeared; if we carry out the same computation for $y,z$ and add the three expressions $u_{xx}+u_{yy}+u_{zz}$ (the many terms involving the angles cancel one another, as in Section 44.2), we finally arrive at the Laplacian in spherical coordinates given in Formula 44.3. Through this exercise one can appreciate that the “shortcut” introduced in the main text effectively spared us this computation in the $y,z$ directions and the enormous simplification that follows it.

Exercise 44.5: Harmonicity of $u=x$ in spherical coordinates

Solution

For $u=r\sin\theta\cos\varphi$, we find each partial derivative. For $r$, $u_r=\sin\theta\cos\varphi$ and $u_{rr}=0$. For $\theta$, $u_\theta=r\cos\theta\cos\varphi$ and $u_{\theta\theta}=-r\sin\theta\cos\varphi$. For $\varphi$, $u_\varphi=-r\sin\theta\sin\varphi$ and $u_{\varphi\varphi}=-r\sin\theta\cos\varphi$. Substituting into Formula 44.3, we obtain

$$ \nabla^2u=0+\frac2r\sin\theta\cos\varphi+\frac{1}{r^2}(-r\sin\theta\cos\varphi)+\frac{\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}(r\cos\theta\cos\varphi)+\frac{1}{r^2\sin^2\theta}(-r\sin\theta\cos\varphi) $$

Simplifying each term, we get

$$ \nabla^2u=\frac{2\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}-\frac{\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}+\frac{\cos^2\theta\cos\varphi}{r\sin\theta}-\frac{\cos\varphi}{r\sin\theta} =\frac{\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}+\frac{\cos\varphi(\cos^2\theta-1)}{r\sin\theta} $$

Here, using $\cos^2\theta-1=-\sin^2\theta$ (a basic trigonometric identity), the second term becomes $\dfrac{-\sin^2\theta\cos\varphi}{r\sin\theta}=-\dfrac{\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}$, so

$$ \nabla^2u=\frac{\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}-\frac{\sin\theta\cos\varphi}{r}=0 $$

and the function indeed satisfies Laplace's equation. In Cartesian coordinates $u=x$, and $\nabla^2x=0$ is trivial. In spherical coordinates the computation is longer, because of the dependence on both $\theta,\varphi$, but it does settle down to $0$ in the end (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 44.6: Harmonicity of $x^2-y^2$ in spherical coordinates

Solution

For $u=r^2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi$, we find each partial derivative. For $r$, $u_r=2r\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi$ and $u_{rr}=2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi$. For $\theta$, using the fact that the derivative of $\sin^2\theta$ is $2\sin\theta\cos\theta=\sin2\theta$, we get $u_\theta=r^2\sin2\theta\cos2\varphi$, and then, since the derivative of $\sin2\theta$ is $2\cos2\theta$, $u_{\theta\theta}=2r^2\cos2\theta\cos2\varphi$. For $\varphi$, $u_\varphi=-2r^2\sin^2\theta\sin2\varphi$ and $u_{\varphi\varphi}=-4r^2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi$. Substituting into Formula 44.3, we obtain

$$ \nabla^2u=2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi+\frac2r\bigl(2r\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi\bigr)+\frac{1}{r^2}\bigl(2r^2\cos2\theta\cos2\varphi\bigr)+\frac{\cos\theta}{r^2\sin\theta}\bigl(r^2\sin2\theta\cos2\varphi\bigr)+\frac{1}{r^2\sin^2\theta}\bigl(-4r^2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi\bigr) $$

Simplifying the dependence on $r,\theta,\varphi$ (computing the fourth term with $\sin2\theta=2\sin\theta\cos\theta$ in mind gives $\dfrac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\cdot2\sin\theta\cos\theta\cos2\varphi=2\cos^2\theta\cos2\varphi$), we get

$$ \nabla^2u=\cos2\varphi\Bigl(2\sin^2\theta+4\sin^2\theta+2\cos2\theta+2\cos^2\theta-4\Bigr) $$

Rewriting the contents of the parentheses with $\cos2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1$ (the double-angle formula used in Chapter 40 and earlier; $\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta$ would also do), we get

$$ 2\sin^2\theta+4\sin^2\theta+2(2\cos^2\theta-1)+2\cos^2\theta-4=6\sin^2\theta+6\cos^2\theta-6=6(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta)-6=6-6=0 $$

so $\nabla^2u=\cos2\varphi\cdot0=0$, and the function indeed satisfies Laplace's equation. By the identity in the hint, $u=r^2\sin^2\theta\cos2\varphi=(r\sin\theta\cos\varphi)^2-(r\sin\theta\sin\varphi)^2=x^2-y^2$, so we have confirmed the same harmonic function as in Example 44.2 (the check in polar coordinates), this time in spherical coordinates, a coordinate system with three variables (verified with SymPy).

← Back to Chapter 44, “Coordinate Transformations and the Laplacian”

Chapter 45 The Schrödinger Equation (Cartesian Coordinates) and the Infinite Square Well Potential

Exercise 45.1: Ratios of energy levels

Solution

We found earlier that the energy eigenvalues of the infinite square well potential (half-width $a$) are

$$ E_n=\frac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}n^2\qquad(n=1,2,3,\dots) $$

Computing $E_5$ and $E_2$, we obtain

$$ E_5=\frac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}\cdot5^2=\frac{25\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2},\qquad E_2=\frac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}\cdot2^2=\frac{4\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2} $$

Taking the ratio, the common factor $\dfrac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}$ cancels out.

$$ \frac{E_5}{E_2}=\frac{25\hbar^2\pi^2/(8ma^2)}{4\hbar^2\pi^2/(8ma^2)}=\frac{25}{4} $$

In general, we can write $E_n=\dfrac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}n^2=E_1\cdot n^2$ ($E_1=\dfrac{\hbar^2\pi^2}{8ma^2}$ is the value for $n=1$), so

$$ \frac{E_n}{E_1}=\frac{E_1\cdot n^2}{E_1}=n^2 $$

holds. Since $a,m,\hbar$ take the same values for every $n$, they all cancel when we compute $E_n/E_1$, and the ratio is determined by $n^2$ alone. (Verified with SymPy: we defined $E_n=\hbar^2\pi^2n^2/(8ma^2)$ and confirmed that $E_5/E_2=25/4$ and $E_n/E_1=n^2$.)

Exercise 45.2: “Accidental” degeneracy in the two-dimensional well

Solution

The energy of the two-dimensional infinite square well potential is $E_{n_1,n_2}=\dfrac{\pi^2\hbar^2}{8ma^2}(n_1^2+n_2^2)$, and it is determined solely by the value of $n_1^2+n_2^2$. Let us compute a few values for pairs with $n_1\le n_2$.

$$ \begin{array}{c|cccccccc} (n_1,n_2) & (1,1) & (1,2) & (2,2) & (1,3) & (2,3) & (1,4) & (3,3) & (2,4)\\\hline n_1^2+n_2^2 & 2 & 5 & 8 & 10 & 13 & 17 & 18 & 20 \end{array} $$

Continuing to list the values of $n_1^2+n_2^2$ in this way, we find that $(1,7)$ and $(5,5)$ both give

$$ 1^2+7^2=1+49=50,\qquad 5^2+5^2=25+25=50 $$

In the range $1^2+7^2\le50$, there is no other pair of positive integers with $n_1^2+n_2^2=50$ ($n_1\le n_2$) (indeed, solving $n_1^2+n_2^2=50$ for $n_1=1,\dots,7$, the quantity $n_2^2=50-n_1^2$ is a perfect square only for $n_1=1$ ($n_2^2=49$) and $n_1=5$ ($n_2^2=25$)). Therefore, the three states $(n_1,n_2)=(1,7)$ (and its swap $(7,1)$) and $(5,5)$ all have the same energy

$$ E_{1,7}=E_{7,1}=E_{5,5}=\frac{\pi^2\hbar^2}{8ma^2}\cdot50=\frac{25\pi^2\hbar^2}{4ma^2} $$

The degeneracy of $(1,7)$ and $(7,1)$ comes from a simple swap, but the fact that $(5,5)$ joins them at the same energy is due to the numerical coincidence $1^2+7^2=5^2+5^2$ ($1+49=25+25$); it is an “extra” degeneracy that cannot be explained by the symmetry of the two-dimensional well alone. (Verified with Python: computing $n_1^2+n_2^2$ for all pairs $n_1,n_2=1,\dots,7$, we confirmed that the smallest repeated value among the pairs with $n_1\le n_2$ is $50$ (that of $(1,7)$ and $(5,5)$).)

Exercise 45.3: Commutator of the momentum operator and the energy operator

Solution

Let $f=f(x,t)$ be an arbitrary sufficiently smooth function (one whose partial derivatives up to second order exist). By definition,

$$ \hat p\hat Ef=\frac\hbar i\pdiff{}{x}\left(-\frac\hbar i\pdiff ft\right) =-\frac{\hbar^2}{i^2}\pdiff{}{x}\left(\pdiff ft\right) =\hbar^2\pdiff{^2f}{x\partial t} $$

(We used $-\dfrac{1}{i^2}=-\dfrac1{-1}=1$.) Similarly,

$$ \hat E\hat pf=-\frac\hbar i\pdiff{}{t}\left(\frac\hbar i\pdiff fx\right) =-\frac{\hbar^2}{i^2}\pdiff{}{t}\left(\pdiff fx\right) =\hbar^2\pdiff{^2f}{t\partial x} $$

Therefore, the commutator is

$$ [\hat p,\hat E]f=\hat p\hat Ef-\hat E\hat pf=\hbar^2\left(\pdiff{^2f}{x\partial t}-\pdiff{^2f}{t\partial x}\right) $$

Now, if $f$ is a sufficiently smooth function (one whose second-order partial derivatives are continuous), then Clairaut's theorem (also called Schwarz's theorem) holds: the result does not change when the order of partial differentiation is exchanged.

$$ \pdiff{^2f}{x\partial t}=\pdiff{^2f}{t\partial x} $$

Hence the expression in the parentheses is identically $0$, and

$$ [\hat p,\hat E]f=\hbar^2\cdot0=0 $$

holds for every $f$. That is, $[\hat p,\hat E]=0$ (the operators commute). (Using SymPy, for a general symbolic function $f(x,t)$ we computed $\hat p\hat Ef-\hat E\hat pf$ and confirmed that it is identically $0$.)

Exercise 45.4: When the initial state is the ground state itself

Solution

The general solution found in Section 45.4 was, for odd $n$,

$$ \psi(x,t)=\frac1{\sqrt a}\,e^{-i\frac{\hbar\pi^2}{8ma^2}n^2t}\cos\frac{n\pi}{2a}x $$

and for even $n$ it had the form with $\sin$ in place of $\cos$. To satisfy the initial condition $\psi(x,0)=\psi_1(x)=\dfrac1{\sqrt a}\cos\dfrac\pi{2a}x$, the expression obtained by substituting $t=0$ must coincide with $\psi_1(x)$. Substituting $t=0$ into the general solution (since $e^0=1$) gives

$$ \psi(x,0)=\frac1{\sqrt a}\cos\frac{n\pi}{2a}x $$

(in the case of odd $n$). This coincides with $\dfrac1{\sqrt a}\cos\dfrac\pi{2a}x$ exactly when $n=1$ (for $n=3,5,\dots$ we get functions such as $\cos\dfrac{3\pi}{2a}x$, which differ from $\cos\dfrac\pi{2a}x$). Therefore, the required solution consists of the $n=1$ term only, namely

$$ \psi(x,t)=\frac1{\sqrt a}\,e^{-i\frac{\hbar\pi^2}{8ma^2}t}\cos\frac{\pi}{2a}x=\psi_1(x)\,e^{-iE_1t/\hbar} $$

(with $E_1=\hbar^2\pi^2/(8ma^2)$). In other words, when the initial state is exactly one eigenfunction (here, the ground state), the time evolution takes the extremely simple form “the state itself multiplied only by the phase factor $e^{-iE_1t/\hbar}$ corresponding to the energy $E_1$.” (When the initial condition is a superposition of several eigenfunctions, each term carries a phase factor determined by its own energy, so the whole undergoes a more complicated change in time—a phenomenon like “beats.” This goes beyond the scope of this chapter, so here we treated only the case where the initial state is a single eigenfunction. Verified with SymPy that $\psi(x,t)=\psi_1(x)e^{-iE_1t/\hbar}$ satisfies the time-dependent Schrödinger equation and coincides with $\psi_1(x)$ at $t=0$.)

Exercise 45.5: Normalization constant in two dimensions (the even × even case)

Solution

When $n_1,n_2$ are both even, $\psi(x,y)=B_1B_2\sin\dfrac{n_1\pi}{2a}x\sin\dfrac{n_2\pi}{2a}y$. The normalization condition is

$$ \iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\abs{\psi(x,y)}^2\,dx\,dy=1 $$

and since $\psi=0$ outside the well, it suffices to integrate over the region $[-a,a]\times[-a,a]$. Because $\psi$ is a product of a function of $x$ alone and a function of $y$ alone, the double integral factors into the product of two single-variable integrals.

$$ \iint_{-a}^{a}B_1^2B_2^2\sin^2\frac{n_1\pi}{2a}x\sin^2\frac{n_2\pi}{2a}y\,dx\,dy =B_1^2B_2^2\left(\int_{-a}^{a}\sin^2\frac{n_1\pi}{2a}x\,dx\right)\left(\int_{-a}^{a}\sin^2\frac{n_2\pi}{2a}y\,dy\right) $$

As shown in the normalization computation of Section 45.3, using the half-angle formula $\sin^2\theta=\dfrac{1-\cos2\theta}2$, for a positive integer $n$ the following holds:

$$ \int_{-a}^{a}\sin^2\frac{n\pi}{2a}u\,du=\int_{-a}^{a}\frac{1-\cos\frac{n\pi}au}2\,du=\frac12(2a)-\frac12\left[\frac{a}{n\pi}\sin\frac{n\pi}au\right]_{-a}^{a}=a-0=a $$

(The integral of the $\cos$ term vanishes because $\sin(n\pi)=\sin(-n\pi)=0$.) Applying this to both the $x$-integral and the $y$-integral, we obtain

$$ B_1^2B_2^2\cdot a\cdot a=B_1^2B_2^2a^2=1 $$

Therefore (if we take $B_1,B_2$ to be positive real numbers) we obtain

$$ B_1B_2=\frac1a $$

This agrees with the value stated in Section 45.5. (Verified with SymPy that $\int_{-a}^a\sin^2(n\pi u/2a)\,du=a$ ($n$ a positive integer).)

Exercise 45.6: Why is $\hat H\psi=E\psi$ “time-independent”?

Solution

The equation $\hat H\psi=E\psi$ itself certainly contains no symbol for the time $t$. However, what the name “time-independent” refers to is not whether the symbol $t$ appears in the equation, but the meaning of the symbol $E$.

As stated in Definition 45.1 of Section 45.1, in the eigenvalue equation $\hat A\psi=\lambda\psi$ the $\lambda$ must be “a constant independent of $\psi$.” The $E$ in $\hat H\psi=E\psi$ is likewise a constant in exactly this sense—a single real number that does not change with time. In other words, the moment we write $\hat H\psi=E\psi$, the physical assumption that “the energy of this system is fixed at a particular value $E$ that does not change as time passes” (the assumption of a stationary state) is already built in.

In contrast, in the general time-evolution equation $\hat H\psi=\hat E\psi$ derived in Section 45.2 (that is, $-\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}\pdiff{^2\psi}{x^2}+V\psi=-\dfrac\hbar i\pdiff\psi t$), the energy operator $\hat E=-\dfrac\hbar i\pdiff{}t$ appears in place of $E$. The operator $\hat E$ is itself the operation of “differentiating with respect to $t$,” and it directly describes how $\psi$ changes with time. In this case, the wave function $\psi(x,t)$ is in general allowed to change its form depending on the time $t$.

To summarize—the $E$ in $\hat H\psi=E\psi$ is “a single number that does not change as time passes,” whereas the $\hat E$ in $\hat H\psi=\hat E\psi$ is “an operator that represents the change in the $t$ direction itself.” We call the former “time-independent” not because $t$ does not appear in the equation as written, but because the name declares that the $E$ appearing there is a constant that does not vary in time. (As stated in the “Caution: The relation between ‘time-independent’ and ‘time-dependent’” in Section 45.2, in fact, a solution of $\hat H\psi=E\psi$, written in the form $\psi(x,t)=\psi(x)e^{-iEt/\hbar}$, is a special solution of $\hat H\psi=\hat E\psi$.)

Chapter 46 The Harmonic Oscillator and the Morse Potential

Exercise 46.1: The coefficients $a_2,a_4$ of the Hermite series

Solution

The recurrence relation is $a_{k+2}=\dfrac{2k+1-\varepsilon}{(k+1)(k+2)}a_k$.

$k=0$: $a_2=\dfrac{2\cdot0+1-\varepsilon}{1\cdot2}a_0=\dfrac{1-\varepsilon}{2}a_0=-\dfrac{\varepsilon-1}{2}a_0$.

$k=2$: $a_4=\dfrac{2\cdot2+1-\varepsilon}{3\cdot4}a_2=\dfrac{5-\varepsilon}{12}a_2=\dfrac{5-\varepsilon}{12}\times\left(-\frac{\varepsilon-1}{2}a_0\right)=-\frac{(5-\varepsilon)(\varepsilon-1)}{24}a_0=\frac{(\varepsilon-1)(\varepsilon-5)}{24}a_0$.

To summarize, we have

$$ a_2=-\frac{\varepsilon-1}{2}a_0,\qquad a_4=\frac{(\varepsilon-1)(\varepsilon-5)}{24}a_0=\frac{(\varepsilon-1)(\varepsilon-5)}{4!}a_0 $$

Since $4!=24$, the latter form agrees with the formula in the main text (Section 46.2.1).

Exercise 46.2: The wave function for $n=2$

Solution

For $n=2$, the normalization constant is

$$ C_2=\left(\frac{m\omega_0}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\frac1{\sqrt{2^2\cdot2!}}=\left(\frac{m\omega_0}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\frac1{\sqrt8} $$

(here $2^2\cdot2!=4\times2=8$). Setting $u=\sqrt{m\omega_0/\hbar}\,x$, we have $H_2(u)=4u^2-2=4\dfrac{m\omega_0}{\hbar}x^2-2$, so we can write

$$ \psi_2(x)=\left(\frac{m\omega_0}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\frac1{\sqrt8}\left(4\frac{m\omega_0}{\hbar}x^2-2\right)e^{-\frac{m\omega_0}{2\hbar}x^2} $$

Using $\dfrac1{\sqrt8}\times2=\dfrac2{\sqrt8}=\dfrac1{\sqrt2}$ to simplify further, we obtain

$$ \psi_2(x)=\left(\frac{m\omega_0}{\pi\hbar}\right)^{1/4}\frac1{\sqrt2}\left(2\frac{m\omega_0}{\hbar}x^2-1\right)e^{-\frac{m\omega_0}{2\hbar}x^2} $$

This is another way of writing the same wave function (verified with SymPy that $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^\infty\psi_2(x)^2\dd x=1$).

Exercise 46.3: Energy levels on a half-infinite domain ($n=0,1$)

Solution

The energy on the half-infinite domain was $E=(2n+\frac32)\hbar\omega_0$ ($n=0,1,2,\ldots$).

$n=0$: $E=\left(2\cdot0+\dfrac32\right)\hbar\omega_0=\dfrac32\hbar\omega_0$. The corresponding value is $n'=2n+1=1$ ($H_1$, the Hermite polynomial of degree one).

$n=1$: $E=\left(2\cdot1+\dfrac32\right)\hbar\omega_0=\dfrac72\hbar\omega_0$. The corresponding value is $n'=2\cdot1+1=3$ ($H_3$, the Hermite polynomial of degree three).

Therefore, $n=1$ on the half-infinite domain (the first excited state) corresponds exactly to what is called the third excited state of the harmonic oscillator on the whole space ($n'=3$, an odd wave function that uses $H_3(u)=8u^3-12u$). On the half-infinite domain, each time $n$ increases by one, the quantum number on the whole space increases by two according to the rule $n'=2n+1$ (because only the odd-degree Hermite functions are used, skipping every other one).

Exercise 46.4: The degeneracy of the three-dimensional isotropic harmonic oscillator ($N=2$)

Solution

We write out all the triples satisfying $n_1+n_2+n_3=2$ ($n_1,n_2,n_3$ are nonnegative integers). There are two cases: one of them equals $2$ and the other two equal $0$, or two of them equal $1$ each and the remaining one equals $0$.

$$ (2,0,0),\ (0,2,0),\ (0,0,2),\ (1,1,0),\ (1,0,1),\ (0,1,1) $$

There are six possibilities in all. On the other hand, the formula of Theorem 46.8 gives $g(2)=\dfrac{(2+1)(2+2)}2=\dfrac{3\times4}2=6$, which agrees. All six of these states have $E=\left(2+\frac32\right)\hbar\omega_0=\frac72\hbar\omega_0$.

Exercise 46.5: The number of bound states of a Morse potential

Solution

Substituting $m=\hbar=1$, $K=50$, $D=1$ into $\lambda=\sqrt{2mK}/(\hbar D)$, we obtain

$$ \lambda=\frac{\sqrt{2\times1\times50}}{1\times1}=\sqrt{100}=10 $$

The number of bound states is $n_{\max}=\lfloor\lambda-\frac12\rfloor=\lfloor10-0.5\rfloor=\lfloor9.5\rfloor=9$, so the allowed values of $n$ are the 10 possibilities $n=0,1,\ldots,9$; that is, there are $n_{\max}+1=10$ bound states (it has been verified by numerical computation that diagonalizing the Hamiltonian by the finite-difference method also gives the same result—however, a different set of parameter values was used for this numerical check; see the verification at the end of the book).

Exercise 46.6: The coefficients of the associated Laguerre series (the case $n=2$)

Solution

The recurrence relation is $c_{k+1}=\dfrac{k-n}{(k+1)(k+2\alpha+1)}c_k$. We compute successively, taking $n=2$ and $c_0=1$.

$k=0$: $c_1=\dfrac{0-2}{(1)(0+2\alpha+1)}c_0=\dfrac{-2}{2\alpha+1}=-\dfrac2{2\alpha+1}$.

(Note: Theorems 46.10 and 46.11 in the main text deal with the equation obtained after the factor $g(u)=u^\alpha$ has been factored out in the form $f=u^\alpha g$, and in $c_1$ the denominator $2\alpha+1$ appears as it is. Here we compute in general, treating $\alpha$ as an arbitrary real number.)

$k=1$: $c_2=\dfrac{1-2}{(2)(1+2\alpha+1)}c_1=\dfrac{-1}{2(2\alpha+2)}\times\left(-\frac2{2\alpha+1}\right)=\dfrac{1}{2\alpha+2}\times\frac1{2\alpha+1}=\frac1{(2\alpha+1)(2\alpha+2)}$.

$k=2$: $c_3=\dfrac{2-2}{(3)(2+2\alpha+1)}c_2=\dfrac0{3(2\alpha+3)}c_2=0$.

Indeed, the numerator becomes $k-n=2-2=0$, so $c_3=0$, and all the later coefficients also become $0$ (because the right-hand side of the recurrence relation is multiplied by $c_3=0$). Therefore $g(u)$ is

$$ g(u)=c_0+c_1u+c_2u^2=1-\frac2{2\alpha+1}u+\frac1{(2\alpha+1)(2\alpha+2)}u^2 $$

a polynomial that stops at exactly degree two (verified with SymPy by actually substituting into the recurrence relation for $k=0,1,2,3$, which gives $c_1=-2/(2\alpha+1)$, $c_2=1/(2(\alpha+1)(2\alpha+1))$, $c_3=0$—see the verification at the end of the book).

← Back to Chapter 46, “The Harmonic Oscillator and the Morse Potential”

Chapter 47 The Schrödinger Equation (Polar and Cylindrical Coordinates) and Bessel Functions

Exercise 47.1: Checking $J_{-1}(x)=-J_1(x)$ with the series

Solution

Substituting $\alpha=-1$ into the definition of the Bessel function of the first kind (Definition 41.2 in Chapter 41) gives

$$ J_{-1}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!\,\Gamma(-1+k+1)}\left(\frac x2\right)^{2k-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!\,\Gamma(k)}\left(\frac x2\right)^{2k-1} $$

Here the term for $k=0$ contains $\Gamma(0)$, but the gamma function is not defined at integers less than or equal to $0$ (we regard $1/\Gamma(0)=0$, following the idea of Chapter 41, Section 41.3.4), so this term vanishes. Hence the sum effectively starts from $k=1$. Changing the summation index to $k=j+1$ ($j=0,1,2,\dots$), we have $k!=(j+1)!$, $\Gamma(k)=\Gamma(j+1)=j!$, and $2k-1=2j+1$, so

$$ J_{-1}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{(j+1)!\,j!}\left(\frac x2\right)^{2j+1} =-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!\,(j+1)!}\left(\frac x2\right)^{2j+1} $$

(we used $(-1)^{j+1}=-(-1)^j$). On the other hand, substituting $\alpha=1$ and using $\Gamma(1+k+1)=(k+1)!$, we have for $J_1(x)$

$$ J_1(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!\,(k+1)!}\left(\frac x2\right)^{2k+1} $$

Comparing the two, the summand of $J_{-1}(x)$ has exactly the same form as the summand of $J_1(x)$ (they coincide if $j$ is read as $k$), so the relation

$$ J_{-1}(x)=-J_1(x) $$

has been verified completely for each term of the series ($k=0,1,2,\dots$) (the numerical verification with scipy.special.jv at $x=0.5,1,\dots,10$ also agrees: the maximum error is less than $10^{-15}$).

Exercise 47.2: The energy for $(m=0,\ l=2)$

Solution

The second positive zero of the Bessel function $J_0$ is $\lambda_0(2)=5.5201$ (Table 47.1 of this chapter). Substituting $m=0,\ l=2$ into the energy-level formula for the circular region

$$ E_m(l)=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m_ea^2}\lambda_m^2(l) $$

gives

$$ E_0(2)=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m_ea^2}(5.5201)^2=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m_ea^2}\times30.4715\fallingdotseq15.236\cdot\frac{\hbar^2}{m_ea^2} $$

(verified with scipy.special.jn\_zeros: $\lambda_0(2)=5.520078\ldots$, $E_0(2)=15.2356\ldots\ \hbar^2/(m_ea^2)$). This is about $5.3$ times the ground-state energy $E_0(1)\approx2.8916\,\hbar^2/(m_ea^2)$ found in Example 47.3, and we see that, even for the same $m=0$, the energy rises considerably by the amount corresponding to one additional oscillation in the radial direction ($l=1\to2$).

Exercise 47.3: The case of a periodic boundary condition in the $z$ direction

Solution

For $Z(z)=D_0\cos\omega z+E_0\sin\omega z$ we consider the periodic boundary conditions $Z(z_0)=Z(-z_0)$ and $Z'(z_0)=Z'(-z_0)$. Since $\cos$ is an even function and $\sin$ is an odd function, we have

$$ Z(z_0)-Z(-z_0)=\bigl(D_0\cos\omega z_0+E_0\sin\omega z_0\bigr)-\bigl(D_0\cos\omega z_0-E_0\sin\omega z_0\bigr)=2E_0\sin\omega z_0 $$

The condition for this to equal $0$ is $E_0=0$ or $\sin\omega z_0=0$. Next, using $Z'(z)=-D_0\omega\sin\omega z+E_0\omega\cos\omega z$, we obtain

$$ Z'(z_0)-Z'(-z_0)=\bigl(-D_0\omega\sin\omega z_0+E_0\omega\cos\omega z_0\bigr)-\bigl(D_0\omega\sin\omega z_0+E_0\omega\cos\omega z_0\bigr)=-2D_0\omega\sin\omega z_0 $$

and the condition for this to equal $0$ is $D_0=0$ or $\sin\omega z_0=0$.

If we set both $D_0,E_0$ to $0$, then $Z(z)$ would be identically $0$ and would be meaningless as a physical solution. Therefore, in order to satisfy both conditions simultaneously without setting either of $D_0,E_0$ to $0$, we must have

$$ \sin\omega z_0=0\qquad\therefore\quad\omega z_0=n\pi\ (n=1,2,3,\dots)\qquad\text{that is}\qquad\omega=\frac{n\pi}{z_0} $$

In this case $D_0,E_0$ can both be chosen freely ($D_0\cos(n\pi z/z_0)+E_0\sin(n\pi z/z_0)$ is a solution as it stands). Whereas the quantization condition in the main text (the Dirichlet-type boundary condition, $Z(\pm z_0)=0$) was $\omega z_0=l\pi/2$ ($l$ a positive integer), for the periodic boundary condition it becomes $\omega z_0=n\pi$ (the condition that the length of the period is the reciprocal of an integer multiple of $2z_0$, that is, that the wavelength is the whole height $2z_0$ of the cylinder divided by an integer), and we see that the spacing of the quantized values is different. We verified with SymPy that, with $D_0,E_0$ kept as symbols, $Z(z_0)-Z(-z_0)=2E_0\sin(\omega z_0)$ and $Z'(z_0)-Z'(-z_0)=-2D_0\omega\sin(\omega z_0)$.

Exercise 47.4: Periodicity in the angular direction and the integrality of $m$

Solution

For $m=2$, substituting $\theta\to\theta+2\pi$ into $\Theta(\theta)=e^{i2\theta}$ gives

$$ \Theta(\theta+2\pi)=e^{i2(\theta+2\pi)}=e^{i2\theta}\cdot e^{i4\pi} $$

By Euler's formula, $e^{i4\pi}=\cos4\pi+i\sin4\pi=1+0i=1$ ($4\pi$ is an integer multiple of $2\pi$), so $\Theta(\theta+2\pi)=e^{i2\theta}\times1=\Theta(\theta)$, and the periodicity indeed holds.

On the other hand, for $m=2.3$ (not an integer), we have

$$ \Theta(\theta+2\pi)=e^{i2.3\theta}\cdot e^{i2\pi\times2.3}=e^{i2.3\theta}\cdot e^{i4.6\pi} $$

We compute $e^{i4.6\pi}=\cos(4.6\pi)+i\sin(4.6\pi)$. Since $4.6\pi=4\pi+0.6\pi$ and $\cos,\sin$ have period $2\pi$, the $4\pi$ part can be ignored, and $\cos(0.6\pi)\fallingdotseq-0.309$, $\sin(0.6\pi)\fallingdotseq0.951$. Therefore

$$ e^{i4.6\pi}\fallingdotseq-0.309+0.951i\neq1 $$

and $\Theta(\theta+2\pi)=(-0.309+0.951i)\,\Theta(\theta)\neq\Theta(\theta)$. That is, if $m$ is not an integer, the periodic boundary condition does not hold. This is a concrete example of the statement “the periodic boundary condition implies $m\in\mathbb Z$” proved in Theorem 47.1 in Section 47.2.3 (we verified with SymPy, by computing the numerical value of $e^{i2\pi\times2.3}$, that it deviates significantly from $1$).

Exercise 47.5: Checking the normalization constant $A_0$ by numerical integration

Solution

Let $a=1$ and use $\lambda_0(1)=2.40483$ (computed with scipy.special.jn\_zeros). Evaluating the integral

$$ A_0^{\text{(integral)}}=\int_0^1 J_0^2(2.40483\,r)\,r\,dr $$

by numerical integration (scipy.integrate.quad) gives $A_0^{\text{(integral)}}\fallingdotseq0.1347571$. On the other hand, the formula in Section 47.2.5 of this chapter is $A_0=\dfrac{a^2}2J_1^2(\lambda_0(1))$ ($a=1$), so

$$ A_0^{\text{(formula)}}=\frac12J_1^2(2.40483)=\frac12\times(0.519153)^2\fallingdotseq0.1347571 $$

and the result of the numerical integration, $A_0^{\text{(integral)}}$, and the value given by the formula, $A_0^{\text{(formula)}}$, agree completely up to the seventh decimal place (verified with scipy; the difference between the two is less than $10^{-14}$). This supports the correctness, at least in this concrete example, of the general formula (Lommel's integral formula) that we agreed in Section 47.2.5 to “accept and use without proof.”

Exercise 47.6: Expressing $J_2(x)$ in terms of $J_0,J_1$ with the recurrence relation

Solution

Substituting $m=1$ into the recurrence relation $J_{m-1}(x)+J_{m+1}(x)=\dfrac{2m}{x}J_m(x)$ derived in Example 47.5 of this chapter gives

$$ J_0(x)+J_2(x)=\frac{2}{x}J_1(x) $$

Solving this for $J_2(x)$, we obtain

$$ J_2(x)=\frac2xJ_1(x)-J_0(x) $$

Thus $J_2(x)$ has been expressed using only the lower-order functions $J_0(x),J_1(x)$ (together with elementary multiplication by $2/x$ and subtraction)—when higher-order Bessel functions are handled on a computer, the method of building them up one after another from lower-order functions by a recurrence relation in this way (upward recurrence) is often used. Using scipy.special.jv, we compared the values of the right-hand side $\dfrac2xJ_1(x)-J_0(x)$ and the left-hand side $J_2(x)$ at several points $x=0.5,1,2,\dots,10$ and confirmed that the maximum error is less than $10^{-15}$, so the two agree completely.

← Back to Chapter 47, “The Schrödinger Equation (Polar and Cylindrical Coordinates) and Bessel Functions”

Chapter 48 The Schrödinger Equation for the Hydrogen Atom (Spherical Coordinates)

Exercise 48.1: Allowed sets of quantum numbers for $n=2,3$

Solution

The azimuthal quantum number ranges over $\ell=0,1,\dots,n-1$, and for each $\ell$ the magnetic quantum number ranges over $m=-\ell,-\ell+1,\dots,\ell$.

(a) For $n=2$. $\ell$ takes the two values $0,1$ ($\ell\le n-1=1$).

  • $\ell=0$: $m=0$ (one possibility, the 2s state)
  • $\ell=1$: $m=-1,0,1$ (three possibilities, the 2p state)

In total there are $1+3=4=n^2$ possibilities, which agrees with the formula for the degree of degeneracy in the main text.

(b) For $n=3$. Since $\ell\le n-1=2$, there are three possibilities, $\ell=0,1,2$ (corresponding to 3s, 3p, and 3d).

Exercise 48.2: The associated Laguerre polynomial for $n=3,\ell=0$ (the 3s state)

Solution

Since $n=3,\ell=0$, we have $p=2\ell+1=1$ and $q=n+\ell=3$. By the defining formula, the sum consists of three terms, $k=1,2,3$.

$$ k=1:\ \frac{(-1)^1(3!)^2}{(1-1)!\,1!\,(3-1)!}\alpha^{1-1}=\frac{-36}{1\cdot1\cdot2}=-18 $$ $$ k=2:\ \frac{(-1)^2(3!)^2}{(2-1)!\,2!\,(3-2)!}\alpha^{2-1}=\frac{36}{1\cdot2\cdot1}\alpha=18\alpha $$ $$ k=3:\ \frac{(-1)^3(3!)^2}{(3-1)!\,3!\,(3-3)!}\alpha^{3-1}=\frac{-36}{2\cdot6\cdot1}\alpha^2=-3\alpha^2 $$

Therefore we have

$$ L_3^1(\alpha)=-3\alpha^2+18\alpha-18=-3(\alpha^2-6\alpha+6) $$

We now check that this satisfies the equation $\alpha y''+(2-\alpha)y'+2y=0$ for the case $p=1,q=3$ ($p+1=2$, $q-p=2$). If $y=-3\alpha^2+18\alpha-18$, then $y'=-6\alpha+18$ and $y''=-6$, so the left-hand side becomes

$$ \alpha(-6)+(2-\alpha)(-6\alpha+18)+2(-3\alpha^2+18\alpha-18) $$

Expanding this gives $-6\alpha+(-12\alpha+36+6\alpha^2-18\alpha)+(-6\alpha^2+36\alpha-36)=-6\alpha-30\alpha+36+6\alpha^2-6\alpha^2+36\alpha-36=0$ (the term linear in $\alpha$: $-6-30+36=0$; the constant term: $36-36=0$; the quadratic term: $6-6=0$), so it is indeed $0$ (verified with SymPy).

Exercise 48.3: The associated Legendre functions for $\ell=1$

Solution

The Legendre polynomial for $\ell=1$ is $P_1(x)=x$.

$m=0$: $\Theta(x)=b_0(1-x^2)^0\dfrac{d^0P_1}{dx^0}=b_0x$. Returning to $x=\cos\theta$, we get $\Theta(\theta)=b_0\cos\theta$.

$m=1$: $\Theta(x)=b_0(1-x^2)^{1/2}\dfrac{dP_1}{dx}=b_0\sqrt{1-x^2}\cdot1=b_0\sqrt{1-x^2}$. Since $1-x^2=1-\cos^2\theta=\sin^2\theta$ ($\sin\theta\ge0$, $\theta\in[0,\pi]$), we get $\Theta(\theta)=b_0\sin\theta$.

$m=-1$: As confirmed in the main text, the right-hand side of the formula is determined by $|m|$, so we use the same formula as for $|m|=1$. Therefore $\Theta(\theta)=b_0\sin\theta$ (exactly the same functional form as for $m=1$; the difference appears in the sign of the exponent in the azimuthal part $\bar\Phi_m(\varphi)=C_0e^{im\varphi}$ that multiplies it in the complete wave function—whether it is $e^{+i\varphi}$ or $e^{-i\varphi}$).

Exercise 48.4: Derivation of the Rydberg constant

Solution

The energy of the photon emitted when the principal quantum number drops from $n_2$ to $n_1$ ($n_1\lt n_2$) is the energy of the higher level minus that of the lower level, $E_{n_2}-E_{n_1}$. Substituting the energy-level formula $E_n=-\dfrac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^2}\cdot\dfrac1{n^2}$, we obtain

$$ E_{n_2}-E_{n_1}=-\frac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^2}\left(\frac1{n_2^2}-\frac1{n_1^2}\right)=\frac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^2}\left(\frac1{n_1^2}-\frac1{n_2^2}\right) $$

(since $n_1\lt n_2$, we have $1/n_1^2\gt1/n_2^2$, so the right-hand side is positive—consistent with the physical direction, namely that energy is emitted). The energy of the photon is $\dfrac{hc}{\lambda}$, so

$$ \frac{hc}{\lambda}=\frac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^2}\left(\frac1{n_1^2}-\frac1{n_2^2}\right) $$

Dividing both sides by $hc$ gives

$$ \frac1\lambda=\frac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^3c}\left(\frac1{n_1^2}-\frac1{n_2^2}\right) $$

Comparing this with the form $\dfrac1\lambda=R_H\left(\dfrac1{n_1^2}-\dfrac1{n_2^2}\right)$, we find

$$ R_H=\frac{m_ee_0^4}{8\varepsilon_0^2h^3c} $$

Substituting numerical values ($m_e=9.109\times10^{-31}\,\text{kg}$, $e_0=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\text C$, $\varepsilon_0=8.854\times10^{-12}\,\text{F/m}$, $h=6.626\times10^{-34}\,\text{J\,s}$, $c=2.998\times10^8\,\text{m/s}$), we obtain $R_H\approx1.097\times10^7\,\text m^{-1}$, which agrees with the experimentally known value of the Rydberg constant (verified with SymPy/numpy using the precise values in scipy.constants—they agree to within 1%).

Exercise 48.5: The radius at which the radial probability density of the 1s state is maximal

Solution

Since $R_{10}(r)\propto e^{-r/a_0}$, the radial probability density is $f(r):=r^2R_{10}(r)^2\propto r^2e^{-2r/a_0}$ (the constant of proportionality does not affect the value of $r$ that gives the maximum, so we may take it to be $1$). Using the product rule for derivatives, we get

$$ f'(r)=2re^{-2r/a_0}+r^2\cdot\left(-\frac2{a_0}\right)e^{-2r/a_0}=2re^{-2r/a_0}\left(1-\frac r{a_0}\right) $$

Here we factored out the common factor $2re^{-2r/a_0}$. The derivative satisfies $f'(r)=0$ exactly when $r=0$ or $1-r/a_0=0$ (that is, $r=a_0$), because $e^{-2r/a_0}$ is never $0$. At $r=0$ we have $f(0)=0$ (the probability density is at its minimum, and this is a boundary endpoint, not a local maximum). We check whether $r=a_0$ is a local maximum by means of the second derivative.

$$ f''(r)=2e^{-2r/a_0}\left(1-\frac ra_0\right)+2r\cdot\left(-\frac2{a_0}\right)e^{-2r/a_0}\left(1-\frac r{a_0}\right)+2re^{-2r/a_0}\cdot\left(-\frac1{a_0}\right) $$

Substituting $r=a_0$ into this expression, we find that $1-r/a_0=0$, so all the terms containing this factor vanish and only the last term remains.

$$ f''(a_0)=2a_0e^{-2}\cdot\left(-\frac1{a_0}\right)=-2e^{-2}\lt0 $$

Since the second derivative is negative, $f(r)$ has a local maximum at $r=a_0$ (if we make an increase–decrease table, we see that $f'(r)\gt0$ (increasing) for $0\lt r\lt a_0$ and $f'(r)\lt0$ (decreasing) for $r\gt a_0$, so $r=a_0$ is the only local maximum and is also the global maximum of the graph). Therefore, the radius at which the radial probability density is maximal is

$$ r=a_0 $$

—exactly the Bohr radius itself. This means that the statement of Bohr's atomic model, “the electron moves on a circular orbit of radius $a_0$,” corresponds precisely to the answer of the Schrödinger equation, “the probability of finding the electron near $r=a_0$ is the highest” (in the picture given by the Schrödinger equation, however, the electron is not located at the single point $r=a_0$ but is distributed with some spread around $r=a_0$—see the 1s curve in Figure 48.4).

Exercise 48.6: Orthogonality of the azimuthal solutions

Solution

The complex conjugate of $\bar\Phi_m(\varphi)=C_0e^{im\varphi}$ is obtained by reversing only the sign in the exponent: $\overline{\bar\Phi_{m'}(\varphi)}=\overline{C_0}e^{-im'\varphi}$. Therefore,

$$ \int_0^{2\pi}\bar\Phi_m(\varphi)\overline{\bar\Phi_{m'}(\varphi)}\,\dd\varphi =|C_0|^2\int_0^{2\pi}e^{im\varphi}e^{-im'\varphi}\,\dd\varphi =|C_0|^2\int_0^{2\pi}e^{i(m-m')\varphi}\,\dd\varphi $$

Let $k:=m-m'$ (since $m\neq m'$, it is an integer such that $k\neq0$). By the formula for the antiderivative of the exponential function ($\displaystyle\int e^{ik\varphi}\dd\varphi=\dfrac{e^{ik\varphi}}{ik}$, $k\neq0$), we obtain

$$ \int_0^{2\pi}e^{ik\varphi}\,\dd\varphi=\left[\frac{e^{ik\varphi}}{ik}\right]_0^{2\pi}=\frac{e^{i2\pi k}-e^{0}}{ik}=\frac{1-1}{ik}=0 $$

(since $k$ is an integer, Euler's formula gives $e^{i2\pi k}=\cos2\pi k+i\sin2\pi k=1+0=1$). Therefore, when $m\neq m'$ the integral is $0$ (when $m=m'$ the integrand is identically $1$ and the integral is $2\pi\neq0$—this corresponds to the “normal” (normalization) part of “orthonormal”).

The reason this result guarantees the orthogonality of two hydrogen-atom states with different magnetic quantum numbers is as follows. The inner product of the complete wave functions (a triple integral) can be decomposed into the product of three integrals, over the radial, zenith-angle, and azimuthal variables (because it is an integral of functions whose variables have been separated).

$$ \int\psi_{n\ell m}\,\overline{\psi_{n'\ell'm'}}\,r^2\sin\theta\,\dd r\,\dd\theta\,\dd\varphi =\left(\int_0^\infty R_{n\ell}R_{n'\ell'}\,r^2\dd r\right)\left(\int_0^\pi\Theta_{\ell m}\Theta_{\ell'm'}\sin\theta\,\dd\theta\right)\left(\int_0^{2\pi}\bar\Phi_m\overline{\bar\Phi_{m'}}\,\dd\varphi\right) $$

If even one of the three integrals on the right-hand side is $0$, the whole product (the triple integral) is also $0$. As we have just shown, when $m\neq m'$ the azimuthal integral (the third factor) is automatically $0$, so regardless of the values of the radial and zenith-angle integrals, $\psi_{n\ell m}$ and $\psi_{n'\ell'm'}$ ($m\neq m'$) are orthogonal. This is the simplest concrete example one can verify of the general theorem in quantum mechanics that “eigenfunctions corresponding to different eigenvalues are orthogonal” (which is beyond the scope of this book).

← Back to Chapter 48, “The Schrödinger Equation for the Hydrogen Atom (Spherical Coordinates)”

Chapter 49 Statistical Mechanics and Black-Body Radiation — The Boltzmann Distribution and Planck's Radiation Law

Exercise 49.1: The altitude at which the density is halved

Solution

In the altitude distribution of gas molecules $N(z)=N(0)e^{-mgz/kT}$, we find the altitude $z$ at which $N(z)=N(0)/2$. Dividing both sides by $N(0)$ gives

$$ e^{-\frac{mgz}{kT}}=\frac12 $$

Taking the natural logarithm of both sides (on the left, $\ln e^{X}=X$; on the right, $\ln\dfrac12=-\ln2$), we obtain

$$ -\frac{mgz}{kT}=\ln\frac12=-\ln2 $$

Multiplying both sides by $-1$ and solving for $z$, we obtain

$$ z=\frac{kT\ln2}{mg} $$

Next, we substitute concrete numerical values. Taking the mean molecular weight of air to be $28.97\ \mathrm{g/mol}$, the mass per molecule is the same as in Example 49.1:

$$ m=\frac{28.97\times10^{-3}}{6.02214076\times10^{23}}\approx4.8106\times10^{-26}\ \mathrm{kg} $$

Substituting $k=1.380649\times10^{-23}\ \mathrm{J/K}$, $T=288\ \mathrm K$, $g=9.8\ \mathrm{m/s^2}$, and $\ln2\approx0.693147$, we obtain

$$ z=\frac{(1.380649\times10^{-23})\times288\times0.693147}{(4.8106\times10^{-26})\times9.8}\approx5846\ \mathrm m\approx5.85\ \mathrm{km} $$

This altitude (verified with SymPy/numpy) is even higher than Mt. Fuji (elevation about $3.8\ \mathrm{km}$) and amounts to about 60% of the cruising altitude of passenger aircraft (about $10\ \mathrm{km}$, Example 49.1). In Example 49.1 we confirmed that “at an altitude of 10,000 m the density drops to about 31% of the surface value,” and the present result does not contradict it—because the ratio $0.306$ at $1$ × 10,000 m is much smaller than $1/2$, the altitude $z\approx5.85\ \mathrm{km}$ at which the density is halved must of course lie below $1$ × 10,000 m, and indeed it does.

Exercise 49.2: A concrete example with $h\nu\ll kT$

Solution

First we compute the value of $h\nu/kT$. Using $h=6.62607015\times10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$, $\nu=1\ \mathrm{GHz}=1.0\times10^9\ \mathrm{Hz}$, $k=1.380649\times10^{-23}\ \mathrm{J/K}$, and $T=300\ \mathrm K$, we have

$$ h\nu=(6.62607015\times10^{-34})\times(1.0\times10^9)\approx6.626\times10^{-25}\ \mathrm J $$ $$ kT=(1.380649\times10^{-23})\times300\approx4.142\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm J $$ $$ \frac{h\nu}{kT}\approx\frac{6.626\times10^{-25}}{4.142\times10^{-21}}\approx1.60\times10^{-4} $$

Indeed, this satisfies $h\nu/kT\approx1.60\times10^{-4}\ll1$ (compared with $h\nu/kT\approx80$ for visible light in Example 49.2, it is in fact smaller by more than $5$ orders of magnitude. For radio waves, whose frequency is far lower than that of visible light, this means that the energy $h\nu$ of a single photon is smaller than the thermal energy $kT$ by orders of magnitude, and that quantum effects can be almost entirely neglected).

Next, for these $\nu,T$, we compare numerically the values of Planck's formula (Theorem 49.2) and the Rayleigh–Jeans formula (Formula 49.1). The coefficient $8\pi\nu^2/c^3$ is common to both, so it suffices to compare only the remaining factors. The factor in Planck's formula is

$$ \frac{h\nu}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1} $$

Substituting $h\nu/kT\approx1.60\times10^{-4}$ and computing numerically (the value of the denominator is very close to that given by $e^{1.60\times10^{-4}}-1\approx1.60\times10^{-4}$), the value of Planck's formula is $E_{\text{Planck}}\approx3.8632\times10^{-27}$ (the value of the energy per unit volume per unit frequency, including $8\pi\nu^2/c^3$, in SI units). On the other hand, using $kT$ as it is, the value of the Rayleigh–Jeans formula is $E_{\text{RJ}}\approx3.8635\times10^{-27}$. The relative difference is

$$ \frac{|E_{\text{Planck}}-E_{\text{RJ}}|}{E_{\text{RJ}}}\approx8.0\times10^{-5} $$

As computed above (verified with SymPy/numpy), the two values agree so well that they coincide to four places after the decimal point. This confirms that the analytic approximation made in Section 49.3.2 ($e^{h\nu/kT}\fallingdotseq1+h\nu/kT$; in the limit $h\nu/kT\to0$, Planck's formula coincides with the Rayleigh–Jeans formula) also holds true in actual numerical values.

Exercise 49.3: Expanding the high-frequency limit yourself

Solution

We rewrite the fraction in Planck's formula using $X:=e^{-h\nu/kT}$ (when $h\nu/kT\gg1$, $X$ is a small positive number close to $0$). First, multiplying the numerator and denominator of the fraction whose denominator is $e^{h\nu/kT}-1$ by $e^{-h\nu/kT}=X$, we get

$$ \frac{1}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1}=\frac{1}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1}\times\frac{X}{X}=\frac{X\cdot e^{-h\nu/kT}\cdot e^{h\nu/kT}}{X\left(e^{h\nu/kT}-1\right)} $$

This is also possible, but let us transform the expression directly, in a way that is easier to follow. Noting that $e^{h\nu/kT}=1/X$, we have

$$ \frac{1}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1}=\frac{1}{\dfrac1X-1}=\frac{1}{\dfrac{1-X}{X}}=\frac{X}{1-X} $$

(We have simply rewritten a complex fraction as a product with the reciprocal.) Now, supposing that $X$ is small ($X\ll1$), we expand $\dfrac{X}{1-X}$ as a power series. Since $\dfrac1{1-X}=1+X+X^2+\cdots$ (the sum formula for an infinite geometric series for $|X|\lt1$, the same formula used in Section 49.4.1), we have

$$ \frac{X}{1-X}=X\left(1+X+X^2+\cdots\right)=X+X^2+X^3+\cdots $$

When $X$ is sufficiently small, the terms from $X^2$ onward are even smaller than $X$ itself and can be neglected, so that in the $1$st-order approximation we obtain

$$ \frac{1}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1}=\frac{X}{1-X}\fallingdotseq X=e^{-h\nu/kT} $$

(This was also verified with SymPy's series expansion \texttt{series}: it gives $X+O(X^2)$—the lowest-order term is exactly $X$.) Substituting this into Planck's formula (Theorem 49.2), we obtain

$$ E(\nu)\,d\nu=\frac{8\pi\nu^2}{c^3}\cdot h\nu\cdot\frac{1}{e^{h\nu/kT}-1}\,d\nu\ \fallingdotseq\ \frac{8\pi\nu^2}{c^3}\cdot h\nu\cdot e^{-h\nu/kT}\,d\nu $$

This is exactly the same expression as Wien's radiation law (Formula 49.2). In Section 49.3.1 of the main text we started from the approximation that “the $-1$ in the denominator is negligibly small,” but here, independently, by a different route—a power series expansion in $X=e^{-h\nu/kT}$—we have arrived at the same conclusion (in the high-frequency limit, Planck's formula coincides with Wien's formula).

Exercise 49.4: The density of states in two dimensions

Solution

We apply the three-dimensional argument of Section 49.5.2 to two-dimensional standing waves confined in a square membrane of side $L$ (area $A=L^2$). For the same reason as in three dimensions (quantization of the wavenumbers by the boundary conditions), the wavenumbers in the $x,y$ directions take the discrete values

$$ k_1=\frac{n_1\pi}{L},\qquad k_2=\frac{n_2\pi}{L}\qquad(n_1,n_2=1,2,3,\dots) $$

The number of pairs $(n_1,n_2)$ for which the magnitude $K=\sqrt{k_1^2+k_2^2}$ of the wave vector $\bm k=(k_1,k_2)$ is at most a given value equals the number of lattice points inside the circle of radius $K$ centered at the origin, within “the first quadrant of the two-dimensional lattice, where $n_1,n_2$ are both positive.” The “$1/8$ of a sphere (the positive octant)” of the three-dimensional case is replaced in two dimensions by “$1/4$ of a circle (the first quadrant).” The area of a circle of radius $K$ is $\pi K^2$, and we consider $1/4$ of it. The lattice points are arranged at intervals of $\pi/L$ in each of the $x,y$ directions, so the area occupied by one lattice point is $(\pi/L)^2$. Therefore, the number of pairs $(n_1,n_2)$ (pairs of positive integers) for which the magnitude of the wavenumber is at most $K$ is

$$ \frac14\cdot\pi K^2\Big/\left(\frac\pi L\right)^2=\frac{\pi K^2L^2}{4\pi^2}=\frac{K^2L^2}{4\pi} $$

As in the three-dimensional case, an electromagnetic wave has two polarization degrees of freedom, so doubling this, the actual total number of modes is

$$ N_{\text{2D}}(K)=2\times\frac{K^2L^2}{4\pi}=\frac{K^2L^2}{2\pi}=\frac{K^2A}{2\pi} $$

($A=L^2$ is the area of the square.) Next, we rewrite the wavenumber $K$ in terms of the frequency $\nu$. Substituting the same relation $K=2\pi\nu/c$ as in the three-dimensional case, we obtain

$$ N_{\text{2D}}(\nu)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{2\pi\nu}{c}\right)^2A=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{4\pi^2\nu^2}{c^2}\,A=\frac{2\pi\nu^2}{c^2}\,A $$

This is “the total number of modes with frequency at most $\nu$.” Differentiating it with respect to $\nu$ gives the increment, for the area $A$, in “the number of modes between frequency $\nu$ and $\nu+d\nu$.”

$$ \frac{dN_{\text{2D}}}{d\nu}=\frac{4\pi\nu}{c^2}\,A $$

Finally, to obtain the density of states per unit area, we simply divide by $A$.

$$ \rho_{\text{2D}}(\nu)=\frac1A\frac{dN_{\text{2D}}}{d\nu}=\frac{4\pi\nu}{c^2} $$

(Verified with SymPy—we retraced exactly the same steps as in the three-dimensional case, now in two dimensions: from the computation of the area of a circle of radius $K$, through the substitution of $K=2\pi\nu/c$, to the differentiation with respect to $\nu$.)

Compared with the three-dimensional result $\rho(\nu)=8\pi\nu^2/c^3\propto\nu^2$, in two dimensions $\rho_{\text{2D}}(\nu)\propto\nu^1$: the power of $\nu$ is lower by one. This is because each time the dimension of space decreases by one, the exponent that expresses the volume of a “(hyper)sphere of radius $K$” ($K^3$ in three dimensions, $K^2$ in two dimensions) decreases by one; and even after the operation of differentiating with respect to $\nu$ to lower the exponent by one ($K^3\to\nu^3\to$ differentiation gives $\nu^2$ in three dimensions; $K^2\to\nu^2\to$ differentiation gives $\nu^1$ in two dimensions), we see that the difference in dimension remains as it is, as the difference in the $\nu$-dependence of $\rho(\nu)$.

Exercise 49.5: The total energy of Wien's formula converges

Solution

Wien's radiation law (Formula 49.2) reads $E(\nu)\,d\nu=\dfrac{8\pi}{c^3}\nu^2\cdot h\nu\cdot e^{-h\nu/kT}\,d\nu=\dfrac{8\pi h}{c^3}\nu^3e^{-h\nu/kT}\,d\nu$. If we collect the part that does not depend on $\nu$ into the constant $C:=8\pi h/c^3$, then $E(\nu)=C\nu^3e^{-h\nu/kT}$. The total energy we seek is

$$ \int_0^\infty E(\nu)\,d\nu=C\int_0^\infty \nu^3e^{-h\nu/kT}\,d\nu $$

To compute this integral, we change to the dimensionless variable $x:=h\nu/kT$. Then $\nu=\dfrac{kT}{h}x$, and differentiating both sides gives $d\nu=\dfrac{kT}{h}dx$ (since $k,T,h$ are constants independent of $\nu$, they come out of the differentiation as constants of proportionality). The limits of integration remain unchanged: $\nu:0\to\infty$ corresponds to $x:0\to\infty$ (as $\nu\to\infty$, $x\to\infty$; at $\nu=0$, $x=0$). Therefore,

$$ \int_0^\infty \nu^3e^{-h\nu/kT}\,d\nu=\int_0^\infty\left(\frac{kT}{h}x\right)^3e^{-x}\cdot\frac{kT}{h}\,dx=\left(\frac{kT}{h}\right)^4\int_0^\infty x^3e^{-x}\,dx $$

(We combined $\left(\dfrac{kT}h\right)^3\times\dfrac{kT}h=\left(\dfrac{kT}h\right)^4$.) Here, $\displaystyle\int_0^\infty x^3e^{-x}\,dx$ is exactly the case $n=4$ of the gamma function $\Gamma(n)=\displaystyle\int_0^\infty x^{n-1}e^{-x}dx$ studied in Chapter 32, and $\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!$ holds for positive integers $n$ (this can be shown by repeated integration by parts), so we obtain the finite value

$$ \int_0^\infty x^3e^{-x}\,dx=\Gamma(4)=3!=6 $$

Therefore, the total energy converges to the finite value

$$ \int_0^\infty E(\nu)\,d\nu=C\left(\frac{kT}{h}\right)^4\times6=\frac{8\pi h}{c^3}\cdot\frac{6(kT)^4}{h^4}=\frac{48\pi(kT)^4}{c^3h^3} $$

(Also verified with SymPy's definite integral: $\displaystyle\int_0^\infty C\nu^3e^{-h\nu/kT}d\nu=6C(kT)^4/h^4$.)

For the Rayleigh–Jeans formula (Example 49.3), the integral diverged because the integrand was $\nu^2$ (a polynomial that does not decay even as $\nu\to\infty$). In Wien's formula, by contrast, the exponential function $e^{-h\nu/kT}$ approaches $0$ faster than any polynomial as $\nu\to\infty$ (as was also noted in the Intuition box of Section 49.2.3), so the decay of the exponential completely cancels the growth of the polynomial $\nu^3$, and the integral settles at a finite value. This is the mathematical heart of the reason why Wien's formula (and Planck's formula, which contains it) does not suffer an ultraviolet catastrophe.

Exercise 49.6: Continuing the original notes (Advanced)

Solution

In Section 49.5.1 of the main text, the product-to-sum formula is used twice to reach the intermediate expression

$$ \sin\frac{\pi n_1}{L}x\cdot\sin\frac{\pi n_2}{L}y\cdot\sin\frac{\pi n_3}{L}z =-\frac12\sin\frac{\pi n_3}Lz\left\{\cos\frac{\pi(n_1x+n_2y)}L-\cos\frac{\pi(n_1x-n_2y)}L\right\} $$

Then, expanding only the product with the first term $\cos\dfrac{\pi(n_1x+n_2y)}L$ inside $\{\ \}$, the original notes begin to write

$$ -\frac12\sin\frac{\pi n_3}Lz\cdot\cos\frac{\pi(n_1x+n_2y)}L=-\frac14\left[\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z+n_1x+n_2y)}L\right. $$

but end there, with the line unfinished. Let us complete the rest. To simplify the notation, let $A:=\dfrac{\pi n_1}Lx$, $B:=\dfrac{\pi n_2}Ly$, and $C:=\dfrac{\pi n_3}Lz$ (in this notation the intermediate expression in the main text can be written as $\sin A\sin B\sin C=-\dfrac12\sin C\{\cos(A+B)-\cos(A-B)\}$).

The first term (the term the original notes had begun to write). Using the formula $\sin\alpha\cos\beta=\dfrac12\{\sin(\alpha+\beta)+\sin(\alpha-\beta)\}$ with $\alpha=C$ and $\beta=A+B$, we obtain

$$ -\frac12\sin C\cos(A+B)=-\frac12\cdot\frac12\bigl\{\sin(C+A+B)+\sin(C-A-B)\bigr\}=-\frac14\sin(C+A+B)-\frac14\sin(C-A-B) $$

This gives the expression above, and it shows that the contents of the large square bracket $[\ $ that the original notes left open split into two terms: $\sin(C+A+B)$ (exactly the term written in the original notes) and one more term, $\sin(C-A-B)$—that is, closing the square bracket of the original notes gives

$$ -\frac14\left[\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z+n_1x+n_2y)}L+\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z-n_1x-n_2y)}L\right] $$

This is the result.

The second term (the $-\cos(A-B)$ term inside $\{\ \}$). Note that inside the $\{\ \}$ of the intermediate expression, there is no $-$ in front of $\cos(A+B)$, while a $-$ is attached in front of $\cos(A-B)$ (as in $\{\cos(A+B)-\cos(A-B)\}$). Multiplying this further by the leading factor $-\dfrac12\sin C$ gives $-\dfrac12\sin C\times\bigl(-\cos(A-B)\bigr)=+\dfrac12\sin C\cos(A-B)$, so the sign is reversed once more and becomes plus. Using the same formula with $\alpha=C$ and $\beta=A-B$, we obtain

$$ \frac12\sin C\cos(A-B)=\frac12\cdot\frac12\bigl\{\sin(C+A-B)+\sin(C-(A-B))\bigr\}=\frac14\sin(C+A-B)+\frac14\sin(C-A+B) $$

That is what we obtain.

Collecting the four terms. Adding the two computations above, we obtain the final complete expansion.

$$ \sin\frac{\pi n_1}Lx\cdot\sin\frac{\pi n_2}Ly\cdot\sin\frac{\pi n_3}Lz =-\frac14\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z+n_1x+n_2y)}L-\frac14\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z-n_1x-n_2y)}L+\frac14\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z+n_1x-n_2y)}L+\frac14\sin\frac{\pi(n_3z-n_1x+n_2y)}L $$

This is an expression in which terms of the form $\pm\dfrac14\sin\dfrac{\pi(\pm n_1x\pm n_2y\pm n_3z)}L$ appear in exactly four sign combinations (the sign of $n_3z$ is always taken to be positive, and among the sign combinations $++,+-,-+,--$ of $n_1x,n_2y$, the four independent ones appear), and it agrees with the form announced in the “Caution” of Section 49.5.1 of the main text, namely an expansion into four terms (verified with SymPy that the values of the left- and right-hand sides agree when numerical values are substituted—and even with $n_1,n_2,n_3,x,y,z,L$ left as symbols, we confirmed, using the expansion rules for trigonometric functions, that the two sides are identically equal).

Why can't we count the number this way? What this expansion computes is an **identity** that rewrites a single standing wave $\sin\dfrac{\pi n_1}Lx\sin\dfrac{\pi n_2}Ly\sin\dfrac{\pi n_3}Lz$ (one particular, definite waveform as a function of $x,y,z$) as a superposition of $4$ plane traveling waves $\sin\dfrac{\pi(\pm n_1x\pm n_2y\pm n_3z)}L$. This rearrangement is a relation that holds each time we fix one triple of integers $(n_1,n_2,n_3)$ (that is, each time we choose one standing wave), and it does not touch the values of $n_1,n_2,n_3$ themselves at all—the same $n_1,n_2,n_3$ as before the expansion remain unchanged in the expanded expression. Therefore, however far we push this expansion, we cannot extract from it the information “how many triples $(n_1,n_2,n_3)$ there are in all.” The operation of decomposing a waveform into components and the operation of counting the total number of sets of components (or of the original quantum numbers) are entirely different tasks.

By contrast, the method of Section 49.5.2, which counts the lattice points in $k$-space, does not go into the waveforms of the individual standing waves (their form as functions of $x,y,z$) at all. It counts the “number” directly, using only the geometry of wavenumber space itself—“where in $k$-space, and how densely, the wavenumber triples $(k_1,k_2,k_3)=\dfrac\pi L(n_1,n_2,n_3)$ that satisfy the boundary conditions are arranged” (the lattice spacing $\pi/L$ and the volume of a ball of radius $K$). The route that the original notes were following on their last page (decomposing the waveform by product-to-sum formulas) cannot, even if carried through to the end, reach the density of states $\rho(\nu)$ on its own—this is why this chapter switches to the standard counting method in Section 49.5.2.
